They reached the bottom of the channel.
 
The truck had carved a perfect, bloody highway through the dead. The corpses that had not been crushed were violently disoriented by the massive physical impact of the vehicle. They were staggering blindly, their rotting limbs tangled together in the mud.
 
Harlan sprinted down the cleared path, his heavy boots splashing through the black fluid left in the truck's wake.
 
Ahead of them, the flatbed truck hit a particularly dense pile of bodies. The front axle caught on a tangle of shattered femurs and ribcages. The momentum flipped the heavy steel chassis completely over. It crashed upside down into the mud with a thunderous metallic clang, pinning a dozen corpses beneath it.
 
The path was blocked by the wreck.
 
"Around it!" Harlan yelled, veering sharply to the right.
 
He drove his iron spike directly into the face of a wandering drowner, clearing a path around the overturned truck. The survivors funneled behind him, a tight, frantic stream of humanity slipping through the narrow gap before the horde could close ranks.
 
They broke through the back of the crowd.
 
Suddenly, there were no more rotting hands reaching for them. There were no more snapping jaws. There was only the wide, empty expanse of the dry Salinas River channel stretching out into the dark.
 
Chapter Nine: The Mud
 
"Do not stop!" Harlan shouted over his shoulder, not breaking his stride. "Keep running!"
 
They sprinted down the center of the dry riverbed. The adrenaline of the breakout was masking their profound physical exhaustion, but Harlan knew it would not last long. Starvation was a cruel master. Their bodies were burning muscle for fuel.
 
Behind them, the horrific moaning of the horde began to fade, replaced by the heavy, ragged breathing of the running survivors.
 
The dead were incredibly slow, but a handful of the drowners had turned and were following them down the channel. They were moving with that terrifying, relentless gait, utterly unburdened by fatigue or lactic acid.
 
"Mr. Harlan!"
 
The scream came from behind him. Harlan risked a glance over his shoulder.
 
A young man had tripped in the deep, cracked mud. He had fallen hard, twisting his ankle. He was scrambling desperately to his feet, but his leg would not bear his weight.
 
Three of the pursuing drowners descended on him instantly.
 
Harlan did not stop. He had given the order. He forced his eyes forward, locking away the empathy that made him human. If he stopped to fight, he would die, and the people following him would die.
 
The young man's screams echoed off the high mud banks of the river, a sharp, agonizing sound that was quickly muffled by the wet tearing of flesh.
 
"Jack!" Ruth yelled.
 
Harlan spun around. Fifty yards back, Little Jack had stumbled. The boy was incredibly malnourished, his thin legs finally failing him. He was stuck in the thick, viscous mud near the edge of the channel, his bare feet sinking deep into the clay.
 
Ruth was not running. The tough woman from Arkansas had stopped entirely. She was sprinting back toward the boy, her heavy cast-iron ladle raised high.
 
A drowner was closing in on Jack, its jaw snapping wildly.
 
"Ruth, leave him!" Harlan roared, though he knew it was useless.
 
Ruth reached the boy just as the corpse lunged. She swung the heavy ladle with both hands, striking the creature perfectly on the temple. The skull shattered, and the drowner collapsed into the mud.
 
But a second corpse was right behind it.
 
The second drowner tackled Ruth from the side. The impact knocked the heavy ladle from her hands. She went down hard in the mud, the rotting corpse falling directly on top of her.
 
Harlan cursed violently. He reversed his direction, sprinting back up the riverbed toward them.
 
The corpse had its hands wrapped tightly around Ruth's throat. She was thrashing wildly, punching the creature's ruined face, but the dead felt no pain.
 
Harlan did not have time to reach them before the jaws found her neck. He stopped his sprint, planted his boots in the mud, and threw his rusted railroad spike.
 
It was the second time that night he had thrown the heavy iron. This time, his aim was true.
 
The heavy iron spike flew through the dark and struck the drowner squarely in the back of the head. The sheer weight of the iron punched right through the rotting occipital bone, burying itself deep into the cerebellum.
 
The corpse instantly went limp, collapsing on top of Ruth.
 
Harlan reached them seconds later. He hauled the heavy, dead weight off Ruth's chest and pulled her out of the mud. He yanked his iron spike free from the skull.
 
"Get up," he gasped, his chest heaving painfully. He grabbed Little Jack by the collar and hauled the boy out of the clay. "Run."
 
Chapter Ten: The Open River
 
They ran until their bodies simply shut down.
 
The sprint degraded into a jog, the jog degraded into a stagger, and finally, the stagger degraded into a slow, agonizing walk. The deep mud of the riverbed sucked at their boots, turning every step into a monumental physical effort.
 
Harlan looked back.
 
The river channel behind them was empty. The dead were gone. They had completely outpaced the slow-moving horde. The distance was their only armor.
 
He slowed his pace, allowing the survivors to gather around him.
 
There were perhaps forty of them left. Forty starving, exhausted people covered in mud and black blood. They had left thousands behind in the Hooverville to be consumed by the horde. The barricade of garbage had failed, and the camp was entirely lost.
 
"We are safe," Miller gasped, falling to his knees in the dry mud. "We outran them."
 
Harlan did not answer. He turned and looked south, down the long, winding scar of the Salinas River.
 
The eastern sky was beginning to lighten. The first pale grey light of dawn was bleeding into the atmosphere, illuminating the desolate landscape. The world was utterly quiet. There were no birds singing. There was no wind. There was only the vast, empty expanse of the cracked riverbed.
 
Ruth walked up beside him. Her burlap apron was ruined, entirely soaked in the foul fluids of the dead. She rested a dirty hand on Little Jack's shoulder. The boy was leaning against her, his eyes closed, fast asleep on his feet.
 
"Where do we go now, Harlan?" she asked quietly. Her voice was completely devoid of hope.
 
Harlan leaned heavily on his rusted railroad spike, using it as a crude walking stick. He looked down at his ruined hand, the missing fingers aching in the cold morning air.
 
They had come to California looking for work. They had come looking for fruit to pick, for wages to earn, for a chance to rebuild the lives the Dustbowl had destroyed. The Hooverville had been a miserable, squalid purgatory, but it had been a place to stop.
 
Now, they were moving again.
 
"We walk south," Harlan said, his voice flat and exhausted. "We follow the riverbed to the ocean. Maybe there is a boat. Maybe there is an island."
 
He knew he was lying. There was no sanctuary. The dead were everywhere. They were in the cities, they were on the farms, and they were walking the dry rivers.
 
The migration was no longer about finding a better life. It was no longer about the American dream.
 
It was simply about surviving the next hour.
 
Harlan adjusted his grip on the heavy iron spike and took a step forward into the mud. The small, broken group of survivors followed him in silence, walking slowly toward the rising sun.
 
Story Three: The Immobilized Locomotive
 
Chapter One: The Derailment
 
The sound of twisting steel was deafening. It was a horrific, metallic shriek that tore through the desolate silence of the New Mexico desert.
 
Frank, the lead engineer of the Atchison, Topeka and Santa Fe Railway locomotive, was thrown violently against the massive iron boiler. The blistering heat of the metal seared through his heavy canvas jacket, but he barely felt the burn. He was fighting frantically to close the throttle.
 
The massive steam engine, weighing over two hundred tons, had struck a massive, hard-packed sand drift at forty miles an hour. The drought had loosened the topsoil for hundreds of miles, and the relentless desert winds had built a concrete-hard dune directly across the tracks in a blind curve.
 
"Brace!" Frank roared at his fireman.
 
The front wheels of the locomotive jumped the rails. The heavy iron cowcatcher plowed into the sand, sending a massive plume of dust high into the air. The train shuddered violently, the incredible kinetic energy ripping the steel track ties out of the ground like splinters.
 
With a final, agonizing groan, the locomotive tilted sharply to the right and skidded to a dead halt in the dirt.
 
Instantly, the cab was filled with a blinding cloud of pressurized steam. A high-pressure line had ruptured in the collision. The hissing was so loud it rattled Frank's teeth in his skull.
 
He scrambled off the hot boiler, his boots slipping on the coal dust covering the floorboards. He found the fireman buried under a pile of loose coal that had spilled from the tender. The man was unconscious, bleeding heavily from a gash on his forehead, but he was breathing.
 
Frank stumbled out of the cab and dropped onto the rocky desert floor. The heat of the afternoon sun hit him like a physical blow.
 
Behind the locomotive, the heavy passenger cars had miraculously stayed upright, though they had concertinaed violently against each other.
 
In the luxury Pullman car at the rear of the train, Thomas, a young porter in a crisp white uniform, was picking himself up off the thick carpet. The opulent mahogany paneling of the car was splintered. Crystal glasses from the bar were shattered everywhere.
 
"Is everyone alright?" Thomas shouted, his voice shaking.
 
Eleanor, a wealthy socialite traveling from Chicago to Los Angeles, was sitting on the floor between two velvet seats. Her expensive silk dress was torn, and her feathered hat was askew, but she appeared physically unhurt.
 
"What happened?" she demanded, her voice shrill with panic. "Did we hit something?"
 
"A derailment, ma'am," Thomas said, offering her a trembling hand. "Please, stay in the car. I must check with the engineer."
 
Chapter Two: The Whistle
 
Before Thomas could reach the door, a terrifying sound erupted from the front of the train.
 
The steam whistle on the locomotive had jammed open during the crash. It began to shriek, a continuous, deafening blast of compressed steam that echoed for miles across the flat, featureless desert basin. It was an agonizing noise, impossible to ignore, drawing the attention of anything within a fifty-mile radius.
 
Frank was already climbing the side of the hot, tilted boiler.
 
"I need a wrench!" he yelled down at a bewildered brakeman who had emerged from the baggage car.
 
The brakeman tossed up a heavy iron wrench. Frank caught it clumsily. His hands were shaking from the adrenaline of the crash. He crawled carefully along the curved, blistering iron of the boiler toward the shrieking whistle valve. The steam was burning his face, cooking the sweat on his skin.
 
He reached the jammed valve and brought the heavy wrench down hard. He struck the brass fitting repeatedly, the metal ringing over the roar of the steam. On the fourth strike, the jammed lever snapped closed.
 
The silence that followed was absolute and terrifying.
 
Frank lay on top of the hot boiler, gasping for breath. The ringing in his ears slowly faded, replaced by the dry, whistling wind of the desert.
 
He sat up and looked out over the vast expanse of the basin. The landscape was a desolate ocean of scrub brush, cracked earth, and shimmering heat waves. They were entirely isolated. The nearest town was seventy miles away.
 
Then, Frank saw the shapes.
 
They were emerging from the shimmering heat distortion on the horizon. At first, he thought it was a rescue party from a distant ranch. But as he squinted against the glaring sun, he realized there were too many of them.
 
They were scattered in a loose, ragged line, moving slowly toward the train from every direction. They were not walking on the tracks; they were wandering aimlessly through the deep sand and scrub. The deafening blast of the whistle had summoned them.
 
Frank felt a cold knot form in his stomach. He had heard the rumors on the telegraph wires. He had heard the stories from the brakemen in Chicago about the quarantine zones in the cities and the strange violence sweeping the countryside. He had not believed them.
 
He watched as one of the figures stumbled over a large rock. The figure did not put its hands out to break its fall. It simply smashed face-first into the dirt, rolled over, and stood back up with a jerky, unnatural motion.
 
"They are not human," Frank whispered to himself.
 
He scrambled down from the boiler, dropping heavily into the dirt.
 
"Lock the doors!" Frank roared at the brakeman. "Lock every door on the train! Do not let the passengers outside!"
 
Chapter Three: The Passengers
 
The passengers were terrified. The sudden halt and the subsequent lockdown had sparked a wave of claustrophobic panic inside the heavy steel cars.
 
In the Pullman car, Eleanor was banging her manicured fists against the locked iron door.
 
"You cannot keep us in here!" she screamed at Thomas. The young porter was standing rigidly in front of the door, his face pale with fear. "It is over a hundred degrees! We need air! Open this door immediately!"
 
"The engineer gave strict orders, ma'am," Thomas stammered. "It is not safe outside."
 
"Not safe from what?" an older businessman demanded, wiping the sweat from his neck with a silk handkerchief. "We are in the middle of a desert! We need to flag down a passing vehicle or send someone for help!"
 
The older man pushed past Thomas and grabbed the heavy brass handle of one of the large, plate-glass windows.
 
"Do not open that!" Thomas pleaded.
 
The man ignored him, throwing his weight against the handle. The window slid upward with a heavy mechanical click, letting in a blast of hot, dry desert air.
 
"Help!" the businessman yelled, sticking his head and shoulders out the window. "We have derailed! We need assistance!"
 
He was shouting directly into the face of a walking corpse.
 
The dead in the desert were different from the bloated drowners of the river. The relentless New Mexico sun had baked them entirely dry. Their skin was pulled tight over their bones like old leather, blackened and cracking. Their lips had shriveled away completely, exposing rows of grinning, yellow teeth.
 
The leather-skinned corpse had been standing silently directly below the window.
 
When the businessman yelled, the corpse lunged upward. The desiccated hands, possessing a terrifying, mechanical strength, grabbed the man by the lapels of his expensive suit.
 
Before the businessman could even scream, the corpse pulled him violently forward, sinking its teeth deep into the soft flesh of the man's exposed neck.
 
Blood sprayed in a bright, horrific arc, splattering against the clean glass of the Pullman window and raining down on the white tablecloths inside.
 
Eleanor shrieked, a sound of absolute, primal terror.
 
Thomas reacted purely on instinct. He grabbed the heavy brass window handle and pulled downward with all of his strength. The heavy plate glass slammed shut, catching the dead man's arms between the frame and the sill.
 
The glass shattered instantly.
 
The corpse did not flinch. It simply released the dying businessman, who collapsed onto the floor of the train in a pool of his own blood. The dead thing reached its leathery, bloody hands through the jagged shards of glass, snapping its jaw wildly at the screaming passengers inside the luxury car.
 
Chapter Four: The Coal Tender
 
Frank heard the screaming from the front of the train. He was standing on the narrow iron catwalk beside the tilted boiler, a heavy iron crowbar gripped tightly in his hands.
 
The dead were swarming the locomotive. Dozens of the sun-baked corpses were pressing against the heavy iron wheels, their leathery hands scraping uselessly against the thick steel of the cowcatcher. They were completely fixated on the smell of the living inside the metal tube.
 
"They are going to break the glass," Frank realized.
 
The passenger cars were built heavy, but the large observation windows were a fatal flaw. Once the dead realized they could shatter the glass by throwing their weight against it, the entire train would become a slaughterhouse.
 
There was only one place on the train that was impenetrable.
 
The coal tender. It was a massive, open-topped iron box attached directly behind the locomotive. It held twenty tons of coal. Its walls were solid steel plate, towering ten feet above the tracks, with absolutely no windows or doors.
 
Frank kicked open the heavy iron door of the dining car.
 
It was absolute chaos. The dead had broken through three windows on the right side of the car. The elegant space, filled with white linen tablecloths and fine china, had been transformed into a brutal, close-quarters battlefield.
 
A leather-skinned corpse was dragging a screaming woman over a mahogany table, shattering crystal plates beneath them.
 
Frank did not hesitate. He stepped forward and swung the heavy iron crowbar like a baseball bat. He struck the corpse squarely in the side of the head. The desiccated skull shattered like dry clay. The creature collapsed instantly, its dark blood soaking into the pristine white tablecloth.
 
"Listen to me!" Frank roared, his voice booming over the screams and the shattering glass. "Move forward! Everyone move to the front of the train!"
 
Thomas appeared in the doorway of the adjoining Pullman car, practically dragging a hysterical Eleanor behind him. His white uniform was covered in blood.
 
"They are breaking the glass in the back!" Thomas yelled.
 
"Get them to the front!" Frank ordered, smashing the skull of another corpse that had managed to crawl through a broken window. "Get them to the engine! We have to put them in the coal tender!"
 
It was a terrifying, violent stampede. The terrified passengers pushed their way through the narrow aisles of the dining car, slipping on the blood and broken china. The dead were pouring through the windows now, a relentless tide of snapping teeth and leathery hands.
 
Frank stood his ground in the center of the dining car, using the heavy iron crowbar to bash the skulls of the dead as they crawled over the tables. He was buying seconds with sheer physical brutality. He aimed strictly for the head, knowing that strikes to the chest or limbs were entirely useless against the reanimated nervous system.
 
"Everyone is through!" Thomas yelled from the front door of the car.
 
Frank swung the crowbar one last time, crushing the face of a corpse wearing a ragged cowboy hat. He turned and sprinted toward the door, slamming it shut behind him and throwing the heavy brass deadbolt.
 
Instantly, a dozen bloody hands slapped against the glass of the door, pressing against the small window.
 
They had abandoned the rear of the train, but the dead now possessed the passenger cars. The survivors were trapped in the engine, and the horde outside was growing larger by the minute.
 
Chapter Five: The Climb
 
The space between the dining car and the heavy iron coal tender was a narrow, terrifying bridge of steel grading. It was fully exposed to the desert air.
 
"Up the ladder!" Frank ordered, pointing with his bloody crowbar to the sheer iron wall of the tender. "Climb over the top and jump into the coal! Do it now!"
 
There were twelve surviving passengers huddled on the grading. They looked up at the towering, ten-foot-tall black iron wall. The prospect of throwing themselves blindly into twenty tons of loose coal was terrifying, but the heavy thudding of the dead against the dining car door was a powerful motivator.
 
A young couple scrambled up the iron rungs first, throwing their legs over the lip of the tender and disappearing into the black box with a loud clatter of shifting coal.
 
"Go! Go!" Thomas shouted, practically shoving the older passengers up the ladder.
 
Eleanor stood frozen at the base of the iron wall. Her expensive silk dress was already ruined, but the sheer physical indignity of climbing the filthy ladder seemed to paralyze her.
 
"I cannot do this," she sobbed, staring at the thick layer of black grease coating the iron rungs. "I will fall."
 
"Ma'am, you must climb!" Thomas pleaded, his crisp uniform now a horrifying patchwork of crimson and soot.
 
Behind them, the heavy brass deadbolt on the dining car door began to groan. The lock was strong, but the wood of the door frame was splintering under the immense, unfeeling pressure of dozens of bodies throwing themselves against the glass.
 
"Move!" Frank roared. He grabbed Eleanor by the waist of her silk dress and literally hoisted her onto the first rung of the ladder.
 
She screamed, but survival instinct finally took over. She scrambled upward, her expensive high heels slipping dangerously on the iron. She reached the top and rolled over the edge, dropping into the dark with a heavy thud.
 
Only Frank and Thomas remained on the grading.
 
A sharp, violent crack echoed through the narrow space. The wooden frame of the dining car door finally shattered entirely. The door swung open, and the dead spilled out onto the grading in a chaotic, snapping wave of leathery limbs.
 
"Up!" Frank yelled, grabbing the rungs.
 
He climbed fast, the heavy crowbar still clutched in his hand. He reached the top of the tender and swung his legs over the lip. He turned to look down.
 
Thomas was halfway up the ladder. Below him, the grading was swarming with the sun-baked corpses. One of them, a tall man with a ruined, grinning face, reached up and clamped its desiccated hand around Thomas's ankle.
 
The porter screamed, his grip on the slippery iron ladder failing. He dangled precariously over the horde, his body weight pulling him downward into the snapping teeth.
 
Frank did not hesitate. He leaned dangerously far over the lip of the tender, holding onto the iron rim with his left hand. He swung the heavy crowbar downward like an axe.
 
The heavy iron struck the wrist of the corpse holding Thomas's ankle. The dry, brittle bone shattered instantly, severing the hand completely.
 
The sudden release of tension sent Thomas swinging hard against the iron wall.
 
"Grab my arm!" Frank roared.
 
Thomas reached up. Frank grabbed the collar of the porter's bloody uniform and hauled backward with all of his strength. The incredible physical effort tore the muscles in Frank's shoulder, but he did not stop pulling until Thomas was dragged completely over the lip of the tender.
 
They both tumbled backward, rolling down the steep, shifting mountain of loose coal into the absolute darkness of the iron box.
 
Chapter Six: The Coal Dust
 
The interior of the coal tender was a suffocating nightmare.
 
The massive iron box was designed to hold twenty tons of bituminous coal. It was a deep, V-shaped hopper, with steep sides that fed the fuel down toward the boiler grate. The space was completely enclosed, save for the open top, but the high walls blocked out the sun and any breeze.
 
It was pitch black.
 
The chaotic scramble of fourteen people falling into the hopper had disturbed the deep layers of loose fuel. The air in the tender was instantly choked with a thick, microscopic cloud of pulverized coal dust.
 
Frank coughed violently, a wet, rattling sound that tore at his lungs. He pulled his red bandana up over his nose and mouth, but the fine black powder penetrated the fabric immediately. Every inhalation tasted of ancient, bitter earth.
 
"We cannot breathe in here," Eleanor cried from somewhere in the dark. Her voice was muffled and raspy.
 
"Cover your faces with your clothes," Frank ordered, spitting a thick glob of black phlegm into the coal. "Breathe as shallowly as you can. Do not move around. Moving stirs up the dust."
 
The survivors lay perfectly still on the shifting, uneven bed of hard black rocks.
 
The immediate threat of the teeth had been removed, but the environment itself was turning hostile.
 
The relentless New Mexico sun was beating down on the heavy black iron walls of the tender. The metal was rapidly absorbing the heat, turning the enclosed hopper into a massive, suffocating oven. The temperature inside the box was already well over a hundred degrees, and it was climbing steadily.
 
Sweat poured down Frank's face, cutting clean trails through the thick layer of soot coating his skin. The heat was oppressive, pressing down on his chest like a physical weight. The claustrophobia in the dark, sweltering space was intense.
 
Outside the iron walls, the desert was not silent.
 
The dead had surrounded the tender. They were pressing their leathery bodies against the thick steel plates, scraping their ruined fingernails against the metal. The sound was horrifying—a continuous, metallic scratching that vibrated through the very walls of their prison.
 
A heavy thump echoed from the right wall of the tender.
 
Then another.
 
"What is that?" Thomas whispered in the dark, his voice trembling.
 
"They are trying to climb the iron," Frank said grimly.
 
The dead were standing on top of one another, using the chaotic pile of their own bodies as a crude ladder to reach the lip of the ten-foot wall. The sides of the tender were sheer, smooth steel, offering no handholds, but their mindless persistence was terrifying.
 
Every time a corpse managed to scramble a few feet up the iron, it would inevitably slip and fall back into the horde, creating the heavy thumping sound. It was a relentless, agonizing rhythm of scratching and falling.
 
Frank lay back against the coal, listening to the horrifying chorus. They were safe from the teeth for the moment, but they were trapped in a slow-cooker. They had no water. They had no food. If they did not suffocate on the coal dust, they would die of heatstroke within twenty-four hours.
 
And then, Frank remembered the boiler.
 
Chapter Seven: The Boiler Pressure
 
The realization hit Frank with the force of a physical blow.
 
When the locomotive had struck the sand drift, the violent impact had thrown him from the controls before he could initiate the emergency shutdown sequence. He had successfully closed the throttle, stopping the forward motion of the wheels, but the massive firebox beneath the boiler was still burning hot.
 
And he had not opened the pressure release valve.
 
"God almighty," Frank whispered, sitting bolt upright in the dark coal hopper.
 
"What is it?" Thomas asked, alarmed by the sudden movement.
 
"The boiler," Frank said, his voice tight with panic. He scrambled toward the front wall of the tender, feeling his way through the dark. "The safety blow-off is jammed. The fire is still burning, and the steam pressure is building."
 
"What does that mean?" Eleanor asked, coughing heavily into her silk sleeve.
 
"It means the locomotive is a two-hundred-ton pipe bomb," Frank said bluntly.
 
The heavy iron boiler of the steam engine was designed to contain immense pressure, but without a release valve, the expanding steam would eventually exceed the tensile strength of the steel. When that happened, the boiler would not simply rupture; it would detonate with the explosive force of a massive artillery shell.
 
"It will blow the tender to pieces," Frank said, pressing his ear against the front wall of the hopper.
 
Through the thick steel, he could hear it. A deep, resonant hum, like a massive tuning fork. It was the sound of superheated steam straining against the iron casing of the boiler. The pressure was already critically high.
 
"We have to run," Thomas said, terror bleeding back into his voice. "We have to get out of the train."
 
"We cannot run," Frank countered. "The dead are swarming the perimeter. If we leave the tender, they will tear us apart in seconds."
 
"Then what do we do?" Eleanor shrieked.
 
"I have to go back to the cab," Frank said, gripping his heavy iron crowbar tightly in the dark.
 
The cab of the locomotive was attached directly to the front of the coal tender. To reach it, Frank would have to climb out of the safety of the hopper, expose himself on the narrow gangway, and climb back into the very space where the crash had occurred.
 
"The dead are in the cab," Thomas said quietly. "I saw them crawling through the broken windows when we were on the grading."
 
"I know," Frank said.
 
He stood up, his boots sinking deep into the shifting coal. The heat in the tender was suffocating, but the cold sweat of sheer terror was rolling down his spine.
 
He had to climb out of the iron box, fight his way through a confined space swarming with sun-baked corpses, and manually crank the heavy brass release valve. If he failed, or if he was bitten, the boiler would explode, and every living soul in the tender would be instantly vaporized.
 
"Stay low in the coal," Frank ordered the terrified passengers. "Do not make a sound."
 
He reached for the iron rungs welded to the inside wall of the hopper and began to climb upward toward the blinding glare of the desert sun.
 
Chapter Eight: The Cab
 
Frank crested the lip of the coal tender. The heat of the afternoon sun hit him like an open furnace door.
 
He swung his leg over the iron rim and dropped onto the narrow steel gangway that connected the tender to the locomotive cab. The gangway was slick with spilled oil and coal dust. He gripped his heavy iron crowbar tightly in his right hand, balancing himself against the tilt of the derailed engine.
 
He stepped up to the open doorway of the cab.
 
It was swarming.
 
Three sun-baked corpses had managed to pull themselves up the iron steps and through the shattered side window. They were trapped inside the confined space of the cab, crawling over the broken brass gauges and the unconscious body of the fireman, drawn by the intense heat of the boiler.
 
One of them, a desiccated corpse wearing the rotting remains of a railway worker's uniform, turned toward the doorway. The skin on its face was pulled so tight that its eyelids had split, exposing completely dry, white eyeballs.
 
Frank did not hesitate. He stepped into the cab and swung the crowbar.
 
The cramped quarters made a full swing impossible. He thrust the iron bar forward like a spear, driving the blunt end directly into the corpse's open, grinning mouth. The iron shattered the rotting teeth and punched through the back of the throat, snapping the cervical vertebrae.
 
The corpse collapsed backward onto the hot iron floorboards, the smell of burning flesh instantly filling the small space.
 
The second corpse lunged from the fireman's seat. It grabbed Frank by the left arm, its leathery fingers possessing the strength of industrial clamps. The dead jaws snapped furiously, burying dry teeth into the heavy canvas of Frank's work jacket.
 
The thick fabric held. The teeth could not pierce the heavy weave.
 
Frank twisted his body violently, throwing the corpse against the massive iron door of the firebox. The intense heat of the cast iron instantly seared the dead flesh. Frank brought the crowbar up in a tight arc, striking the creature sharply on the temple. The brittle skull caved inward, and the corpse went limp.
 
The third corpse had crawled over the boiler controls. It dropped down directly onto Frank's back.
 
The sudden, dead weight drove Frank to his knees. His chin struck the iron floorboards hard. The crowbar slipped from his grasp, clattering away under the brake stand. The corpse wrapped its arms around Frank's neck, its jaw snapping wildly inches from his ear.
 
Frank reached backward with his bare hands, grasping the dry, hot flesh of the creature's head. He found the eye sockets. He dug his thumbs deeply into the dry tissue and pushed with all of his strength, using the horrific leverage to peel the corpse off his back.
 
He threw the thrashing body onto the floor. He scrambled forward on his hands and knees, grabbing the crowbar. He spun around and brought the heavy iron down with a desperate, two-handed strike, crushing the skull completely against the iron floorboards.
 
The cab was clear.
 
Frank lay on the hot iron, gasping for breath, the smell of roasting dead flesh burning his throat.
 
Chapter Nine: The Release Valve
 
The massive steam boiler was vibrating violently.
 
Frank dragged himself up the tilted floor of the cab and looked at the primary pressure gauge. The brass needle was buried entirely in the red zone, pinned hard against the peg. The thick glass face of the gauge was already spider-webbed with stress fractures.
 
He looked up. The primary pressure release valve was a large, heavy brass wheel located near the roof of the cab, directly above the firebox door.
 
Frank climbed onto the fireman's seat, stepping over the body of his unconscious partner. The heat radiating from the boiler was agonizing. His heavy canvas jacket was smoking slightly, the trapped coal dust beginning to smolder.
 
He reached up and grabbed the heavy brass wheel with both hands. The metal was searing hot. It burned the skin off his palms instantly, but he ignored the pain.
 
He pulled downward with all of his remaining strength.
 
The wheel did not move. The sheer pressure of the superheated steam had fused the brass threads.
 
Frank cursed, his voice a hoarse rasp over the deep, terrifying hum of the expanding boiler. He let go of the wheel and reached down for his crowbar. He brought the heavy iron up and struck the edge of the brass wheel with a massive, desperate blow.
 
The iron sparked loudly against the brass.
 
He struck it again. And again. On the fourth strike, the heavy wheel shifted a fraction of an inch.
 
Frank dropped the crowbar, ignoring his blistered, bleeding hands, and grabbed the wheel again. He threw his entire body weight into a violent twist.
 
The valve cracked open.
 
The sound was apocalyptic. A massive geyser of superheated steam erupted from the safety stack on the roof of the locomotive. It was a solid, blinding pillar of white vapor, roaring into the desert sky with the kinetic force of a jet engine.
 
The sheer volume of the noise was a physical shockwave that slammed Frank backward against the cab wall. He clamped his hands over his ears, screaming in agony as the pressure change threatened to rupture his eardrums.
 
The entire two-hundred-ton locomotive shuddered violently as the massive internal pressure was released into the atmosphere. The heavy hum of the stressed iron slowly began to fade, replaced entirely by the deafening roar of the escaping steam.
 
The needle on the shattered pressure gauge slowly, agonizingly, began to drop back into the green.
 
Frank slid down the wall of the cab, his back pressed against the hot iron. He looked at his ruined, blistered hands.
 
The bomb was defused. The locomotive was safe.
 
Chapter Ten: The Desert Night
 
The roar of the escaping steam lasted for twenty minutes before it finally died down to a low, steady hiss.
 
Frank dragged the unconscious fireman out of the cab and across the narrow gangway. It was a brutal, agonizing effort, but he managed to haul his partner over the lip of the coal tender and drop him into the safety of the hopper.
 
Frank climbed the iron rungs slowly, his burned hands screaming in protest with every movement. He threw his leg over the rim and looked down into the dark box.
 
The fourteen passengers were huddled together in the coal, their faces covered in soot, their eyes wide with sheer terror.
 
"The boiler is safe," Frank said, his voice entirely raw. "We are not going to explode."
 
A collective, shuddering sigh of relief went up from the passengers. Thomas slumped back against the shifting coal, and Eleanor began to weep softly into her ruined silk dress.
 
"We are safe," the young porter whispered.
 
"No," Frank said grimly. He did not climb down into the hopper. He stayed perched on the lip of the heavy iron wall, looking out over the vast expanse of the New Mexico desert. "We are not safe."
 
The sun was setting, painting the western horizon in deep, bruised shades of purple and red. The temperature in the desert was dropping rapidly, replacing the suffocating heat of the afternoon with a biting, arid cold.
 
But the drop in temperature was not the problem.
 
The deafening roar of the pressure release valve had echoed across the flat desert basin for miles. It was a massive, auditory beacon, a dinner bell ringing out across the desolate wasteland.
 
Frank looked down at the base of the tender.
 
The desert was no longer empty. The flat, scrub-covered basin was entirely black with movement. Thousands upon thousands of leathery, sun-baked corpses were pressing against the heavy iron wheels of the derailed locomotive. The horde stretched out in every direction as far as the eye could see, a silent, relentless sea of rotting flesh drawn by the noise.
 
They were banging against the thick steel walls of the coal tender, a chaotic, continuous thudding that sounded like heavy rain falling on a tin roof.
 
Frank swung his leg back over the lip and dropped down into the pitch-black coal hopper, joining the terrified passengers in the dark.
 
The locomotive had not exploded. But their iron sanctuary was completely surrounded. They were buried alive in a steel box in the middle of a dead desert, entirely cut off from the world, with twenty tons of coal, no water, and a million snapping teeth waiting in the dark outside.
 
Story Four: The Outlaws
 
Chapter One: The Robbery
 
The air inside the First National Bank of Oakhaven smelled sharply of burnt gunpowder and old brass.
 
"Dutch" Vance racked the heavy bolt of his Thompson submachine gun. The mechanical clack of the weapon echoed loudly across the high plaster ceiling of the bank lobby. He stepped over the shattered glass of the teller windows, his polished leather boots leaving bloody footprints on the marble floor.
 
"How are we looking on that vault, Emmett?" Dutch yelled over his shoulder. His voice was brimming with reckless, manic energy.
 
"The tumblers are stiff," Emmett replied from the back room. The older, pragmatic safecracker was kneeling in front of a massive iron vault door, a stethoscope pressed tightly to his ears. "Give me five minutes, Dutch. This ain't a tin can."
 
Dutch laughed loudly. He walked to the center of the lobby and looked down at Sheriff Miller.
 
The local lawman was lying on his back, clutching his stomach. His khaki uniform shirt was soaked in dark blood from a forty-five caliber slug that had caught him just beneath the ribs during the initial breach. He was breathing in shallow, ragged gasps.
 
"You boys are making a mistake," Miller coughed, spitting a thick glob of blood onto the marble. "The whole county is going to come down on you."
 
"That is exactly what I am counting on, Sheriff," Dutch said, a wide, arrogant grin splitting his handsome face. He pulled a silver cigarette case from the pocket of his pinstripe suit. "A man cannot build a legend by robbing empty banks in ghost towns. He needs an audience. He needs a posse."
 
"You are insane," Miller whispered.
 
Dutch ignored him. He lit the cigarette and walked to the front of the bank.
 
The large plate-glass windows offered a clear view of Main Street. Oakhaven was a typical, dusty West Texas town. Wooden storefronts lined a wide dirt road that baked under the relentless afternoon sun.
 
It should have been chaotic. The sound of the Tommy gun shattering the teller glass should have brought every able-bodied man in town running with a shotgun. But the street was eerily, unnervingly quiet.
 
"Where is everybody, Dutch?"
 
"Kid" Cooper, the youngest member of the gang, was standing nervously by the front door. He was nineteen years old, clutching a heavy twelve-gauge pump shotgun that looked too big for his thin frame. He was sweating profusely under his wool flat cap.
 
"They are gathering their courage, Kid," Dutch said smoothly, blowing a long stream of blue smoke against the glass. "They are deputizing the hardware store owner and the blacksmith. They are forming up a grand posse to take down the infamous Vance Gang."
 
Dutch was a man completely consumed by his own mythos. He read dime novels about Jesse James and Billy the Kid, and he desperately wanted his name printed in the same cheap ink. He did not see the world as it was; he saw it as a stage for his own violent, romanticized play.
 
He peered down the dusty street. The heat shimmer distorted the horizon.
 
Then, the shapes began to appear.
 
Chapter Two: The Delusion
 
"Here they come," Dutch announced, his voice rising in triumphant excitement. He raised the heavy Thompson, resting the wooden foregrip on his hip.
 
The Kid stepped up beside him, his hands trembling violently on the pump of his shotgun.
 
Dozens of figures were emerging from the alleyways and stepping out from the wooden boardwalks. They were moving into the center of Main Street, forming a wide, ragged line that completely blocked the escape route.
 
But there was something deeply wrong with the posse.
 
They were not carrying shotguns or hunting rifles. They were not walking with the purposeful stride of angry men defending their town. They were shuffling. Their movements were jerky, uncoordinated, and agonizingly slow.
 
"They look drunk," the Kid whispered, wiping his sweaty palms on his trousers.
 
"They are terrified," Dutch corrected confidently. He took a long drag of his cigarette. "They know who is waiting for them in this bank. They know this is going to be a bloodbath. Look at them dragging their feet."
 
The crowd of figures drew closer. The bright Texas sun illuminated them clearly.
 
The man leading the charge was the local butcher. He was wearing his heavy canvas apron, but it was not stained with animal blood. His throat had been violently torn open, the flesh hanging in ragged, red ribbons. His skin was incredibly pale, drawn tight over his skull, and his jaw hung slack.
 
He was dead. They were all dead.
 
The infection had swept through Oakhaven quietly in the night, moving from house to house, turning the sleeping town into a charnel house. The horrific noise of Dutch's Tommy gun had simply woken the dormant horde.
 
"Dutch," the Kid stammered, taking a step backward. "Dutch, look at his neck. That man is dead."
 
"He is bleeding," Dutch said, entirely dismissing the horrific reality unfolding outside the glass. The dime novels did not feature walking corpses. They featured glorious shootouts and tragic heroes. Dutch's mind simply refused to process the impossibility of the dead butcher. "Probably caught a stray bullet when we breached the door. Get ready, Kid. We are going to give them a show."
 
The dead reached the front of the bank. They did not shout for the outlaws to surrender. They did not take cover behind the wooden horse troughs. They simply walked directly up to the large plate-glass windows and pressed their rotting, bloody faces against the panes.
 
Dozens of hands began to slap against the glass, a chaotic, uncoordinated drumming that sounded like heavy hail.
 
"They ain't got guns, Dutch!" the Kid yelled, genuine panic bleeding into his voice as a woman with half her face chewed away began licking the windowpane.
 
"Then they are fools!" Dutch roared.
 
He dropped his cigarette onto the marble floor, leveled the heavy Thompson submachine gun at the crowd pressing against the glass, and squeezed the trigger.
 
Chapter Three: The Shootout
 
The deafening roar of the fully automatic weapon filled the bank.
 
The heavy .45 caliber bullets shattered the thick plate-glass windows instantly, spraying sharp shards of glass outward into the crowd. The massive heavy slugs tore into the densely packed bodies of the dead with devastating kinetic force.
 
Dutch swept the heavy barrel of the Tommy gun back and forth, cutting a violent arc through the horde. The physical impact of the heavy bullets knocked the front row of corpses backward. Chests exploded in dark bursts of coagulated blood. Shoulders were shattered. Legs were blown out from under them.
 
The Tommy gun clicked empty. The heavy brass casings rolled across the marble floor, ringing like tiny bells.
 
"That is how you handle a posse!" Dutch yelled triumphantly, dropping the empty drum magazine and slapping a fresh one into the receiver.
 
The smoke in the lobby cleared.
 
The bodies that Dutch had knocked down were already moving.
 
The butcher with the torn throat had taken three .45 caliber slugs directly to the center of his chest. The heavy bullets had shattered his ribs and destroyed his heart. He did not care. He simply rolled over in the shattered glass on the sidewalk, pushed himself up onto his knees, and stood back up.
 
Dozens of others followed suit. They ignored the massive, fatal trauma to their organs. They stepped over the corpses of the few whose spines or skulls had been accidentally severed by the gunfire, their jaws snapping mindlessly as they pushed through the empty window frames and into the bank lobby.
 
"They're getting back up!" the Kid screamed, raising his twelve-gauge and firing blindly into the crowd. The buckshot blew a massive hole through a dead woman's stomach, but she continued to walk forward, dragging her own intestines across the marble floor.
 
Dutch stared at the advancing corpses. The romanticized illusion was finally cracking. The dime novels had not prepared him for men who absorbed machine-gun fire without flinching.
 
"They must be wearing iron plates," Dutch muttered, his voice losing its confident swagger. He backed away from the windows, raising the Tommy gun again. "Under their clothes. It's a trick."
 
"Dutch!" Emmett yelled from the back of the bank.
 
The older safecracker ran into the lobby. His hands were covered in grease, and a heavy iron crowbar was gripped tightly in his fist. He stopped dead in his tracks, staring at the horrific scene unfolding in the front of the bank.
 
He saw the pale, rotting skin. He saw the horrific wounds that were not bleeding. He saw the dead eyes.
 
Unlike Dutch, Emmett was a pragmatist. He lived in the real world of heavy iron, complex tumblers, and cold hard facts. He instantly recognized that the rules of reality had fundamentally changed.
 
"They are not a posse, Dutch," Emmett said, his voice entirely devoid of panic. It was cold and utterly absolute. "They are dead. Look at them. They are corpses."
 
Before Dutch could argue, the sheer weight of the horde pushed deeper into the lobby.
 
Chapter Four: The Vault
 
The bank lobby devolved into an absolute slaughterhouse in seconds.
 
The Kid was screaming. He pumped his twelve-gauge as fast as his trembling hands would allow, blowing massive holes in the advancing line of corpses. But the buckshot was useless against the reanimated nervous system unless it struck the brain. The dead simply absorbed the kinetic impact and kept walking.
 
A tall man in a ruined business suit grabbed the barrel of the Kid's shotgun. The corpse possessed an impossible, mechanical strength. It yanked the heavy weapon completely out of the teenager's hands, tossing it aside onto the marble floor.
 
"Dutch!" the Kid shrieked, backing away frantically.
 
Two more corpses descended on the young outlaw. They grabbed his arms, pulling him violently forward into the mass of rotting bodies. The Kid kicked and thrashed, his screams echoing horribly against the high plaster ceiling as the dead jaws found his neck and shoulders.
 
"Kid!" Dutch roared.
 
The gang leader finally snapped out of his delusion. The horror of the reality crashed down on him. He raised the Thompson and fired a sustained burst into the crowd swarming the teenager, but he only succeeded in shooting his own man. The dead ignored the bullets entirely, burying the Kid under a writhing pile of decaying flesh.
 
"We cannot fight them!" Emmett yelled over the deafening noise. The safecracker grabbed Dutch by the collar of his pinstripe suit and hauled him backward. "The vault! Get in the vault!"
 
Dutch did not resist. The romanticized outlaw hero was gone, replaced by a terrified man desperate to live.
 
They ran toward the back of the bank. Emmett had successfully cracked the heavy steel door of the First National Bank vault. It was a massive, impenetrable cube of reinforced concrete and solid steel, designed to withstand dynamite and drill bits.
 
They reached the open vault door.
 
Sheriff Miller was still lying on the floor nearby, bleeding heavily from the gut wound Dutch had given him. He was watching the horde of the dead tearing the Kid apart with wide, disbelieving eyes.
 
"Help me!" the Sheriff coughed, holding a bloody hand up toward them.
 
"Leave him!" Dutch yelled, diving past the heavy steel door and into the vault.
 
Emmett ignored the gang leader. The safecracker grabbed the wounded lawman by his heavy leather gun belt and hauled him across the polished floor, dragging him over the heavy steel threshold and into the vault.
 
The dead had finished with the Kid. They turned their attention to the living meat retreating into the back room. They surged forward, a horrific wave of bloody hands and snapping jaws.
 
Emmett grabbed the massive brass handle on the inside of the vault door. He threw his entire body weight backward, pulling the incredible mass of solid steel inward.
 
The heavy door swung shut on its perfectly oiled hinges.
 
The leading edge of the horde reached the vault just as the door closed. A dozen rotting hands slapped against the cold steel, but it was too late.
 
Emmett spun the heavy brass locking wheel on the inside of the door. The massive steel locking bolts slid into their reinforced striking plates with a deep, final, metallic thud.
 
The deafening chaos of the bank lobby was instantly severed.
 
The inside of the vault was perfectly silent, perfectly dark, and entirely cut off from the end of the world.
 
Chapter Five: The Darkness
 
The darkness inside the vault was absolute. It was a heavy, physical absence of light that pressed against their eyes.
 
"Dutch?" Emmett whispered into the black void.
 
There was no answer. The only sound in the small, enclosed space was the ragged, wet breathing of the wounded sheriff lying on the floor.
 
Emmett reached into the pocket of his greasy trousers and pulled out his Zippo lighter. He flicked the striking wheel. The small yellow flame flared to life, casting long, dancing shadows across the cold steel walls.
 
The First National Bank vault was a ten-by-ten foot cube. The walls were lined top-to-bottom with small brass safety deposit boxes. In the center of the room sat a heavy oak table, piled high with neat stacks of crisp, newly minted paper currency.
 
It was the haul of a lifetime. It was enough money to buy a ranch in Mexico and live like kings for the rest of their natural lives.
 
And it was completely, utterly worthless.
 
Dutch was sitting in the corner of the vault, his knees pulled up to his chest. The heavy Thompson submachine gun lay discarded on the floor beside him. The gang leader was staring blankly at the stacks of money, his eyes wide and unfocused.
 
The manic, romanticized illusion of the outlaw hero had completely shattered. The horrific reality of the dead walking the earth had finally broken his mind. He was weeping silently, the tears cutting clean tracks through the soot on his face.
 
"They ate the Kid," Dutch whispered, his voice trembling. "They just pulled him apart."
 
"Pull yourself together, Dutch," Emmett said coldly, kneeling beside Sheriff Miller.
 
The lawman was in bad shape. The forty-five caliber slug had shattered his lower ribs and torn through his intestines. The bleeding was severe. His khaki uniform shirt was entirely soaked in dark, coagulating blood.
 
Emmett reached onto the oak table and grabbed a stack of one-hundred-dollar bills. He ripped the paper bank band off the stack and pressed the thick wad of high-denomination currency directly against the sheriff's bleeding gut wound.
 
Miller groaned in agony as the safecracker applied heavy pressure.
 
"Hold that," Emmett ordered, pulling the sheriff's weak hands onto the bloody money. "Keep the pressure hard."
 
Emmett stood up and looked around the small steel room. The Zippo flame flickered, casting a dim, orange glow over the wealth and the horror. They had survived the immediate slaughter in the lobby, but the safecracker's pragmatic mind was already working on the next problem.
 
And the next problem was fatal.
 
Chapter Six: The Air
 
"Turn off the lighter," Sheriff Miller gasped from the floor.
 
"I need to see," Emmett replied, walking toward the massive steel door.
 
"You are burning the oxygen," Miller wheezed. "Turn it off."
 
Emmett stopped. He looked at the small, flickering yellow flame of the Zippo. He slowly lowered the lid, snapping the lighter shut. The absolute, suffocating darkness instantly returned to the vault.
 
"He is right," Emmett said into the black.
 
The First National Bank vault was designed to be waterproof, fireproof, and entirely airtight. The heavy rubber gaskets sealing the massive steel door ensured that not a single molecule of gas could pass in or out of the room.
 
It was a ten-by-ten foot box.
 
Emmett did the brutal, pragmatic math in his head. Three grown men, breathing heavily in a state of elevated panic, locked in a sealed cube. The oxygen consumption rate was critical.
 
"We have maybe ten hours," Emmett announced calmly. "Maybe twelve, if we stay perfectly calm and slow our heart rates."
 
"Ten hours until what?" Dutch asked from the corner. His voice was hollow.
 
"Until we suffocate," Emmett replied. "The carbon dioxide will build up in the room. We will get sleepy, we will get headaches, and then we will simply pass out and die."
 
Dutch let out a sharp, hysterical laugh. "Suffocate. We are sitting on fifty thousand dollars in cash, and we are going to choke to death."
 
The gang leader stood up in the dark. Emmett could hear him pacing frantically across the small room, his leather boots scuffing against the concrete floor.
 
"Sit down, Dutch," Emmett ordered sharply.
 
"I am not going to die in a box!" Dutch yelled, his panic spiking again. He kicked the heavy oak table, sending stacks of cash spilling onto the floor in the dark. "I am Dutch Vance! I am a legend! I am not going to die suffocating in the dark like a rat!"
 
"You are burning the air!" Emmett roared, his voice echoing loudly off the steel walls. "Every time you move, every time you shout, your muscles consume oxygen. You are killing us faster. Sit down and shut your mouth!"
 
Dutch stopped pacing. The heavy silence fell over the vault again, broken only by the ragged, desperate breathing of the wounded sheriff.
 
Emmett slid his back down the cold steel wall and sat on the floor. He closed his eyes in the pitch black. The irony was suffocating. They were surrounded by the greatest wealth they had ever known, but the only currency that mattered now was invisible, and they were spending it entirely too fast.
 
Chapter Seven: The Fever
 
Three hours passed in the absolute darkness.
 
The temperature inside the vault began to rise noticeably, heated entirely by the body heat of the three men. The air was growing stale, taking on a heavy, metallic taste.
 
"Water," Sheriff Miller rasped.
 
His voice was incredibly weak. Emmett flicked the Zippo lighter open for just a second, illuminating the lawman's face.
 
Miller was ghastly pale. Sweat was pouring down his forehead in thick sheets. His eyes were glassy and unfocused. The gut wound was terrible, but the massive blood loss was not the only thing killing him.
 
He was burning up with a massive fever.
 
"He is infected," Dutch said from the corner, his voice trembling. "The dead things bit him."
 
"They did not bite him," Emmett corrected, snapping the lighter shut to preserve the air. "I shot him. It is a dirty bullet. His gut is perforated. The infection is from his own stomach."
 
"It does not matter," Dutch said.
 
The gang leader was right. It did not matter how the sheriff died. The horrific rumors that had circulated the outlaw camps in the weeks before the robbery were terrifyingly clear on the rules of the new world.
 
If you were bitten, you died, and you turned.
 
But if you died of anything else—a heart attack, a dirty bullet, or simple old age—the result was exactly the same. The dead walked. Period.
 
Sheriff Miller was going to die. The massive trauma to his intestines was fatal without immediate surgical intervention, and there was no surgeon in the vault.
 
"He is going to turn," Dutch whispered into the dark. "When he dies, he is going to stand up in this box with us."
 
Emmett did not answer. He sat perfectly still in the blackness, listening to the sheriff's wet, ragged breathing. The lawman's lungs were filling with fluid. Every breath was a desperate, gurgling struggle.
 
"Emmett," Sheriff Miller whispered, his voice barely audible over the hum of the silence.
 
"I am here, Sheriff," Emmett said.
 
"I am dying," Miller wheezed. "You know it. I know it."
 
"Yes," Emmett agreed softly. There was no point in lying in the dark.
 
"You know what happens when I stop breathing," Miller said.
 
"Yes."
 
The vault was a ten-by-ten foot sealed box. It was pitch black. If the sheriff died and reanimated in the small, confined space, it would be a bloodbath. They would not be able to see him coming. They would only hear the snapping of the jaws in the dark before the teeth found them.
 
"Do it now," Miller whispered.
 
"Do what?" Dutch asked, his voice cracking with fear.
 
"The brain," Miller coughed, a wet, heavy sound. "You have to destroy the brain before the heart stops. Do it now, Emmett. Do not let me turn into one of those things."
 
Emmett sat in the dark, the heavy iron crowbar resting across his knees. He understood the horrific logic of the request. It was the only pragmatic choice.
 
Chapter Eight: The Execution
 
"You cannot do that," Dutch said, his voice rising in panic. He scrambled backward in the dark, his boots scraping loudly against the concrete floor until his spine hit the brass deposit boxes. "It is murder, Emmett. You shoot a lawman in a gunfight, that is business. But you don't cave a man's skull in while he is bleeding out on the floor. That ain't right."
 
Dutch was clinging desperately to the tattered remnants of his outlaw code. Even locked in an airtight box at the end of the world, he needed to believe he was an honorable thief.
 
"He is already dead, Dutch," Emmett said, his voice a flat, unemotional rasp. "The bullet killed him. I am just making sure he stays dead."
 
"I won't let you do it!" Dutch yelled, the hysteria bleeding fully into his voice. He patted the floor frantically in the dark, searching for the dropped Thompson submachine gun.
 
Emmett ignored him. He flicked the Zippo lighter open one last time.
 
The small flame illuminated the horrific scene. The sheriff was completely still, his chest rising in shallow, agonizing jerks. The blood on the floor was thick and black. Dutch was huddled in the corner, his pinstripe suit ruined, his hands frantically feeling the floorboards.
 
Emmett picked up the heavy iron crowbar. He did not hesitate. He did not look at the sheriff's face. He raised the heavy iron high above his head and brought it straight down.
 
The sound was heavy, wet, and absolutely final.
 
The heavy iron shattered the frontal bone completely, destroying the brain stem instantly. The sheriff's body went limp, the agonizing, wet breathing stopping abruptly.
 
Emmett snapped the lighter shut. The absolute blackness returned.
 
Dutch began to hyperventilate in the corner. His breaths were sharp, rapid gasps that echoed loudly off the steel walls.
 
"Control your breathing," Emmett ordered calmly, sliding his back down the wall and resting the bloody crowbar across his knees. "You are burning the oxygen. The sheriff is gone. We are safe for now."
 
"We are not safe," Dutch sobbed in the dark. "We are dead men. We are just dead men sitting on a pile of worthless paper."
 
Chapter Nine: The Suffocation
 
Several more hours passed. The exact measurement of time became impossible in the pitch black.
 
The air inside the vault grew incredibly heavy. The oxygen levels were dropping rapidly, replaced by the toxic buildup of carbon dioxide from their own lungs. The temperature was sweltering, the air thick with the smell of sweat, copper blood, and stale breath.
 
Emmett felt the lethargy setting into his bones. His limbs felt impossibly heavy, as if they were cast in lead. A dull, throbbing headache had settled firmly behind his eyes, a classic symptom of hypoxia.
 
He was dying, slowly and quietly, in the dark.
 
"Emmett," Dutch whispered.
 
The gang leader's voice was completely different. The panic and the hysteria were gone. He sounded strangely calm, almost euphoric.
 
"Emmett, listen to me," Dutch continued, his words slurring slightly. The lack of oxygen was acting as a narcotic on his brain. "They are waiting for us outside."
 
"The dead," Emmett corrected weakly, not opening his eyes.
 
"The posse," Dutch insisted. He was hallucinating. The hypoxia had finally fractured his mind, retreating into the comforting, romanticized delusion that had defined his life. "The whole town is out there, Emmett. The sheriff's deputies, the federal marshals, all of them. They have the bank surrounded."
 
"No, Dutch," Emmett rasped.
 
"We cannot stay in here," Dutch said. Emmett heard the rustle of fabric as the gang leader slowly stood up in the dark. "If we stay in here, we suffocate like rats. But if we go out there... if we go out there, they will write songs about us."
 
"Sit down," Emmett ordered, but his voice lacked any real authority. He was simply too weak to fight.
 
"Butch Cassidy and the Sundance Kid," Dutch mumbled, his boots shuffling slowly across the concrete floor toward the massive steel door. "They went out in a blaze of glory, Emmett. They did not die in a dark hole. They took the fight to the posse."
 
"Dutch, don't touch the door," Emmett said, forcing his heavy eyelids open. The darkness was absolute. He could not see the gang leader, but he could hear his heavy breathing near the vault entrance.
 
"I am going to give them a show, Emmett," Dutch said. He sounded happy. He sounded like a man who had finally found his purpose.
 
The loud, metallic clack of the heavy brass locking wheel spinning broke the silence.
 
Chapter Ten: The Blaze of Glory
 
"No!" Emmett yelled, the sudden surge of adrenaline briefly cutting through the suffocating fog in his brain.
 
He scrambled to his feet in the dark, grabbing the iron crowbar. But the physical effort was too much. The lack of oxygen caused his vision to swim violently, even in the pitch black. His knees buckled, and he collapsed back onto the concrete floor, his lungs burning for air.
 
"Get ready, Emmett!" Dutch yelled triumphantly.
 
The heavy steel locking bolts slid out of their striking plates with a deep, echoing thud.
 
Dutch grabbed the massive brass handle and threw his entire body weight backward, pulling the heavy door inward.
 
The sudden influx of light and air was a physical shock. The blinding afternoon Texas sun poured into the dark vault, illuminating the stacks of useless cash and the bloody body of the sheriff on the floor. The fresh oxygen hit Emmett's starving lungs like a wave of cold water, causing him to cough violently.
 
But the air was not empty.
 
The horde had not left. They had been standing silently in the ruined bank lobby, pressing their leathery faces against the thick steel of the vault door for hours, waiting patiently for the meat inside.
 
The second the door swung open, the dead poured into the vault.
 
"Come and get it, boys!" Dutch roared.
 
The delusional outlaw stood in the center of the vault, fully illuminated by the sunlight. He had found the empty Thompson submachine gun in the dark. He leveled the heavy weapon at the surging wall of rotting corpses and pulled the trigger.
 
The gun clicked empty.
 
"Bang! Bang!" Dutch yelled, his mind completely gone, imitating the sound of the gunfire as the dead descended upon him.
 
He did not fight. He stood tall, a wide, euphoric smile on his handsome face, as the first corpse sank its dry, yellow teeth directly into his throat. The horde swarmed him instantly, burying the legendary outlaw under a writhing pile of ragged clothing and snapping jaws.
 
Emmett lay on the floor near the back wall, too weak to stand.
 
He watched the chaotic slaughter unfold in the bright sunlight. He felt the cold iron of the crowbar in his hand, but he did not raise it. There was no point. The vault was breached. The bank was lost. The world was gone.
 
A leather-skinned corpse with a shattered jaw detached itself from the pile swarming Dutch. It turned its dead eyes toward the safecracker lying on the floor. It shuffled forward, its ruined boots leaving bloody footprints on the scattered, useless one-hundred-dollar bills.
 
Emmett closed his eyes, took one final, deep breath of the fresh, coppery air, and waited for the dark.
 
Story Five: The Stranded Carnival
 
Chapter One: The Breakdown
 
The convoy of brightly painted trucks looked absurd against the desolate, grey backdrop of the Oklahoma panhandle.
 
Barnaby's Traveling Spectacular was three days behind schedule, pushing hard through a blinding dust storm to reach the next county fair. The Great Depression had practically killed the entertainment business. People did not have a nickel to spare for bread, let alone a freak show, but Barnaby was a relentless optimist and a professional liar.
 
"Keep them moving!" Barnaby yelled over the roar of the wind, leaning out the window of the lead truck. The grit was sanding the paint off the vehicle doors.
 
The convoy consisted of four heavy transport trucks, each hauling a folded-up attraction or a canvas tent. The vehicles were old, poorly maintained, and completely unsuited for the harsh conditions of the Dust Bowl.
 
The disaster happened instantly.
 
The lead truck struck a massive, hidden sinkhole in the dirt road. The front right tire dropped into the void. The heavy steel axle snapped with a sound like a cannon shot. The truck pitched violently to the right, skidding to a grinding halt in a cloud of thick, choking brown dust.
 
The three trucks behind it slammed their brakes, swerving wildly to avoid a pileup.
 
"Damn it all to hell," Barnaby muttered, wiping the dirt from his eyes. He kicked his door open and dropped onto the road.
 
The wind was howling, carrying thousands of tons of topsoil through the air. Visibility was reduced to less than twenty feet. The temperature had dropped twenty degrees in an hour.
 
"What happened, boss?"
 
Tiny emerged from the second truck. He was the carnival's strongman, standing six-foot-eight and weighing over three hundred pounds of solid muscle. He wore a heavy wool coat over his leopard-print leotard, shivering in the sudden cold.
 
"Axle is snapped," Barnaby said, pointing at the ruined wheel. "We are not going anywhere tonight, Tiny. Tell the roustabouts to secure the canvas. We ride out the storm here."
 
Tiny nodded his massive head and disappeared into the blowing dust.
 
Barnaby pulled his collar up against the wind. They were stranded in the middle of nowhere. No town, no telephone, no mechanics. It was a logistical nightmare for a traveling show on the brink of bankruptcy.
 
He had no idea that the broken axle was the luckiest thing that had ever happened to them. Ten miles down the road, the town they were heading for had already fallen.
 
Chapter Two: The Fortune Teller
 
Madame Rosa sat in the back of the third truck, her heavy wooden wagon bolted securely to the flatbed.
 
The interior of her wagon was a masterpiece of cheap theatricality. Heavy velvet curtains hung from the walls, masking the corrugated steel. A crystal ball sat on a small table, illuminated by a flickering kerosene lantern.
 
Rosa was a complete fraud. She was a woman from New Jersey who adopted a fake accent and read cold cues from desperate farm wives. She did not possess the gift of second sight.
 
She poured herself a generous glass of cheap gin, her hands shaking from the violent jolt of the breakdown.
 
The wind howled against the thin walls of her wagon, a lonely, terrifying sound. She hated the Dust Bowl. She hated the poverty, the dirt, and the sheer emptiness of the landscape.
 
A sharp tap on the small, round window of her wagon made her jump.
 
Rosa set her glass down and pulled back the heavy velvet curtain. The dust storm was a solid wall of brown outside, but she could make out a dark silhouette standing on the flatbed of the truck, right outside her window.
 
"Barnaby?" she called out, assuming it was the boss checking on her.
 
The figure did not answer. It pressed its face directly against the thick glass of the window.
 
Rosa gasped, dropping her gin glass. It shattered on the wooden floorboards.
 
The face in the window was a horrific ruin. It was an older man, a farmer judging by the faded overalls, but his jaw was completely missing. The bottom half of his face had been violently torn away, leaving only a jagged stump of bone and a lolling, bloody tongue. The eyes staring back at her were completely white, devoid of any humanity.
 
The dead farmer raised a rotting hand and began to slap the glass.
 
Rosa stumbled backward, her fake accent instantly vanishing. "Help!" she screamed, her voice shrill with genuine terror. "Someone help me!"
 
The heavy wooden door of her wagon rattled violently. The dead man had found the brass handle. He was pulling at it with a mindless, relentless strength. The lock held, but the cheap wood of the door frame began to splinter.
 
Rosa backed against her fortune-telling table, her hands grasping the heavy glass crystal ball.
 
The wood cracked. The door flew open, the wind howling into the small space, bringing the smell of dry earth and rotting meat.
 
Chapter Three: The Strongman
 
Tiny heard the scream over the wind.
 
The massive strongman was near the rear of the convoy, securing the heavy canvas tarps over the folded Ferris wheel. He dropped his ropes and sprinted toward the third truck, his heavy boots shaking the ground.
 
He rounded the corner of the flatbed and saw the shapes moving in the dust.
 
There were dozens of them. The blowing topsoil had masked their approach, allowing the horde of wandering dead to simply stumble into the stranded convoy. They were scattered among the trucks, their arms outstretched, drawn by the noise and the heat of the engines.
 
Tiny saw the man in the overalls pulling at Madame Rosa's door.
 
The strongman did not ask questions. He did not care about the missing jaw or the white eyes. Someone was attacking the carnival family, and Tiny was fiercely protective of his own.
 
He reached into the bed of the nearest truck and grabbed his working tool. It was a massive, twenty-pound iron sledgehammer with a four-foot oak handle, used for driving the main stakes of the big top tent.
 
Tiny swung the hammer with the ease of a man swatting a fly.
 
The heavy iron head struck the jawless farmer squarely in the center of the back. The sheer kinetic force of the blow shattered the spine instantly, folding the corpse backward over itself. The dead man collapsed off the flatbed and into the dust.
 
"Tiny!" Rosa sobbed, standing in the doorway of her wagon, clutching her crystal ball.
 
"Stay inside, Rosa," Tiny rumbled, his deep voice carrying easily over the storm.
 
He turned to face the rest of the horde.
 
Three more corpses were advancing on him through the dust. They were gaunt, starving Dust Bowl victims who had succumbed to the infection on the roads.
 
Tiny stepped forward. He swung the massive sledgehammer in a wide, horizontal arc. The iron head connected with the side of the first corpse's skull. The sound was like a bursting watermelon. The skull simply disintegrated under the three hundred pounds of muscle driving the iron.
 
The strongman used the momentum to bring the hammer back around, catching the second corpse under the chin. The blow launched the rotting body into the air, its neck snapping with a sharp crack.
 
The third corpse lunged, its teeth snapping at Tiny's thick arm.
 
Tiny simply dropped the hammer, reached out with his massive hands, and grabbed the creature by the throat. He lifted the dead man completely off the ground, his thick fingers crushing the windpipe. With a brutal, effortless twist, Tiny snapped the neck and tossed the limp body into the ditch.
 
"Barnaby!" Tiny roared into the storm. "We have trouble!"
 
Chapter Four: The Funhouse
 
Barnaby was already running. He had grabbed a heavy tire iron from the cab of the lead truck.
 
The situation was deteriorating rapidly. The dust storm was blinding them, but the dead did not rely on sight. They were homing in on the smell of sweat and fear. The scattered roustabouts were fighting desperate, hand-to-hand battles in the dirt between the trucks.
 
A young clown, still wearing his greasepaint, was dragged down by three corpses near the Ferris wheel. His screams were quickly muffled.
 
"We cannot hold the ground!" Barnaby yelled, dodging the grasping hands of a dead woman in a faded floral dress. He swung the tire iron, catching her on the temple and dropping her to the dirt.
 
He looked around frantically. They needed a fortified position, but the trucks were open flatbeds, and the canvas tents offered zero protection.
 
His eyes landed on the fourth truck in the convoy.
 
"The Funhouse!" Barnaby screamed, his voice hoarse from the dust. "Everyone into the Funhouse!"
 
The attraction was folded up on the back of the massive flatbed. It was a large, rectangular wooden structure, painted with garish neon clowns and warped geometric shapes. It was designed to be a disorienting maze of mirrors, trapdoors, and dead ends.
 
Crucially, it had heavy wooden walls and only one entrance.
 
Tiny heard the order. He picked up his massive sledgehammer and began clearing a path through the horde, his heavy swings crushing bone and spraying dark blood into the blowing dust.
 
"Move!" Tiny roared, grabbing roustabouts by their collars and shoving them toward the fourth truck.
 
Barnaby climbed onto the flatbed and threw open the heavy wooden door of the Funhouse. The interior was pitch black.
 
"Get inside!" he yelled as the surviving members of the carnival scrambled up the side of the truck.
 
Madame Rosa ran past him, her crystal ball still clutched tightly to her chest. Three roustabouts followed, bleeding and terrified. Tiny was the last man on the ground, holding off a dozen corpses with massive, sweeping arcs of his hammer.
 
"Tiny, come on!" Barnaby yelled.
 
The strongman crushed one last skull, threw the bloody sledgehammer into the back of the truck, and vaulted onto the flatbed with surprising agility for a man his size.
 
He dove through the door of the Funhouse just as the leading edge of the horde reached the truck.
 
Barnaby grabbed the heavy wooden door and slammed it shut, throwing the thick iron deadbolt on the inside.
 
The noise of the storm was instantly muffled. They were standing in the narrow, pitch-black entry corridor of the maze. Outside, dozens of heavy fists began to pound against the painted wood.
 
"We are safe," Rosa sobbed in the dark.
 
"No," Barnaby said grimly, feeling the wooden walls shake under the relentless pressure. "We are trapped in a wooden box, and they are going to tear it apart."
 
Chapter Five: The Mirrors
 
"I have a lantern," Tiny rumbled in the dark.
 
He fumbled in the deep pockets of his wool coat and pulled out a heavy brass flashlight. He clicked the button. The sharp beam of yellow light cut through the blackness, illuminating the narrow corridor.
 
They were standing at the entrance of the Hall of Mirrors.
 
The walls were lined with cheap, distorted glass. Reflections bounced wildly off the surfaces, creating an immediate, nauseating sense of claustrophobia. Dozens of panicked Madame Rosas, bleeding roustabouts, and massive strongmen stared back at them from every angle.
 
A loud, splintering crack echoed from the wall directly behind Barnaby.
 
The painted plywood of the Funhouse exterior was thin. It was built to be lightweight for easy transport, not to withstand a siege. A rotting, dirt-caked hand punched completely through the cheap wood, its fingers grasping blindly in the narrow space.
 
"They are breaking through the hull!" one of the roustabouts screamed, backing away from the thrashing hand.
 
"Move deeper into the maze," Barnaby ordered, pushing past the terrified boy. "Get away from the exterior walls. Stick to the center of the truck bed."
 
The small group shuffled forward into the maze. The flashlight beam bounced off the funhouse mirrors, creating a disorienting kaleidoscope of light and shadow. It was impossible to tell which way was forward and which way was simply a reflection of a dead end.
 
The pounding on the outside of the truck grew louder, a continuous, deafening drumbeat of rotting fists against cheap wood. The entire structure was vibrating.
 
More hands began to punch through the walls.
 
The painted plywood splintered inward in a dozen different places. Sun-baked arms, missing skin, and rotting muscle reached into the Hall of Mirrors, snapping blindly at the passing survivors.
 
"Stay in the center!" Barnaby yelled, swinging his tire iron to break the wrist of a corpse that had reached too far into the corridor.
 
Tiny walked at the rear of the group, using his massive body to shield Madame Rosa. He grabbed the wrists of the corpses that broke through the walls and simply snapped them like dry twigs, leaving the dead hands hanging uselessly from the splintered holes.
 
They turned a corner in the maze, leaving the mirrors behind and entering the spinning tunnel section.
 
"The floor is moving," Rosa whimpered, looking down at the painted wooden planks.
 
"It is just a trick," Barnaby said. "Keep moving."
 
But the funhouse was full of tricks, and in the dark, with the dead breaking through the walls, the cheap illusions were becoming deadly traps.
 
Chapter Six: The Trapdoors
 
The floor of the Funhouse was uneven, designed with hidden pressure plates that triggered sudden drops, blasts of compressed air, and loud, startling noises.
 
Under normal circumstances, the attraction was powered by a loud diesel generator, and the traps were harmless pranks meant to elicit screams from teenagers. But the generator was off. The mechanics were dead.
 
The roustabout walking in front of Barnaby stepped heavily onto a painted clown face on the floorboards.
 
A sharp, metallic click echoed through the dark corridor.
 
The floor beneath the boy's boots instantly gave way. It was a simple trapdoor designed to drop the floor two inches to scare patrons, but the heavy iron spring mechanism had rusted and snapped.
 
The trapdoor swung open completely.
 
The roustabout fell. He did not drop two inches; he dropped straight through the floor of the Funhouse and into the dark, mechanical underbelly of the flatbed truck.
 
He landed hard on the iron chassis, his leg twisting painfully in the gears of the spinning tunnel mechanism.
 
"Help me!" the boy screamed, looking up through the square hole in the floorboards. "My leg is stuck!"
 
Barnaby dropped to his knees, reaching his hand down into the dark hole. "Grab my hand, kid. I've got you."
