The train did not stop. It rolled past the depot, disappearing into the blowing grit of the south curve, the clatter of its wheels fading into the static of the wind. Clara watched it go, her heart hammering against her ribs. The train was a ghost, a machine running on the last of its steam, carrying the dead to nowhere. The wind closed in behind it, erasing the tracks in a fresh drift of red sand.
 
Chapter Four: The Night Shift
 
The depot was cold. They had burned the last of the coal at noon, and Clara refused to light the kerosene heater.
 
"The smell," she said when Arthur reached for the matches. "It carries through the floorboards. The dead do not see well in the dust, but they smell the oil. They smell us. A flame is a beacon, Arthur. We stay in the dark."
 
They sat in the dark, the silence broken only by the clicking of Clara's key as she sent a continuous query into the dead lines. CQ CQ CQ Cleburne calling. Anyone. The sound was lonely, a tiny spark of electricity searching for an ear in the dark.
 
The wind outside was rising, rattling the loose corrugated tin on the roof of the baggage room. Every creak of the timber made Arthur flinch. Clara kept her hand on the key, her fingers numb, but she didn't stop. She remembered Henry teaching her the code twenty years ago, when she was a young bride and he was the new agent. "The wire is a river," he had said. "You have to keep it flowing." Now, the river had dried up, leaving only dry stones.
 
"Clara," Arthur said from the dark corner. "Do you think my mother made it to the church?"
 
Clara stopped her hand. The silence that followed was heavy. She thought of the Methodist church at the edge of town, its heavy oak doors and narrow windows. She thought of the three hundred people who had gathered there when the first reports came from the highway. She had heard the shooting at noon—three short, ragged volleys from the sheriff’s deputies, followed by a long, terrible silence that was eventually filled by the sound of wood splintering and screaming.
 
"Your mother is a sensible woman, Arthur," Clara said, her voice steady, lying with the practiced ease of a mother. "She would have kept the cellar door locked. She would have stayed away from the crowd."
 
"She had her Bible," Arthur whispered. "She said God wouldn't let the dead walk. She said it was a test."
 
"Do you think anyone is left in Wichita?" Arthur asked. "Or Oklahoma City?"
 
"I don't know," Clara said. "The last message we got from the dispatcher in Wichita was yesterday. He said they were abandoning the station. He said the streets were full of them. Then he just stopped mid-sentence."
 
Clara did not answer. She had seen the look on the face of the engineer. There was no test in those blank eyes. Only hunger. She reached for the key again, her fingers cold. CQ... CQ... The sounder was silent. The wire felt cold and dead, a line running into a grave.
 
Chapter Five: The Static
 
At midnight, the sounder clicked once.
 
It was not the crisp, clean signal of an operator. It was a long, dragging scrape, the iron armature pulling down and staying down for several seconds, then releasing with a metallic ping.
 
Clara bent over the table, her ear close to the instrument, her breath catching.
 
Click.
 
Dash.
 
Dot. Dot. Dot. Dash. Dash.
 
It was a mess of letters, letters that made no words. T-X-M-N-O-E-E-E.
 
"Is it a code?" Arthur asked, leaning over her shoulder. His breath was warm against her cheek, smelling of the stale cornmeal they had eaten for breakfast.
 
"No," Clara whispered. She looked at the brass key. "Someone is leaning on the key. They are not trying to send a message. They are just resting their weight against the desk. Or their face. They're dead, Arthur, and they've fallen onto the key."
 
The sounder clicked again, a rapid, frantic succession of dots that sounded like a mechanical shudder. Then, it died completely. The line did not even have the low hum of the batteries anymore. The circuit was open. The wires had been broken physically, somewhere close to the station. The line was dead, and they were completely alone in the dark.
 
"Why would they break the wire?" Arthur asked.
 
"They didn't break it on purpose," Clara said. "They stumble. They walk into the poles. Or the horses... if there are any horses left. They drag themselves along the fence lines and the wires get caught in their clothes. They pull them down by weight alone."
 
She touched the metal armature of the sounder. It was cold, without the faint, electrical vibration that usually hummed through the coil. She felt a sudden, sharp pang of isolation. The telegraph was her shield, her window to the world. Now, the window was shuttered, the shield shattered.
 
Arthur walked back to the woodbox, his legs shaking. "What do we do now?"
 
"We wait for dawn," Clara said. "In the light, we can see them coming. In the dark, we only hear them."
 
She looked at the clock. The pendulum swung back and forth, its steady tick-tock the only constant in the room. Three hours until dawn. The longest three hours of her life.
 
Chapter Six: The Shadows
 
They heard the boots on the platform at two in the morning.
 
It was not the quick, purposeful stride of a traveler. It was a heavy, dragging sound, the leather soles scraping against the rough pine planks. Scrape. Pause. Thud. Scrape. Pause. Thud.
 
Arthur rose, his knuckles white around the iron poker. Clara felt her skin go cold.
 
Through the frosted glass of the bay window, Clara saw the silhouette. It was tall, its head tilted at an unnatural angle, one shoulder hanging lower than the other. It pressed its face against the glass, the nose flattening against the pane.
 
The white glare of the emergency lantern in the yard caught its features. It was Old Man Miller, the station agent from the next siding north. His nose was gone, his lips torn back from his teeth in a dry, frozen snarl. He did not knock. He simply leaned his forehead against the pane, the glass creaking under the weight of his skull.
 
Another figure emerged from the blowing sand behind him—a woman in a floral print dress, her skirt torn to ribbons, her hands reaching out blindly toward the window. Her fingers tapped against the glass, a soft, horrific echo of Clara's telegraph key. Tap. Tap. Tap.
 
"It's Mrs. Gable," Arthur whispered, his voice failing him. "From the bakery."
 
"Don't look at her, Arthur," Clara said, her voice a low command. She pulled him back from the window. "Look at the floor. Don't let them see your eyes."
 
The tapping continued, joined by the sound of fingernails scratching against the wood of the door. It was a dry, insect-like sound that made Clara's nails dig into her palms. They were testing the wood, searching for a gap, a way inside to the warmth of the living.
 
More shadows appeared on the platform, silhouettes rising from the red dust like spirits from the grave. Clara counted six, then eight. They moved with a slow, mechanical gait, their limbs stiff, their heads nodding as if they were asleep. But they were not sleeping. They were drawn by the smell of life, the faint heat of two bodies trapped in a wooden box.
 
The glass in the bay window groaned as another face pressed against it. The frame creaked. Clara knew the window wouldn't hold. The glass was thin, and the wood was old and dry.
 
Chapter Seven: The Hand Crank
 
"The backup generator," Clara said, her voice a sharp whisper that cut the silence. "In the battery shed. We need the power if we are going to send anything. The batteries in here are dying. The main lines are dead, but if we can get the generator running, we can send a signal on the emergency wire. It runs along the roof line to the water tower."
 
"The shed is twenty feet across the gravel," Arthur said, his voice trembling. "They are on the platform. We can't go out there. They'll catch us."
 
"They are slow, Arthur. If we move together, we can reach the door. We have to try. If we stay here, we're trapped when the window breaks."
 
They slipped out the rear door of the baggage room, the cold air hitting Clara’s face like a slap. The wind had dropped slightly, but the air was still thick with red dust that tasted of iron and sand.
 
A shadow moved near the water crane. It was the telegraph lineman, Jesse, his heavy leather tool belt clinking as he shuffled forward. He was missing his left arm below the elbow, the sleeve pinned to his shirt with a rusted safety pin. His gray horse was nowhere to be seen.
 
Arthur lunged forward, the iron poker striking the lineman’s temple with a hollow, wooden crack. The lineman fell, but he did not groan. He immediately began to crawl, his fingers digging into the gravel as he turned his dead eyes back toward the boy.
 
Clara reached the shed, her key turning the brass lock. They slipped inside, slamming the heavy oak door just as the lineman’s boots scraped against the bottom step. Arthur leaned against the door, panting, his forehead pressed against the wood.
 
"We made it," he gasped.
 
"Not yet," Clara said, looking around the dark room. "Get the generator ready."
 
The battery shed was built of heavy oak timbers to protect the acid cells from the heat of the summer and the cold of the winter. It was stronger than the depot, but it had no windows except for a small ventilation screen near the roof. The air inside was thick with the smell of sulfur and grease.
 
On the shelf stood the row of lead-acid batteries, their glass jars cracked and dry. They were useless. The only hope was the hand-cranked generator.
 
Clara listened to the door. The lineman was scratching at the wood outside. Scrape... scrape... His fingernails were breaking against the oak, but he didn't stop. He had all the time in the world.
 
Chapter Eight: The Window Screen
 
The battery shed was small, smelling of sulfuric acid and damp timber. Clara found the hand-cranked emergency generator, a heavy iron box with a double handle.
 
"Crank it," she ordered, pointing to the handles.
 
Arthur gripped the handles, his shoulders straining as he turned the iron gears. The generator hummed, a low, rising whine that sent a dim, yellow glow to the single bulb overhead. The light flickered, casting long, monstrous shadows across the shelves of acid jars.
 
Outside, the scraping had multiplied. There were four of them now, their dead fingers clawing at the pine siding of the shed. One of them found the small window screen. The wire mesh groaned as a rotting hand pressed through, the fingers curling around the wooden frame.
 
Clara did not run. She took a heavy iron wrench from the shelf and struck the fingers, breaking the bones against the sill. The hand did not retreat. It continued to press forward, the broken bones clicking against the wood as the creature tried to pull its chest through the narrow opening.
 
"Keep turning," Clara whispered, her face covered in sweat. "Don't look at the window, Arthur. Keep turning."
 
The smell of the rotting flesh was thick now, coming through the broken screen. It was the smell of the cellar, the smell of the dry earth when it turned. Clara struck the hand again, but the skin was leathery, the muscle cold and dry. There was no blood, only a gray fluid that smeared the white paint of the sill.
 
Arthur was crying now, the tears leaving clean tracks through the soot on his face. His breath came in ragged, sobbing gasps, but he kept his arms moving, turning the iron crank with a frantic, desperate strength. The generator's whine rose to a high shriek.
 
"I can't," he whispered. "My arms..."
 
"You can," Clara said, her voice hard. "You turn that crank or we die in the dark. Turn it, Arthur!"
 
She watched the window. Another hand appeared, the fingers clawing at the screen. The wooden frame was beginning to split. She stood her ground, the heavy wrench raised, her teeth clenched. She had survived the dust and the debt; she would not let them take her in a dark shed.
 
Chapter Nine: The Roof Line
 
The sound of wood splintering came from the main depot.
 
The dead had broken the bay window. Clara could hear the heavy glass crashing onto the office floor, followed by the clatter of the telegraph desk being overturned. The sounder was gone, smashed under their boots.
 
"We cannot go back," Arthur said, his voice shaking as he cranked the generator. The light bulb flickered, casting long, dancing shadows across the acid jars.
 
"We don't need to," Clara said. She connected the generator leads directly to the auxiliary key on the workbench. The wires ran up through the ceiling, connecting to the main line that climbed the tall wooden pole outside the shed.
 
She sat on a wooden crate, her fingers resting on the auxiliary key. The metal was cold, but the current was there. She could feel the faint, electric tickle in her fingertips, the pulse of the generator running through the wire.
 
Dot. Dash. Dot. Dot.
 
"Who are you sending to?" Arthur asked, his breath coming in ragged gasps.
 
"Anyone," Clara said. "The line to the west is still clear. Maybe the station at the border is still open. Maybe they can hear us."
 
She tapped the key, her hand steady. The clatter of the auxiliary key was loud in the small shed, a rhythmic drumming that competed with the scratching at the door. The wire was a thin thread of copper running across the desert, carrying her voice into the void.
 
The roof of the shed creaked as something dragged itself across the tin. They were climbing, trying to find a way down. Clara didn't look up. She kept her eyes on the key.
 
"If they hear us," Arthur said, "what will they do?"
 
"They'll know we were here," Clara said. "They'll know we held the line."
 
She knew there was no rescue coming. The world was dying, its lights going out one by one. But she had a duty. She was the operator of Cleburne. She would keep the wire alive until the end.
 
The generator was running hot, the smell of scorched oil mixing with the acid fumes. Arthur's shirt was soaked with sweat.
 
"Faster, Arthur," she said. "Keep the current steady."
 
He nodded, his eyes wide with terror, his teeth grinding as he forced the crank to turn. The light bulb glowed a bright, angry orange.
 
Chapter Ten: The Silent Key
 
Clara did not send a warning. She did not ask for help.
 
She knew there was no help. She had seen the train; she had heard the silence of the regional loop. Instead, she began to tap the names.
 
She tapped her husband's name: HENRY.
 
She tapped Arthur's mother's name: MARY.
 
She tapped the names of the farmers who had lived along the siding, the names she had written on the freight manifests for twenty years. She sent them out into the dark, one by one, the dots and dashes traveling along the copper wire, carrying the memory of the living into a world that was rapidly becoming silent.
 
The door of the shed began to give way. The heavy hinges groaned, the wood splitting around the lock. The lineman's fingers appeared through the crack, the gray skin torn and bleeding.
 
Arthur stopped cranking. His arms were shaking, his chest heaving. He looked at Clara, his eyes begging for an answer.
 
"Is anyone listening?" he asked.
 
Clara did not answer. She kept her fingers on the key, her rhythm unbroken. Dot, dot, dash, dot.
 
The door gave with a loud, final crack. A flood of grey, dust-covered figures poured through the opening, their snapping teeth catching the dim, dying light of the bulb. Clara did not look up from the key. She tapped the last letter—a single, long dash—and held the key down, sending a continuous, unbroken scream of electricity along the wire as the dark took the room.
 
The sounder at the next station would hum, a long, steady note that would not stop until the batteries died or the wire was cut. It would be the final voice of Cleburne, a single line of light in the dark.
 
Arthur did not run. He stood beside the generator, the fire poker raised in his hands, his face set in a grim, hopeless mask. As the first of the dead reached him, he swung the poker with all his strength, a loud crack echoing in the small room.
 
Clara felt the cold fingers wrap around her shoulder, the sharp bite of teeth sinking into her neck. She did not scream. She only held the key down, her hand steady, her eyes closed, as the current faded into the silence of the dust.
 
Story Eighteen: The Grain Elevator
 
Chapter One: The Tower
 
The concrete silos of the Farmers' Cooperative Elevator rose one hundred and twenty feet above the flat, sun-baked soil of Ochiltree County. They were the tallest structures for fifty miles in any direction, massive, windowless concrete pillars that looked like the ruins of some ancient, forgotten temple rising from the red dust of the Panhandle. In the pre-dawn light, they cast long, gray shadows that stretched across the empty railway siding, where a line of wooden boxcars sat rusting on the rails, their doors open and empty.
 
Otis stood on the metal catwalk that ran along the top of the silos, looking out over the barren expanse. His eyes were red-rimmed and crusty, his throat raw, his lungs burning with the fine, powdery grain dust that hung constantly in the air. He wore a damp rag cut from an old flannel shirt tied across his nose and mouth, but it did little to filter the smell of wheat, hot grease, and the faint, sweet rot of decay drifting from below. He had worked this elevator since the concrete was poured in the spring of '27, when the wheat was thick and the prices were high enough to buy a man a decent tractor. Now, the wheat was just a dry, dangerous mountain, and the prices didn't matter anymore.
 
Virgil, his fifteen-year-old son, sat on the steel cover of Bin Number Four, his legs swinging over the edge. His boots were worn thin, the soles held together with copper wire. He held a rusted iron crescent wrench in his lap, his fingers tapping against the metal in a nervous, arhythmic beat.
 
"How many, Pa?" Virgil asked, his voice quiet, muffled by the bandana tied over his mouth.
 
Otis leaned over the iron railing and looked down. At the base of the tower, the gravel driveway was choked with a slow-moving mass of grey, dust-covered figures. They looked like ants crawling over a pile of spilled sugar. There were hundreds of them, their upturned faces white and featureless from the height, their arms reaching up toward the sky. They didn't scream, they didn't call out. There was only the dry, rattling sigh of their breathing and the clicking of their teeth.
 
"Too many to count, son," Otis said. "They must have walked all the way from Perryton. The town is empty. And the wind is picking up from the north. The dust will be here by noon."
 
Chapter Two: The Bin
 
The grain elevator was a fortress of concrete and steel, but it was also a tomb.
 
When the horde had swept through the town of Perryton, Otis had gathered twelve of the local farmers, men he had done business with for years, and retreated into the elevator. They had pulled up the iron ladder that led to the ground floor loading bay and locked the heavy steel chute doors with heavy chains. They had thought the height would keep them safe, that the concrete walls would be an impenetrable shield against the madness.
 
They were safe from the teeth, but they were trapped.
 
They sat in the dark of Bin Number Four, a massive concrete cylinder sixty feet deep and twenty feet across, half-filled with raw winter wheat. The grain was cool and dry, but it was also unstable, a treacherous floor that could turn deadly in an instant. If a man fell into the center of the bin while the grain was shifting, he would be sucked down into the golden kernels, his mouth and nose filled with chaff, suffocated within seconds.
 
"We have enough wheat to feed the state," said Ben, a local sharecropper, his voice coming from the deep shadows near the concrete wall. Ben had lost his farm to the bank in '32, and his eyes had held a hollow, desperate look ever since.
 
"You can't eat raw wheat, Ben," Otis said. He sat on a wooden crate near the ladder. "Not without water to boil it. And our water keg is already half-empty. We have twelve men and a boy, and two gallons of water left. That wheat won't do us any good when our tongues swell up."
 
"We could crush it," another farmer, Frank, suggested. "Make a mash."
 
"With what?" Otis asked. "We have no tools but a few wrenches and a shovel. And no light. We stay still. The more we move, the more water we burn."
 
The grain beneath them shifted with a soft, rustling hiss, a reminder of the depth and the danger of their golden floor.
 
Chapter Three: The Dust Cloud
 
The dust storm hit at noon.
 
It was not a normal storm, not even by the standards of the dry years. It was a rolling wall of black earth, a "black blizzard" that rose ten thousand feet into the sky, completely blocking the sun and plunging the top of the silos into pitch darkness. The wind howled through the steel vents at the top of the tower, a high, screaming whistle that rattled the sheet-metal roof. It carried the fine, powdery topsoil through the cracks, a dry rain of red sand that mixed with the wheat dust inside the bins.
 
The air became thick, almost solid. It tasted of iron and dead earth.
 
Otis listened to the sound of the wind, but he also listened to the sound of the dead below. The wind carried their moans, a low, rhythmic drone that vibrated through the concrete walls of the silos. It was as if the tower itself were groaning, a concrete monument crying out in the dark.
 
"It's getting hard to breathe," Virgil whispered, coughing into his sleeve. The boy had crawled close to his father, his hand gripping the sleeve of Otis's heavy canvas coat.
 
"Keep the rag wet, Virgil," Otis said, his own voice raspy. He reached into his pocket and poured a few drops of water from his canteen onto the boy's mask. "The dust is settling. If we move too much, we will kick up the wheat chaff, and then we won't be able to breathe at all. The chaff gets in your throat and it swells. Just sit tight."
 
In the dark, the farmers were silent, but Otis could hear them shifting, their boots crunching against the grain. Every movement sent a small hiss of wheat sliding down the slopes of the bin, a reminder of the depth beneath them.
 
Chapter Four: The Lantern
 
"Light a lantern," Ben said, his voice tight with panic. He was breathing hard, the sound wet and shallow. "I can't see my hands. I don't know who's next to me. Frank? Frank, are you there?"
 
"I'm here, Ben," Frank's voice came from the opposite wall, sounding thin and dry. "Just sit down."
 
"No matches," Otis said, his voice rising in warning, sharp and clear over the wind. "The grain dust in here is thick enough to choke a horse. One spark, and the whole tower will blow."
 
"We're going to die in the dark anyway!" another farmer, a young man named Tommy, yelled. "Let's see what we're doing! We have three kerosene lanterns on the shelf. Just one!"
 
Otis reached out in the dark, his hand finding the collar of Ben’s shirt. He pulled the man close, his grip tightening until he could feel the fabric strain. "Listen to me, you fool," Otis whispered, his voice trembling with a cold anger. "The air in a grain elevator is like gunpowder. The dust hangs in the air, waiting for a flame. If you strike a match, the explosion will split these concrete walls like a ripe watermelon. We will be buried under five thousand bushels of burning wheat, or blown to pieces before we hit the ground. You want to see? You'll see the fire for a second, and then you'll never see anything again."
 
Ben did not answer, but Otis could hear his rapid, shallow breathing in the dark. Slowly, Otis let go of the collar. The silence returned, heavier than before, filled only with the smell of the dust and the heat of their bodies.
 
"I saw an explosion in Kansas back in '29," Frank said quietly from the dark. "Split a concrete silo right down the middle. Blew the roof two hundred yards into a wheat field. Nothing left but ash."
 
Chapter Five: The Suffocation
 
By the second day, the air in the bin was turning sour.
 
The vents at the top of the silos had been choked by the blowing sand, and the heat inside the concrete tower was rising. The moisture from the farmers' breath was making the grain sweat, creating a damp, musty heat that smelled of rot and fermentation.
 
"Pa," Virgil whispered, his hand finding Otis’s arm. "The dead... they are climbing."
 
"They can't climb concrete, Virgil," Otis said. "There are no ladders on the outside, and the walls are smooth. They can't reach us."
 
"They aren't climbing the walls," Virgil said, his voice shaking. "They are piling up. In the driveway. Look."
 
Otis crawled to the edge of the catwalk and looked down through the small hatch. The storm had passed, leaving a sickly yellow light. The dead were packing themselves into the narrow space between the two main silos, driven by the smell of the living above. The pressure of the crowd was so great that the creatures at the bottom were being crushed, their bones breaking under the weight as more of the dead were pushed on top of them.
 
They had created a writhing, shifting ramp of rotting flesh that was already twenty feet high, reaching toward the lower machinery platform. The lineman's tool belt, the overalls of the town marshal, the summer dresses of farm wives—all of it was mixed into a gray, crawling hill of bodies.
 
"They'll be at the catwalk by tomorrow," Virgil muttered.
 
Chapter Six: The Snapping
 
The sound of the dead was different now.
 
It was no longer a distant moan. It was the sound of dry teeth snapping against the steel chute doors at the base of the elevator. The creatures were pressing their faces into the small gaps in the metal, their jaws clicking in a rhythmic, mechanical frenzy.
 
"They want the wheat," Ben muttered, his mind beginning to wander. He had been staring into the dark for thirty hours, and his voice had a dry, hollow laugh to it. "They want the harvest. They're hungry, Otis. We should feed them."
 
"They don't care about the wheat, Ben," Otis said. "They want the meat. They want us."
 
Tommy could not stand the darkness any longer. The air was too hot, the smell of the rotting grain and their own unwashed bodies suffocating. He began to climb the iron ladder that led to the catwalk, his boots slipping on the dusty rungs.
 
"Get down, Tommy!" Otis called out, standing up on the grain. "The catwalk is exposed! Don't go up there!"
 
But Tommy did not listen. He reached the catwalk and ran toward the outer hatch, desperate for a breath of fresh air, even if it was filled with dust. He threw open the heavy steel door, and the wind screamed through the opening, carrying a cloud of red sand and the sound of a hundred snapping jaws. He stood there, his head thrown back, inhaling the hot, dusty air, oblivious to the danger.
 
Chapter Seven: The Conveyor
 
A figure stood on the catwalk, silhouetted against the dusty sky.
 
It was not Tommy.
 
A zombie had managed to crawl up the conveyor housing from the lower platform, its limbs mangled by the iron gears, its clothes torn to rags. Its jaw was split, hanging by a thread of grey sinew, but it did not stop. It dragged itself along the steel grating, its hands reaching out toward Tommy.
 
Tommy screamed, backing away, his boots slipping on the grain dust that covered the catwalk. He lost his balance, his heel catching on the edge of the guardrail. He fell backward, dropping through the open hatch of Bin Number Four.
 
He did not hit the concrete floor sixty feet below. He hit the wheat.
 
The dry winter wheat shifted instantly under his weight. It was like falling into quicksand. The grain rose around his chest, his frantic struggles only pulling him deeper into the golden kernels. Every movement caused the wheat to slide down, filling the space around him.
 
"Don't move, Tommy!" Otis yelled, scrambling toward the ladder, reaching for the rope they had tied to the railing. "Keep still! If you struggle, you'll sink!"
 
But Tommy was panicking, his arms thrashing, his legs kicking. The wheat closed over his head, his muffled screams dying out as the grain settled into a smooth, silent cone. The wheat looked exactly as it had before, flat and golden, with no sign of the man beneath.
 
Chapter Eight: The Spark
 
"He's gone," Ben whispered. "He's under the wheat. He's down there with the harvest. We have to dig him out!"
 
"If you dig, you will sink too," Frank said, his voice flat. "The wheat is too loose. We can't save him. He's dead, Ben."
 
"We need a light!" Ben screamed, his voice breaking into a manic sob. He scrambled toward the shelf in the dark, his hands knocking over tools. "I'm not dying in the dark with a dead man under my feet! I want to see! I want to see!"
 
A scrape of metal against stone echoed through the bin.
 
Otis lunged in the direction of the sound, his boots sinking into the wheat. "No! Ben, don't!" he roared.
 
But the match had already struck.
 
The small, yellow flame flared in the darkness, illuminating Ben’s gaunt, terrified face. His eyes were wide, his skin gray with dust. For a single fraction of a second, Otis saw the air around them. It was not empty. It was filled with a thick, swirling cloud of golden chaff, dry wheat dust, and sand, hanging like a fog in the small room.
 
The fire did not start as a roar. It started as a flash of blue light that zipped across the ceiling of the bin, a silent, beautiful wave of electricity that ignited the dust. Then, the air itself caught fire, expanding with terrifying speed.
 
Chapter Nine: The Chute
 
The explosion was deafening.
 
The pressure inside the concrete silo blew the heavy steel cover off Bin Number Four, sending it crashing through the sheet-metal roof of the tower. The concrete walls did not crumble, but they cracked, a spiderweb of fractures opening to let the dust-choked wind howl through the bin.
 
The wheat was on fire.
 
A sheet of orange flame swept across the surface of the grain, consuming the remaining oxygen in seconds. Otis was thrown against the concrete wall, his shirt scorched, his lungs burning as he gasped for air that was no longer there. The heat was immense, the smell of burning wheat thick and sweet, like baking bread, but choking.
 
"Virgil!" he screamed, his voice a hoarse croak.
 
He crawled through the burning grain, his hands blistering against the hot kernels. He found the boy near the ladder, his face covered in soot, his eyes wide with terror, his clothes smoking.
 
"The chute!" Otis gasped, grabbing the boy's collar. "The release chute!"
 
He dragged Virgil toward the emergency discharge lever. If they opened the bottom chute, the grain would dump into the railway loading bay below, carrying them out of the burning silo before they suffocated.
 
Chapter Ten: The Fire
 
Otis pulled the heavy iron lever with all his remaining strength.
 
The iron gate at the bottom of the bin slid open with a screech. The burning wheat shifted violently, a golden whirlpool that sucked Otis and Virgil down into the dark vortex. They tumbled through the steel pipe, surrounded by hot grain and choking smoke.
 
They fell through the chute, dropping heavily onto the concrete floor of the railway loading bay. The air was thick with smoke and red dust, but it was cold, and they could breathe.
 
They were out of the silo.
 
But they were not alone.
 
The explosion had shattered the lower loading doors, and the railway bay was filled with the dead. The heat of the burning wheat above had drawn them into the tunnel, their snapping jaws silhouetted against the orange glare of the fire that was pouring down the chute.
 
Otis looked at Virgil, then at the burning ruins of the elevator above. The tower was a giant chimney, sending a column of black smoke and golden sparks high into the dust-choked sky. The concrete was glowing dull red in the dark.
 
He took his son's hand. He did not have a weapon, and there was no water left. They stood in the dark loading bay, the slow, relentless line of the dead closing in from all sides, and watched the fire consume the last harvest.
 
Story Nineteen: The Crop Duster
 
Chapter One: The Propeller
 
The wooden propeller of the Curtiss Jenny biplane was scarred with sand-pocking, its varnished birch laminate splitting at the tips where the dry winds had gnawed at the wood. The Texas Panhandle had no mercy for machines; the wind carried a constant grit that acted like sandpaper, stripping the varnish down to the raw, pale grain and leaving the blades looking like bleached bones recovered from some forgotten desert shipwreck. Mac stood on a weathered wooden milk crate, his grease-stained fingers wrapping around the thick, heavy edge of the upper blade. He had flown in the Lafayette Escadrille in the autumn of 1917, chasing Fokkers through the gray, damp skies over the Argonne. His left shoulder still held the stiff, aching reminder of those days—a jagged chunk of German shrapnel lodged close to the scapula, a cold metal knot that throbbed with a dull, sickening ache whenever the pressure changed. But there was no moisture here to predict; there was only the bone-dry heat of the southern plains and the electric tension of a gathering storm.
 
"Clear!" Mac called out, his voice hoarse, scraped raw by the dust that hung like a permanent red haze over the runway.
 
Billy, his mechanic, sat in the front cockpit, his shoulders hunched. The boy was barely nineteen, but he looked forty, his face gaunt from weeks of living on boiled cabbage, salt pork, and whatever cornmeal they could scavenge from abandoned pantry shelves. His cheeks were hollow, his eyes sunken back into his skull, and a pair of scratched leather goggles sat high on his forehead, holding back greasy, dust-caked hair. He reached for the magneto switch on the crude wooden instrument board, his fingers trembling slightly.
 
"Contact!" Billy yelled back, his voice cracked and thin, instantly swallowed by the wind.
 
Mac took a deep breath, braced his boots against the parched grass, and pulled the propeller down with all the weight of his small, wire-hard body. His boots slipped on the dry sod, kicking up a small swirl of red silt that stung his eyes. The engine, a temperamental eight-cylinder Curtiss OX-5, resisted at first, its pistons cold and gummed with heavy castor oil. Mac swung again, throwing his hips into the turn, his shoulder protesting with a sharp, hot needle of pain. This time, the engine coughed. It spat a wet, black mixture of unburnt gasoline and oil from the exhaust stacks, spraying the dry grass beneath, and then caught with a deafening, metallic roar. The biplane shuddered, its linen-covered wings vibrating so hard the wooden struts blurred. The wire rigging sang, a high-pitched, metallic hum that sounded like a chorus of small, dying animals.
 
"She’s running rough, Mac!" Billy shouted over the din, looking back over his shoulder. The exhaust plume was thick and blue, smelling of scorched oil and low-grade fuel.
 
"She’s running!" Mac shouted back, kicking the milk crate away. "That’s all that matters! Keep her head into the wind!"
 
Chapter Two: The Flight
 
The air at two thousand feet was cold, but it carried the same metallic taste of red topsoil that had choked the life from the fields below. The sky was not blue; it was a bruised, coppery orange, the sun a pale, bloodless disc that struggled to pierce the dust suspended in the upper atmosphere. From the open rear cockpit, the Texas Panhandle stretched out to the horizon like a massive, dried steer hide, gray and wrinkled, scarred by the empty, sandy beds of creeks that had not seen a drop of water since the dry years began in '30. Mac kept his hands light on the wooden control stick, feeling the constant, unstable bumps of the thermal currents rising from the baked earth. The Jenny was old, a war-surplus trainer that had been patched with doped canvas so many times it looked like a quilt, but it flew.
 
Mac checked the fuel pressure gauge. The needle was hovering near the zero mark, vibrating against the glass. The engine was running on tractor fuel siphoned from a rusted John Deere that had been left in a ditch outside Spearman. The low-grade kerosene made the engine knock, a heavy, pre-ignition rattle that Mac could feel through the soles of his boots and the seat of his pants. It was a race against time, and both of them knew it.
 
Below them, the highway was a line of stalled cars, their roofs glittering in the dust-choked sunlight like the scales of a dead snake. Mac squinted through his goggles, his eyes watering from the wind. A miles-long column of the dead was moving south along the dirt road, their progress slow and uniform. They were not running; they had no reason to hurry. They simply walked, a gray, shifting carpet of bodies that filled the road from ditch to ditch. From this height, they looked like a slow stream of muddy water flowing across the dry earth, bypassing the abandoned model-Ts and the broken fences, drawn by some silent, collective instinct that Mac could not begin to understand. There was no end to the line; it stretched all the way to the northern horizon, where the sky was turning the color of charcoal.
 
Chapter Three: The Empty Fields
 
They were looking for the Miller farm, a small homestead situated in a shallow valley three miles south of the river. A group of thirty refugees, mostly women and children from the outskirts of Perryton, had barricaded themselves in the heavy timber barn three days ago. Mac had promised them he would fly south, scout the road to see if the bridge over the Canadian River was clear of the dead, or if it had been blocked like the others. He had no radio, no way to transmit what he saw. His only method of communication was a lead-weighted leather bag containing a hand-written note, which he was supposed to drop into the farmyard.
 
He spotted the farm on the edge of a dry valley. The homestead was already gone, a black rectangle of charcoal in the middle of a dry wheat field that had never been harvested. The farmhouse had burned down to its stone chimney, which stood like a tombstone in the ash. The barn was still standing, its tin roof reflecting the dull sun in brief, blinding flashes.
 
But the yard was not empty. Hundreds of the dead had surrounded the barn, their grey, dust-covered bodies pressed against the wooden doors, their arms scratching at the dry timber. They looked like grey lichen encrusting the bottom of the building. Mac flew low, the engine roar echoing off the tin roof with a deafening rattle. As the shadow of the biplane swept over the barn, Mac saw a face at the small hayloft window—a girl’s face, white and terrified, her fingers gripping the wooden frame. She looked up at the plane for a brief second before she vanished back into the shadows of the loft.
 
Mac leaned out of the cockpit, the wind tearing at his collar, and threw the leather bag over the side. He watched it fall through the dusty air, landing with a small puff of red dirt in the middle of the yard, just ten feet from the reaching arms of the dead.
 
Chapter Four: The Engine Sputter
 
The engine did not fail all at once. It began with a sharp, metallic clatter from the third cylinder, followed by a sudden drop in engine speed. The biplane shuddered, the nose dropping as the thrust fell away. Then came a thick cloud of blue, acrid smoke that blew back into the cockpit, stinging Mac’s eyes and filling his lungs with the taste of burning copper and lead.
 
"Loss of pressure!" Billy yelled from the front seat, pointing frantically to the oil gauge on the small wooden dash. The needle had dropped to the stop, and the temperature was climbing rapidly.
 
The propeller slowed, the rhythmic throb of the engine turning into a wet, scraping rattle, like a bucket of bolts being shaken. Mac adjusted the fuel mixture, trying to clear the cylinder, but the damage was done. The engine coughed once more, a loud bang echoing from the carburetor that sounded like a rifle shot in the quiet sky, and then the propeller stopped completely. The heavy wooden blades froze in a vertical line directly in Mac's field of vision.
 
The silence was sudden and absolute. The constant roar that had filled Mac's ears for the last hour vanished, replaced by the whistling of the wind through the wire bracing of the wings and the creaking of the wooden fuselage. The Jenny was no longer a machine; it was a heavy glider, its nose pulling down toward the dry fields below.
 
"Where are we going down, Mac?" Billy screamed, his voice filled with a high, panicked edge.
 
Mac did not answer. He scanned the terrain below. The wheat fields were filled with deep, wind-carved sand drifts, some of them five feet high. If the landing gear hit a drift, the plane would nose-over instantly, flipping the heavy engine block onto the open cockpits and crushing them both.
 
Chapter Five: The Landing
 
The only flat surface within miles was the narrow dirt road that ran along the western boundary of the Miller farm. It was barely wider than the plane's wingspan, bordered on both sides by deep ditches and strands of rusted barbed wire.
 
Mac aligned the nose of the biplane with the narrow strip of dirt, fighting the heavy controls. Without the engine, the plane was sluggish, the control surfaces losing their effectiveness as the airspeed bled away. The wind was blowing from the side, a steady crosswind that drifted the plane toward the eastern ditch. Mac's left shoulder screamed in pain as he kicked the rudder bar, trying to slip the plane into the wind to maintain his alignment.
 
"Hold on, Billy!" Mac yelled, his teeth clenched.
 
The tires touched the dirt road with a loud, hollow thud that shook the entire frame. The plane bounced, its wooden tail skid scraping along the gravel, kicking up a plume of dust. For a split second, Mac thought they had made it. The road was clear of the dead for this short stretch, and the plane was slowing.
 
Then, the left wingtip dipped. A sudden gust of wind caught the right wing, lifting it, and the left wingtip caught the top wire of the fence. The steel barbed wire tore through the doped linen of the wing with a scream of ripping fabric. The wooden ribs splintered, snapping like dry twigs under the tension. The plane spun violently to the left, its nose diving into the dry, sandy ditch at the side of the road.
 
Chapter Six: The Ditch
 
The impact was not fast, but it was brutal. The wooden fuselage buckled under the sudden deceleration, the wire stays snapping and whipping through the air like steel cables. The heavy engine bulkhead collapsed backward, the iron block of the Curtiss engine pushed directly into the front cockpit.
 
Mac was thrown forward, his forehead striking the padded leather rim of his cockpit. The impact left him dazed, a high-pitched ringing in his ears. He lay still for a minute, his cheek pressed against the wood, the taste of copper in his mouth. His vision was blurred, the world swimming in a haze of red dust and smoke. He could hear the sound of gasoline dripping from the ruptured tank onto the hot exhaust pipes, a steady, terrifying tick-tick-tick that sounded like a countdown.
 
"Billy," he gasped, his voice barely a whisper. He tried to clear his eyes, but the blood from a cut on his hairline was running down his face, warm and sticky.
 
There was no answer from the front cockpit.
 
Mac pulled himself up, his ribs aching with a sharp, stabbing pain that made it hard to draw a breath. The front cockpit was completely crushed, the heavy engine block pushed back into Billy’s chest, pinning him against the seat. The young mechanic’s eyes were open, staring blankly at the coppery sky, but they were already glassy, his mouth filled with dark, frothy blood. He was gone, his chest crushed by the heavy cast-iron engine.
 
Chapter Seven: The Wreckage
 
Mac tried to climb out of the cockpit, but his left leg was trapped. The collapsed control column had bent under the force of the crash, the steel tube pinning his boot against the wooden floorboards. He pulled, his foot slipping inside the leather stirrup, but the metal was bent tight over his ankle, locking it in place. He was stuck in the splintered wooden cage of the fuselage, three feet off the ground.
 
"Damn it," Mac muttered, tugging at the metal with his bare hands. The steel was hot from the engine heat, burning his palms, but it didn't budge.
 
The wind had dropped, and the silence of the ditch was total, broken only by the tick of the cooling metal and the drip of gasoline. But the silence didn't last.
 
Mac heard the feet.
 
The engine roar and the sound of the crash had been heard for miles. From the dry wheat fields on both sides of the road, figures began to emerge. They shuffled through the dry stalks, their boots making a dry, rustling sound like locusts. They came from the direction of the Miller farm, and from the highway to the north. The leading edge of the horde was only fifty yards away, their grey, dust-coated faces turned toward the wreck. They walked with a slow, swaying gait, their arms hanging limp at their sides, their eyes fixed on the smell of the spilled gasoline and the fresh blood of the mechanic.
 
Chapter Eight: The Fabric Wing
 
Mac took his pocketknife from his trousers, his hands shaking as he tried to cut the leather strap of his boot to free his foot. The blade was small, the steel dull from years of cutting rope and canvas. He hacked at the leather, but the heavy cowhide was thick and greased, resisting the dull edge.
 
Outside, the first of the dead reached the plane.
 
They did not try to climb the metal cowling. They reached the lower wings, which lay shattered across the road. Their fingernails tore through the patched linen, looking for a way through. The fabric made a loud, ripping sound as they pulled it away in long strips, their hands reaching through the wooden ribs.
 
A woman in a gingham apron, her throat torn open and black with dried blood, reached through the splintered spars of the left wing. Her dead fingers brushed against Mac’s trousers, her nails hooking into the fabric of his leg. Mac struck her hand with the heavy handle of the pocketknife, breaking her fingers, but she did not pull back. She simply leaned her weight against the wing, her snapping teeth inches from his boot, her eyes cloudy and lifeless. She did not feel the pain of her broken bones; she only felt the hunger. More of them pressed behind her, their weight crushing the remaining structure of the wing.
 
Chapter Nine: The Cockpit
 
By nightfall, the biplane was completely stripped of its fabric. The wings were skeletons of splintered spruce and pine, the fuselage a bare frame of ash longerons and steel wire. The dead surrounded the cockpit, a solid wall of snapping jaws and reaching arms.
 
Mac sat in the center of the frame, his trapped leg swollen and numb. He had used the heavy brass fire extinguisher from the cockpit wall to break the wrists of the creatures that climbed the sides, but there were too many. He was elevated, sitting in the pilot’s seat three feet above their heads, but the frame was creaking under their weight.
 
They were not trying to break the wood. They were simply leaning, their collective mass pushing the fuselage down into the dirt of the ditch. The wooden longerons were bowing, the steel tie-rods singing with tension like the strings of a piano. Mac looked down into the mass of grey faces. He could see their teeth, worn and broken, clicking together in a rhythmic, mechanical frenzy. The smell of their decay was thick, mixing with the smell of the gasoline and the dry dust.
 
He had no more strength in his arms. The brass fire extinguisher was heavy, slipping from his grease-covered fingers. He dropped it, and it fell into the crowd below, striking a zombie’s head with a dull clank before disappearing into the mass of legs.
 
Chapter Ten: The Wind
 
The red dust began to blow again, a light sand-storm that covered the dead in a layer of red grit.
 
Mac looked out at the horizon. The sun had set, and the sky was a deep, silent black. The stars were bright, cold, and distant, completely indifferent to the struggle in the ditch. He had survived the skies of France, the trenches of the Somme, and the poverty of the Depression. He had thought he would die in the air, falling from the clouds in a streak of fire, his name remembered as a flyer.
 
Instead, he was trapped in a wooden cage on a dirt road, three miles from nowhere.
 
The wooden frame of the cockpit gave way with a loud, final snap. The longeron shattered, the pilot's seat collapsing down into the middle of the horde. Mac did not scream. He felt the cold, rotting fingers wrap around his neck, his eyes remaining open to the dark sky as the wind blew the red dust over the wreckage of the Curtiss Jenny, burying his bones along with the dust of the Panhandle.
 
Story Twenty: The Oil Rig
 
Chapter One: The Derrick
 
The wooden scaffolding of the Oakhaven No. 4 oil rig rose eighty feet above the baked red clay of Creek County, Oklahoma. It was a skeletal tower of heavy, creosote-soaked pine timbers, held together by rusted iron bolts and guy-wires that thrummed like cello strings in the hot wind. The structure was a monument to the quick-money boom years of the late twenties, but now, in the dry summer of 1934, it looked more like a scaffold erected for some giant's execution. The drilling line, a thick steel cable slick with heavy black grease, creaked as it ran through the crown block at the very top of the derrick, vibrating under the strain of the heavy rotary drill pipe that stretched down into the dark depths of the earth.
 
Red stood on the derrick floor, his boots coated in a thick, slippery cake of mud and crude. His face was a mask of grease, coal soot, and sweat, his eyes stinging from the fumes of the steam engine that sat in the shed at the edge of the clearing. The engine was a roaring monster, its heavy iron flywheel spinning with a rhythmic, bone-jarring clatter that shook the wooden planks beneath his feet, rattling the teeth in his head.
 
"We’re down to seven hundred feet, Red!" Cobb yelled, leaning close to his ear to be heard over the din. Cobb was the derrickman, a lean man with skin like tanned leather and hands wrapped in greasy rags to protect them from the wire slivers of the steel cables. "The bit is hitting hard shale! She's jumping like a wild mule!"
 
"The mud pump is clacking!" Red shouted back, his hand on the vibration-heavy throttle lever. "We’ve got a blockage! We need to clear the suction screen in the pit, or the pressure will blow the hose right off the standpipe!"
 
Red stepped to the edge of the derrick floor and looked down. The mud pit below was a square excavation, six feet deep and filled with a thick, gray-brown soup of clay, water, and barite. The mixture was used to flush the drill cuttings out of the hole, but as Red watched, the surface of the heavy liquid began to ripple. Something was moving beneath the grey slime.
 
Chapter Two: The Boiler
 
The steam boiler sat thirty feet from the derrick, a horizontal iron cylinder jacketed in cracked red brick. Its firebox door was slightly ajar, glowing a fierce, cherry red that cast long, dancing shadows across the cleared pine clearing. The boiler was the heart of the operation, supplying the high-pressure steam that turned the engine, which in turn rotated the drill pipe and ran the pumps. Without steam, the rig was just a dead pile of wood and iron.
 
Shorty, the fireman, stood by the boiler, his iron coal shovel suspended in mid-air. He was not looking at the pressure gauge, which was already trembling near the safety limit. Instead, he was staring into the scrub oak woods that bordered the clearing.
 
"Red!" Shorty called out. His voice was faint, instantly shredded by the exhaust of the steam engine, but there was a sharp note of panic in it that made Red look up.
 
Red walked to the edge of the derrick floor, wiping his hands on a greasy rag. Through the blowing red dust, he saw them.
 
They were coming from the woods. They emerged slowly from the dry undergrowth, their movements stiff and heavy. They were not running, but their numbers were staggering. A slow, crawling line of grey, dust-covered figures, their clothes torn and bleached by the sun, their skin the color of wet clay. The relentless, thrumming vibration of the steam engine had been traveling through the earth for days, acting like a dinner bell, drawing them from the abandoned farms and dry-land towns for miles around.
 
"Shut her down, Shorty!" Red yelled, waving his arm. "Dump the fire! Kill the steam!"
 
Shorty dropped his shovel and reached for the main steam throttle, but the iron wheel was hot enough to blister bare skin. He grabbed a greasy rag, wrapped it around the wheel, and pulled with all his weight.
 
The wheel did not budge. The lime-heavy well water they had been using to feed the boiler had scaled the valve seat, freezing the iron threads solid.
 
Chapter Three: The Siphon
 
"The valve is stuck!" Shorty screamed, his face white with terror.
 
Red jumped down from the derrick floor, his boots sinking six inches into the wet clay. He scrambled toward the tool chest, his fingers searching through the grease-covered tools until they found a heavy three-foot pipe wrench. He ran toward the boiler, his lungs burning with the dry air and the smell of sulfur.
 
The first of the dead had already reached the edge of the clearing. It was a former oil scout, a man Red had known in Tulsa, his leather jacket torn to shreds, his throat and half his face eaten away by wild dogs or other teeth. He shuffled toward the mud pump, his boots leaving dark, wet tracks in the red clay. He didn't look at the men; his milky eyes were fixed on the vibrating machinery.
 
Red didn't look at him either. He set the heavy iron jaws of the wrench on the spokes of the throttle wheel and threw his weight against the handle, his teeth bared in a snarl.
 
*Crack.*
 
The cast-iron wheel snapped under the torque. The wrench slipped, the iron teeth shearing the rusted spokes and leaving only the central stub of the valve stem. The valve remained wide open. The steam engine continued to roar at full speed, its exhaust pipe sending a rhythmic puff of black smoke and white steam high into the coppery sky. The pressure gauge on the boiler was climbing past the red line, the needle vibrating against the pin.
 
"She’s going to blow!" Shorty yelled, backing away.
 
Chapter Four: The Mud Pit
 
"The dead are in the pit!" Cobb yelled from the derrick floor.
 
The mud pit was six feet deep, a thick, heavy pool of drilling mud. A group of five zombies, drawn by the clanking of the suction pump, had shuffled straight off the edge into the grey clay. They did not drown; they had no breath to lose. They simply struggled, their legs caught in the suction of the heavy mud, their arms reaching up through the grey slime as they tried to pull themselves toward the wooden platform of the rig.
 
The suction of the clay was too great, holding their legs like cement, but more of the dead were pushing behind them. The creatures behind did not stop; they walked directly onto the bodies of the trapped ones, using them as a bridge to cross the pit.
 
"We can't get to the truck!" Shorty said, his back pressed against the boiler brickwork. "They’ve blocked the road! They’re coming through the clearing!"
 
Red looked at the woods. The clearing was filled with them now, a grey wall of rotting flesh that was closing in from three sides. The road back to the highway was choked with a mass of figures, their arms reaching, their jaws snapping.
 
"Up!" Red ordered, pointing to the derrick ladder. "Get to the monkey board! It’s the only place left!"
 
Chapter Five: The Cable
 
The monkey board was a narrow wooden platform forty feet above the derrick floor, where the derrickman stood to guide the heavy sections of drill pipe. It was reached by a wooden ladder bolted to the pine legs of the derrick.
 
Red climbed first, his boots slick with grease and mud. The pine timbers of the derrick were vibrating violently, the engine running at dangerous, uncontrolled speeds as the drill bit bound and caught on the hard rock below. Every rung of the ladder shook, threatening to throw him off into the engine pit.
 
Cobb followed close behind him, but Shorty, his boots heavy with the red clay of the clearing, was slow.
 
A dead roustabout, his overalls soaked in black oil, reached the base of the ladder. He did not try to climb, but his hand caught Shorty’s boot, his fingers locking around the leather heel with the iron grip of rigor mortis.
 
"Red!" Shorty screamed, his fingers slipping on the greasy wooden rungs. He hung by his hands, forty feet above the floor, his legs dangling.
 
Cobb reached down, his heavy pipe wrench striking the zombie’s skull with a wet crunch. The bone shattered, but the dead fingers did not loosen their grip. The weight of the creature was pulling Shorty down, his hands slipping farther on the oil-slicked wood.
 
The ladder gave way. The bolts that held the lower section of the ladder to the derrick frame sheared under the weight. The wooden rails snapped, and Shorty fell backward, dropping into the mud pit with a dull splash, the zombie still holding his heel.
 
Chapter Six: The Gush
 
Shorty did not rise. The mud pit was instantly filled with a writhing mass of grey bodies, their jaws snapping beneath the surface of the clay as they pulled him down into the grey slime.
 
On the derrick floor, the steam engine gave a loud, metal-tearing shriek. The drill pipe, bound tight in the hole and forced by the racing engine, sheared at the collar.
 
Without the resistance of the drill bit, the engine raced, the flywheel spinning into a blur of iron. The pressure inside the well bore had been building for hours, held down only by the weight of the heavy drilling mud that was no longer being pumped down the hole.
 
With the drill pipe gone, the well blew.
 
A column of thick, black crude oil shot seventy feet into the air, striking the derrick crown and spraying over the wooden timbers. It was a black rain that coated Red and Cobb, making the derrick floor and the ladders slick as ice. The smell of methane and raw oil was suffocating, a heavy gas cloud settling over the rig.
 
The oil rained down in heavy, warm drops, coating the derrick in a slick, black skin that made every surface a hazard.
 
Chapter Seven: The Platform
 
Red and Cobb clung to the monkey board, their clothes soaked in the black, sweet-smelling oil.
 
Below them, the derrick floor was completely covered in oil, a black pool that was overflowing into the mud pit. The dead were shuffling through the oil, their bodies black and shining, their eyes white.
 
"The boiler!" Cobb screamed, wiping the oil from his face.
 
The boiler was still hot, its firebox glowing. The spray of oil from the derrick was drifting toward the open firebox door.
 
"It's going to catch!" Red said.
 
He looked at the derrick structure. There was no escape. The derrick was surrounded by hundreds of the dead, and the ladder below them was gone. They were stranded on a narrow pine board, forty feet in the air, with a lake of raw oil below.
 
The gas fumes were thick, making Red's head swim. He knew it was only a matter of time before a spark from the boiler or the engine ignited the oil.
 
Chapter Eight: The Iron Pipe
 
"We can slide down the guy wire," Cobb said, pointing to the steel cable that ran from the derrick crown to the anchor stake in the field.
 
"The cable is slick with oil," Red said. "You won't be able to hold it."
 
"It's better than burning!" Cobb yelled.
 
He reached for the cable, his greasy gloves slipping on the steel wire. He tried to wrap his legs around the cable, but he could not get a grip. He slid, his hands screaming as the steel strands tore through his rags and skin.
 
He fell halfway down, his body striking the wooden frame of the mud pump before rolling into the black pool of oil below.
 
The dead closed in instantly, their black-coated hands pulling him under the surface of the oil.
 
Chapter Nine: The Torch
 
The oil caught.
 
It did not start with an explosion, but with a soft, whooshing roar as the gas cloud reached the boiler firebox. A sheet of yellow flame swept across the ground, igniting the oil pool and the derrick floor in a fraction of a second.
 
The derrick was a wooden frame coated in fresh crude oil.
 
It became a giant torch. The flames climbed the pine timbers with terrifying speed, the wood crackling and spitting as the oil burned.
 
Red was trapped on the monkey board. The heat was a physical wall, blistering his skin through his oil-soaked shirt. He backed away, his boots smoking against the pine planks, until he was pressed against the iron guy-wire.
 
Below him, the dead did not retreat from the fire.
 
They walked straight into the flames, their clothes and flesh catching fire, turning them into walking torches. They continued to shuffle toward the base of the derrick, their jaws snapping through the smoke.
 
Chapter Ten: The Well
 
The heat was unbearable. Red's hair was singeing, his clothes beginning to smolder.
 
He looked down at the wellhead. The column of oil was still gushing, feeding the fire with a continuous supply of fuel. The derrick was collapsing, the heavy pine timbers burning through at the base.
 
The derrick began to lean.
 
It did not fall quickly. It bowed slowly to the east, the guy-wires singing as they snapped one by one.
 
Red did not scream. He stood on the burning platform, his eyes closed against the glare of the fire, and felt the wooden tower tip. The derrick crashed down into the lake of burning oil, a massive splash of fire and black smoke rising to meet the red dust of the Oklahoma sky, burying the living and the dead under the ash of the well.
 
Story Twenty-One: The Coal Mine
 
Chapter One: The Shaft
 
The iron headframe of the Black Ridge No. 3 coal mine rose fifty feet above the gray slag heaps of southern Illinois. It was a triangular tower of heavy steel beams, weathered to the color of dried blood, holding the massive sheave wheels that lowered the men down into the deep earth. In the heat of 1934, the coal mines were running on skeleton crews, the coal prices bottomed out, but the work didn't stop because a man had to eat, even if the food was nothing but cornmeal and salt pork.
 
Thomas sat in the iron cage, his knees touching the knees of the five other miners crammed into the narrow, damp space. His face was already black with coal dust from the previous shift, the sweat cutting clean tracks through the soot on his cheeks. Clamped to the front of his leather cap was a brass carbide lamp, its small, white flame flickering as the cage descended, casting dim, jumping shadows against the wet, limestone walls of the shaft.
 
Barnaby, the old draft mule, stood in the center of the cage, his head hanging low between the men. His nostrils flared, letting out a hot, rattling breath that smelled of wet hay and manure. The mule had spent ten years underground, pulling heavy coal cars through the low entries, and his eyes had long since gone milky and blind from the absolute dark. He didn't need eyes; he knew the feel of the tracks and the sound of Thomas's voice.
 
"The air is heavy today, Thomas," Walter said, a veteran miner whose voice was accompanied by a wet, hacking cough. He spat a dark glob of phlegm into the sump water at their feet. Walter's lungs were ruined, filled with the black dust of thirty years on the coal face.
 
"The fan is running slow," Thomas said, looking up into the dark tube of the shaft. "The steam boiler on the surface must be low on coal, or the fireman is sleeping. There’s no breeze coming down the shaft at all."
 
The cage touched the bottom of the shaft with a heavy, wet clatter, settling onto the wooden sump timbers. They were four hundred feet below the Illinois cornfields, in a world of absolute, velvety blackness that was held back only by the tiny, flickering flames of their caps.
 
Chapter Two: The Cage
 
The miner’s day did not start with the pick; it started with the mule.
 
Thomas reached out, his grease-blackened fingers guiding the leather halter of Barnaby. He led the blind animal out of the narrow iron cage, his boots splashing in the muddy water that gathered at the bottom of the shaft. The mule walked slowly, his heavy iron shoes clicking rhythmically against the steel rails of the coal car tracks. The rails ran down the main entry, a long, timbered tunnel that stretched a mile into the coal seam.
 
They had been working the low coal for three hours, the pickaxes swinging in the cramped space, when the hoist line stopped.
 
It did not stop with the usual three bells from the hoist house on the surface—the signal for a shift change or a lunch break. It stopped with a violent, screeching jerk that resonated through the entire mine. The heavy steel cables whipped against the sides of the shaft, the noise sounding like a cannon shot in the confined space.
 
Thomas dropped his pick and ran back toward the shaft station, his carbide lamp casting a wild, swinging light. When he reached the bottom, he found the hoist cage was gone. It was jammed halfway up the shaft, its steel frame twisted and bent at a sharp angle against the guide timbers, blocking the only way out of the mine.
 
"Hoist operator!" Walter yelled up the dark, dripping tube of the shaft, his hand cupped around his mouth. "Hey! Send down the auxiliary!"
 
There was no answer from the surface. There was only the sound of water dripping from the timber sets into the sump, and a low, wet scratching that seemed to come from the walls of the shaft.
 
Chapter Three: The Dark
 
The carbide lamps were running low, the water in the upper chambers dripping onto the calcium carbide stones in the bottom containers, producing the acetylene gas that fed the flames.
 
Thomas sat on a wooden prop, his fingers cold and slick as he opened his leather pouch. He tipped the lamp over, dumping the spent, white lime paste onto the ground, and took out a handful of the cold, grey carbide stones. He added a pinch of the stones to the dry chamber, poured a little water from his canteen into the top, and screwed the two halves back together. The sharp, sour smell of the acetylene gas filled the air, and he struck the flint wheel. The flame flared back to life, a bright, white point of light in the gloom.
 
"The fan has stopped," Walter said, his voice tight and dry. He was sitting with his back against a coal rib, his chest heaving as he tried to draw air.
 
The ventilation fan on the surface was the lungs of the mine, drawing fresh air down the shaft and pushing the bad gases out the return. Without it, the mine would choke. The "black damp"—air depleted of oxygen and heavy with carbon dioxide—would settle in the low headings, displacing the breathable air within hours.
 
"We have the ladder shaft," Thomas said, pointing toward the old, abandoned air shaft fifty yards away.
 
"The ladder is rotten, Thomas," Walter said, shaking his head. "It hasn't been inspected since the strike in twenty-eight. The timbers are soft as sponge, and the iron bolts are rusted through. It's a long climb to the top on rotten pine."
 
A heavy, metallic thud echoed down the shaft, vibrating through the iron rails.
 
It was not a rock fall. It was the sound of something heavy hitting the iron roof of the jammed cage, four hundred feet above them.
 
Chapter Four: The Mule
 
The draft mule, Barnaby, stopped.
 
He did not nudge Thomas’s shoulder as he usually did when he wanted a handful of oats or a pat on his scarred neck. He stood perfectly still in the middle of the track, his long ears twitching back toward the shaft station, his blind, white eyes staring into the dark.
 
A low, wet snarl echoed through the entry timbering.
 
It was a crawl.
 
A figure emerged from the sump pit at the bottom of the shaft, dragging itself out of the black water. It was the hoist operator from the surface. Thomas recognized the man's blue denim shirt, but his face was white and bloated from the long fall down the shaft. Both of his legs were broken, the white bone pins sticking through his torn trousers, but he did not stop. He dragged himself along the steel rails, his dirty fingernails scraping against the iron ties, his jaw clicking open and closed.
 
"Get back!" Walter yelled, raising his heavy coal shovel like a club.
 
But the hoist operator was not alone. Behind him, more of the dead were dropping through the gaps around the jammed cage, their bodies breaking on the iron frame before they began to crawl into the dark workings. They were the people from the surface town, their clothes covered in red dust and coal soot, their eyes lifeless and vacant.
 
Chapter Five: The Air Shaft
 
The miners retreated down the main entry, leading the blind mule by his halter. The animal was nervous, his nostrils flaring as he smelled the rot behind them, his hooves slipping on the wet clay between the ties.
 
The air was getting thick. The lack of ventilation was causing the black damp to roll out of the old, abandoned rooms, filling the entry with a heavy, suffocating gas. The flame of Thomas’s carbide lamp began to shrink, its bright white light turning into a small, dull red bead.
 
"Methane," Thomas whispered, his hand going to the lamp.
 
In the presence of explosive methane gas, the lamp flame would expand into a blue envelope, warning them that a single spark could blow the entire mine to pieces. But if the oxygen was simply gone, the flame would go out, leaving them in the absolute dark. The flame was dying, struggling for air just as they were.
 
"The air shaft at the third entry," Walter gasped, his head hanging low as he coughed, his body bent double. "The ladder... we have to try it, Thomas. There’s no other way."
 
They turned into the narrow crosscut, the ceiling so low that Thomas had to walk bent double, his leather cap scraping the shale roof, his shoulder rubbing against the rough coal ribs.
 
Chapter Six: The Lamp
 
The air shaft was a vertical raise, three feet square, lined with rotting hemlock timbers that had been blackened by years of coal dust. A rusted iron ladder was bolted to the wood, climbing four hundred feet to the surface.
 
Thomas looked up. The top of the shaft was a tiny, grey square of dust-choked light, so distant it looked like a star.
 
"We have to leave the mule," Walter said, his breath coming in ragged gasps.
 
Thomas looked at Barnaby. The old draft mule stood in the low coal, his blind eyes turned toward his handler, his sides heaving in the thin air. He had pulled the heavy coal cars for ten years, trusting the hand that fed him, never seeing the sun.
 
"He won't survive the dark alone," Thomas said, his hand resting on the mule's damp nose.
 
"He’s a mule, Thomas!" Walter screamed, his voice cracking from the carbon monoxide that was beginning to fill the entry. "He’s going to die anyway! Get on the ladder!"
 
A scraping sound came from the crosscut behind them.
 
The hoist operator had reached the entry, his broken legs dragging behind him like sacks of coal. Behind him, three more of the dead were crawling through the low space, their jaws snapping in the dark, their fingers clawing at the dirt.
 
Chapter Seven: The Cave-In
 
Walter climbed the ladder first, his boots shaking on the rusted iron rungs. The old iron groaned under his weight, the bolts creaking in the rotting wood.
 
Thomas followed him, his fingers wrapping around the cold iron bars. He did not go far.
 
At thirty feet, a hemlock timber set gave way with a loud, splitting roar. The rock above—a massive slab of grey shale that had been held in place by the timber—collapsed into the shaft, burying the lower ladder and the entrance of the crosscut in a heap of heavy debris.
 
Thomas was thrown against the side of the raise, the impact knocking his carbide lamp from his cap. He watched it drop into the dark below, its small white light spinning before it hit the rocks and went out.
 
The light went out.
 
The darkness was immediate, absolute, and heavy as a blanket. Thomas could not see his hands, his face, or the rungs of the ladder. He could only hear the sound of the shale settling, the dust choking his nose, and the low, muffled braying of the mule trapped beneath the rock fall.
 
Chapter Eight: The Crawl
 
"Walter!" Thomas yelled into the dark, his voice echoing off the wet limestone walls.
 
"I'm here," Walter's voice came from ten feet above, sounding thin and weak. "The ladder... it's loose, Thomas. The bolts are pulling out of the wood."
 
Thomas reached out, his fingers feeling the cold, rough shale of the shaft wall. The air was hot and thick, the smell of sulfur and coal dust filling his mouth and throat.
 
He climbed by touch, his fingers wrapping around the rusted iron bar of the next rung, pulling his weight up one step at a time. The iron was cold and wet with acid mine water, which stung the cuts on his fingers.
 
Behind him, beneath the pile of fallen rock, the braying of the mule stopped.
 
It was replaced by a different sound.
 
A rhythmic, dry scraping. The dead were digging through the loose shale, their fingers clawing at the stones to reach the ladder. They did not have tools, but they did not feel pain or fatigue. They simply pulled the rocks away, their broken nails clicking against the iron rungs as they began to climb.
 
Chapter Nine: The Tracks
 
"The ladder is gone!" Walter screamed from above.
 
A loud, metallic twang echoed through the shaft as the upper bolts of the ladder sheared under Walter’s weight.
 
Thomas felt the iron bar beneath his boots tilt. He lunged forward in the dark, his hands wrapping around a rotting timber sill just as the ladder fell, crashing down into the raise to join the debris at the bottom.
 
He was hanging by his fingers from the hemlock sill, his feet swinging in the empty air.
 
"Walter!" Thomas called out.
 
There was no answer. Only a wet, heavy thud from the bottom of the raise, followed by the sound of the crawl starting again. Walter was gone.
 
Thomas pulled himself up with the last of his strength, his muscles screaming, and rolled onto the small wooden platform that marked the old entry at the two-hundred-foot level. He was alone in the dark, the air so thin that his heart was pounding against his ribs like a hammer.
 
Chapter Ten: The Deep
 
He crawled along the abandoned workings of the second level, his hands feeling the cold iron rails of the old tracks. He had no light. He had no water.
 
He reached a dead end where the roof had collapsed years ago, sealing the entry with a wall of solid rock. He sat against the coal rib, his knees pulled up to his chest, his fingers touching the dry dust of the floor.
 
He could hear the dead coming.
 
They were not climbing the ladder. They were crawling through the old ventilation tubes, their bodies squeezing through the narrow iron pipes, their broken bones clicking against the sheet metal.
 
Thomas did not pray. He did not have matches to strike.
 
He simply lay back against the cold coal, his eyes wide in the dark, and listened to the dry, relentless scratching of the apocalypse finally reaching the deep.
 
Story Twenty-Two: The Telephone Exchange
 
Chapter One: The Switchboard
 
The massive oak cabinet of the Western Electric switchboard was six feet long, its dark, varnished face a complex grid of brass jacks, drop shutters, and red indicator lamps. Maeve sat on the high, cane-seated stool, her back straight despite the ache that had settled in her lower spine. A heavy black Bakelite headset was clamped over her ears, its leather strap catching in her dark hair, and her fingers moved with the practiced speed of a seamstress, lifting fabric-covered patch cords, plugging them in, and throwing the brass talk-keys.
 
The switchboard room was located on the second floor of the Cleburne Municipal Building, a drafty brick structure that smelled of floor wax, dry paper archives, and the sharp, hot-metal odor of the vacuum tubes in the line amplifiers. It was the nerve center of Cleburne, Texas, connecting three hundred subscribers across the town.
 
Colleen, her twelve-year-old sister, sat on the floor by the battery locker in the corner of the room. The girl was quiet, her fingers wrapping a piece of white wool yarn around a wooden spool, her face pale in the dim light of the window.
 
"Cleburne Exchange," Maeve said, her voice dropping into the flat, professional cadence she had used for four years. She plugged a cord into Jack Forty-Two. "Operator."
 
A woman’s voice came through the receiver, high and trembling, background static hissing like dry leaves. "Maeve? Is that you? Maeve, the sheriff... they’re at the courthouse. They’re shooting, Maeve. My Arthur... he went down to the store for flour and he hasn't come back. The street is full of people, but they aren't walking right. They aren't moving like they're well."
 
"The courthouse lines are busy, Mrs. Gable," Maeve said, her voice calm and steady, though her own heart gave a sudden, hard thud. "I will try to connect you to the doctor's office. Please hold."
 
Chapter Two: The Plug
 
Mrs. Gable’s line was not the only one.
 
By three in the afternoon, the red lamps on the switchboard were lighting up faster than Maeve could plug the cords. The drop shutters were clattering down in a continuous, metallic rain, indicating that subscribers were lifting their receivers all over town. The small town was panic-stricken, three hundred people trying to reach their neighbors, their relatives, or the police station at the exact same time.
 
*Click. Click. Click.*
 
Maeve’s hands flew across the board, her sleeves catching on the brass frames of the jacks.
 
"Operator."
 
"Maeve, there’s someone in my yard. He’s walking in the garden, stepping right through the squash plants. He won't answer when I call out from the porch. He’s... he’s got no shirt on, Maeve, and his skin is grey."
 
"Operator."
 
"Maeve, the train... the three-fifteen freight from Fort Worth. It didn't stop. It went straight through the crossing. The gate is broken, and there’s cars piled up on the tracks, but the train just kept going, throwing sparks. Maeve, you have to call the next station!"
 
"Operator."
 
"Maeve, connect me to the bank. Cole is inside. I need to tell him to lock the vault and get the gun. They’re walking up the steps."
 
Maeve moved the plugs, her hands slick with sweat, her head aching from the constant hum of the static on the lines. She did not have time to think about what the calls meant, to assemble the pieces of the puzzle that was Cleburne’s sudden, quiet collapse. She was just the operator, the thin copper wire that held the town together.
 
Chapter Three: The Whispers
 
At five, the nature of the calls began to change.
 
The panicked voices, the questions, the frantic demands for the sheriff—all of it began to fade, replaced by a different kind of noise on the line. It was no longer words. It was the sound of breathing.
 
Maeve lifted a patch cord, plugging it into a calling jack.
 
"Operator."
 
No voice answered. Only a low, wet rattling, a sound like liquid in a pipe, followed by the dry, rhythmic clicking of teeth against the Bakelite mouthpiece of a telephone.
 
"Hello? Cleburne Exchange. Is anyone there?" Maeve asked, her skin prickling.
 
The clicking continued, steady and mindless. She pulled the plug, the line going silent.
 
She plugged into another jack.
 
"Cleburne Exchange."
