"Maeve... they’re in the kitchen," Mrs. Gable’s voice came through, but it was a whisper now, thin and dry. "The door didn't hold. The wood... it was too dry from the heat. They just pushed until the hinges split. Maeve, tell my sister in Waco that we—"
 
The call ended with a sharp, gasping scream, a wet tearing sound, and then the heavy thud of the telephone receiver hitting the pine floorboards. The line remained open, the transmitter catching the sound of heavy, dragging footsteps crossing the floor, and the low, wet chewing sounds that followed. Maeve did not unplug the line. She sat on the stool, the receiver pressed to her ear, paralyzed by the transmission of death.
 
Chapter Four: The Screams
 
"Maeve," Colleen said, standing up from the floor and walking over to the board. She reached out, her small hand touching Maeve’s apron. "Why is the red light on for the doctor’s office? It’s been on a long time."
 
Maeve looked at the board, clearing the blood from Mrs. Gable’s line from her mind. Jack Seven, Dr. Howard’s private line, had been open for twenty minutes, the red lamp glowing steadily, a miniature beacon of distress.
 
Maeve plugged into the jack.
 
"Dr. Howard?" Maeve said, her voice shaking slightly now. "Cleburne Exchange."
 
A scream came through the wire—a high, piercing wail that was distorted by the carbon transmitter until it sounded like a steam whistle. It was not the doctor. It was the nurse, Edith, her voice cracked and frantic, her words running together.
 
"The windows!" Edith screamed, her voice cracking. "They’re through the quarantine screens, Maeve! Dr. Howard is down! He’s... oh God, he’s biting the beds! He’s biting the patients! Maeve, disconnect the line! Disconnect the—"
 
The call was cut by a loud, metallic screech as the heavy telephone base was overturned, the receiver clattering against the wall. The line did not go silent; it went dead with the dry, hollow hiss of an open circuit, the copper wire still carrying the faint vibration of a struggle in the doctor’s office.
 
Chapter Five: The Severed Line
 
One by one, the red lights on the switchboard began to go out.
 
It was not because the callers had hung up their receivers. Maeve knew the board too well. The lines were going open, the copper wires that spanned Cleburne’s streets being broken. The dead, walking blindly through the town, were running into the wooden telephone poles, their weight dragging against the drop lines that ran from the poles to the houses, tearing the insulation and the copper wires from the siding.
 
"Maeve," Colleen whispered, her hand tightening on her sister’s sleeve. "The lights... they’re almost gone."
 
On the massive board, only three lines remained active: the sheriff's office, the railway station, and the private line to the Methodist church.
 
Maeve plugged into the church line, her fingers cold.
 
"Is anyone there?" she asked.
 
A voice answered—a young man’s voice, hoarse and whispered. "We are in the loft, Maeve. The doors are gone. They broke them. The stairs... we are pulling the steps up into the loft, but there are fifty of them below us in the sanctuary. They aren't trying to climb yet. They’re just... they’re just standing there, Maeve. Looking up in the dark. Tell someone we're here."
 
Chapter Six: The Lock
 
"We have to go, Maeve," Colleen said, her fingers catching the corner of Maeve’s woolen shawl. Her eyes were wide, reflecting the few remaining amber filaments of the switchboard. "They’re coming. I heard them on the street."
 
"The exchange must remain open, Colleen," Maeve said, her voice flat, repeating the rule from the Western Union operator's manual she had memorized. "In times of public emergency, the operator must maintain the circuit. We are the only connection they have."
 
"There is no circuit, Maeve!" Colleen cried, tears wetting her dusty cheeks. "There’s no one left to talk to!"
 
Outside the window, the street was silent. The red dust storm had settled, leaving a thick, velvety layer of red silt over the roofs, the telephone poles, and the abandoned cars.
 
A scraping sound came from the stairwell.
 
The Cleburne Municipal Building was old, its first floor containing the tax office and the town archives, empty rooms that had been abandoned hours ago. The stairs to the second-floor switchboard room were wooden, wide, and open.
 
Maeve rose from her stool, the Bakelite headset cord pulling tight and then popping free from the jack. She ran to the door, slid the heavy iron bolt into the frame, and braced her shoulder against the wood, listening to the creaking of the pine panels.
 
Chapter Seven: The Battery Room
 
The switchboard ran on electricity, supplied by sixty large lead-acid batteries stored in the small, windowless room behind the oak cabinet. The batteries were the heart of the exchange; without them, the lines would have no current, the transmitters no power to carry a voice.
 
Maeve walked into the battery room, carrying a flashlight. The air was sour and heavy with sulfuric acid fumes, the glass jars sitting in long, dusty rows on the wooden shelves.
 
One of the large jars on the bottom shelf was cracked, probably from the vibration of the train crash earlier. The clear acid was dripping slowly onto the floorboards, making the wood hiss and bubble in the dark.
 
"The batteries are draining," Maeve said, checking the hydrometer. "They won't last the night."
 
"Why?" Colleen asked, looking over her shoulder.
 
"Because the phones are off the hook," Maeve said, her voice flat. "Every phone in town that was dropped... it is still connected to the exchange. The current is flowing into empty rooms, into dead hands, into the dirt. The batteries cannot hold the load when every circuit is closed. It’s draining them dry."
 
Chapter Eight: The Stairwell
 
The thumping on the wooden door began at nine.
 
It was not a knock. It was the weight of a body leaning against the pine panel, the wood creaking as the hinges groaned.
 
"Operator!" a voice called from the stairs.
 
It was the voice of the mayor, Mr. Higgins, but it was wrong. The vowels were elongated, the words slurred and wet, as if his tongue were too large for his mouth.
 
"Maeve... connect... the line... to the... capital," he said, his heavy leather boots scraping against the wooden landing outside. "The governor... must... know... the situation."
 
Maeve did not answer. She stood by the battery locker, her hand wrapping around the cold iron handle of the heavy terminal wrench she used to tighten the battery bolts.
 
Through the gap beneath the door, she saw a dark, greasy liquid beginning to drip onto the threshold. It was Mr. Higgins’s blood, thick, cold, and dead, carrying the rot into the wood.
 
"Operator," the wet voice called again, followed by the dull, rhythmic thud of a forehead striking the pine panel.
 
Chapter Nine: The Cord
 
The switchboard gave a low, dying hum, the tone dropping in pitch as the voltage fell. On the grid, the few red lights that remained began to fade, their amber filaments shrinking to small, red pins of light.
 
Maeve sat back on the high stool, her headset still clamped over her ears. She took the patch cord for Jack Seven—the doctor's line—and plugged it into Jack One, the sheriff's line.
 
"Cleburne Exchange," she whispered into the Bakelite mouthpiece.
 
A voice answered—the sheriff’s deputy, his voice barely audible over the static of the dying batteries. "Maeve? Is anyone there? The church... it’s gone, Maeve. The roof fell in under the weight. We are out of shells. The office is... we’re going to the basement. Don't leave."
 
"Hold the line, Deputy," Maeve said, her voice cracking. "I am connecting you to the doctor."
 
She plugged the cord in. She did not tell him the doctor was dead. She did not tell him the clinic had been overrun hours ago. She just wanted the lines to be together, two voices in the dark, before the batteries failed completely.
 
Chapter Ten: The Line Busy
 
The switchboard lights went out at midnight.
 
The indicators were dark, the lines dead. The receiver of Maeve's headset was silent, the static vanishing as the last of the battery current drained into the red dirt of the street below.
 
The wooden door panel split.
 
A hand—white, swollen, its fingers covered in dry red clay—reached through the gap in the wood, its nails hooking into the iron bolt and rattling it against the frame.
 
Maeve did not run. There was nowhere to run; the windows looked out onto a street that was no longer Cleburne. She took Colleen’s hand, her fingers wrapping tight around the girl’s small, warm palm.
 
"What do we do now, Maeve?" Colleen whispered in the dark, her voice trembling.
 
"We wait," Maeve said.
 
She sat on the high switchboard stool, her headset still clamped over her ears, and listened to the wooden door splintering, the telephone lines outside swinging in the wind, completely silent and busy forever.
 
Story Twenty-Three: The Relief Camp
 
Chapter One: The Red Cross
 
The canvas of the main medical tent snapped like a pistol shot in the dry wind. It was a massive, olive-drab surplus tent from the Great War, held up by thick pine poles and anchored to the hard Kansas clay by frayed hemp ropes that groaned under the constant strain. Outside, the sky was a bruised, yellowish-grey, the color of a fresh welt on a beaten horse. The wind had been blowing from the south for three days, carrying the topsoil of three states with it, depositing a fine, red grit over everything. It sifted through the tightest seams of the canvas, settling in a thin, velvet layer on the surgical instruments, the medicine bottles, and the faces of the dying.
 
Dr. Vance wiped his spectacles with a piece of gauze. The gauze itself was grey with dust, and it left a smear across the round lenses. He sighed, adjusting the wire frames behind his ears, and looked down at the patient on the cot. It was a young boy, no older than ten, his face pale and sunken beneath a crust of dried mud that had formed from his tears and the blowing dirt. His chest rose and fell in shallow, rattling gasps.
 
"Dust pneumonia," Martha said, not looking up from her tray. She was boiling steel needles over a small kerosene stove, the blue flame flickering whenever the wind found a gap in the tent flaps. The smell of burning oil and carbolic acid was heavy, but it could not entirely mask the metallic scent of blood and the sweet, rotten odor that had begun to drift in from the camp’s perimeter. "His lungs are as full of sand as a dry creek bed."
 
"He won't last the night without oxygen," Vance said, his voice flat with exhaustion. "And the tanks we ordered from Wichita are three weeks overdue."
 
"Nothing is coming from Wichita, Doctor," Martha said, her voice flat, dry, and level. She had been in the camp for six months, long before the dead began to walk, when there was only the drought and the debt. She had seen forty children die of the black spit in the winter, and she had stopped praying sometime in November. "The trains aren't running. You know that as well as I do. The rails are buried under sand drifts in the cuts, and the crews aren't working the lines anymore. Nobody is coming to help us."
 
Vance did not answer. He pressed his stethoscope to the boy's chest, listening to the wet, bubbling sound of fluid-filled lungs. It was a sound he heard forty times a day now, but lately, there were other sounds coming from the cots—sounds that did not belong to the living.
 
Chapter Two: The Grit
 
The relief camp sat in a shallow depression outside the town of Meade, a collection of two hundred canvas tents, wood-frame shacks, and rusted automobiles. It was run by the Red Cross and the state relief administration, a temporary refuge for families whose farms had been blown away. But the refuge had become a corral. The perimeter was marked by a single strand of rusted barbed wire, held up by crooked fence posts that leaned in the sand.
 
Martha moved down the row of cots, her white apron stained with rust-colored grease from the cot frames and the yellow discharge of infected eyes. Every patient she touched was coated in dust. It was in their hair, their ears, their teeth. It settled in the folds of their skin like ash.
 
"We need more clean water for the wash basin," she said, standing empty-handed at the washstand.
 
"The pump in the draw is bringing up nothing but red mud," Vance said. He was scrubbing his hands with a stiff brush, the water in the tin basin already the color of brick dust. "We’ll have to boil the ditch water."
 
"The ditch is full of them," Martha said.
 
She did not need to explain who "them" were. They all knew. They had seen them three days ago, coming out of the sand hills—slow, grey figures that walked with a heavy, dragging gait, their clothes tattered by the barbed wire they had pushed through. They did not shout, they did not run, but they did not stop. The camp guards, armed with hunting rifles and old Krag-Jorgensen carbines, had shot several at the wire, but more had appeared in the coulees, their eyes milky and dry in the glare of the sun.
 
No one knew what had set them moving. The wireless operator in town had claimed it was a gas from the oil wells in Oklahoma, while the preacher at the tabernacle said it was the hand of God smiting a greedy nation. Vance did not care about the cause. He only knew that when they bit a man, the fever rose within six hours, the blood turned to black gel, and the heart stopped. And then, the patient got up.
 
Chapter Three: The Cough
 
The boy on the end cot began to cough. It was a dry, hacking bark that shook his small frame, followed by a wet, choking sound that made Vance's stomach turn. Vance ran to him, turning the boy on his side as a dark, tarry fluid spilled from his lips onto the dirt floor.
 
"Is it the dust?" Martha asked, holding the boy’s head.
 
"No," Vance said, his fingers on the boy's neck. The pulse was gone. The boy’s skin was already turning cold, the skin beneath his fingernails a dull, slate blue. But the eyes—the eyes were still open, staring at the canvas ceiling. "He's gone, Martha."
 
"Get the straps," Vance whispered.
 
Martha did not move. "Doctor, he's just a child. We can't..."
 
"Get the straps, Martha!" Vance's voice cracked, the strain of the last three days finally showing. "We don't have the luxury of pity. Not today. You know what happens if we wait."
 
Before she could reach the wooden box where they kept the leather restraints, the boy's fingers twitched. The small, dirty hand curled into a claw, his nails scratching against the canvas of the cot. A low, whistling breath came from his throat, though his lungs were full of dust and blood. His head turned slowly, the milky pupils of his eyes focusing on Vance's hand.
 
Vance stepped back, his heart thudding against his ribs. The boy’s mouth opened, his small teeth clicking together with a dry, mechanical rhythm. He was trying to rise, his joints cracking as he pushed his weight against the rotting canvas of the cot.
 
Martha stepped forward and threw a heavy wool blanket over the boy’s head, pinning his arms to his sides. Vance leaped onto the cot, his knees digging into the boy's chest as he wrapped a length of hemp rope around the blanket, tying the bundle to the iron frame of the cot. The bundle beneath the blanket continued to thrash, a muffled, wet growling coming from the wool.
 
Chapter Four: The Tents
 
By late afternoon, the wind had grown stronger, carrying with it a dark wall of sand that rose five hundred feet into the air. It was a black blizzard, the kind that turned noon into midnight.
 
Vance stood at the tent entry, holding the canvas flap open an inch. The camp was disappearing in the gloom. The kerosene lanterns hung from the tent poles were nothing but dim, orange smudges in the swirling dust. The wind howled through the guy-lines, a high, screaming note that drowned out the sounds of the camp.
 
"The guards won't be able to see the wire," Martha said, standing behind him. "If they come now, we'll have no warning."
 
"They have the lanterns," Vance said, though he knew it was a lie. In a storm like this, the dust was so thick it choked the draft of the lanterns, soot-blackening the glass chimneys until the light was choked out.
 
A scream rose from the eastern section of the camp—a thin, distant sound that was instantly cut off by the roar of the wind.
 
"Was that the guards?" Martha asked.
 
"I don't know," Vance said, his face grim. "We have twenty patients in the secondary tent. We need to check their restraints. If the wind tears the canvas, they'll be exposed."
 
They walked out into the storm, their heads bowed, their faces covered with damp rags. The sand hit Vance's skin like needles, drawing blood from his cheeks. The air was hot and tasted of sulfur and iron. He could see nothing but the red ground beneath his boots and the outline of his own hand holding the guide rope that ran between the tents. The wind nearly knocked him off his feet twice, but he clung to the rope, his fingers raw from the rough hemp.
 
Chapter Five: The Bite
 
In the secondary tent, the lanterns had already gone out. The darkness was absolute, save for the occasional flash of lightning in the dust clouds above.
 
Vance struck a match, his fingers shaking, the small flame immediately blown out by the wind whistling through the canvas seams. He struck another, sheltering it with his palm, and touched it to the wick of a hand-lantern. The yellow light flickered, casting long, jumping shadows across the row of cots.
 
A man lay on the second cot, his leg wrapped in a blood-soaked bedsheet. It was Miller, one of the camp guards who had been bitten at the fence that morning.
 
"Vance," Miller whispered, his voice dry as paper. "The water... give me some water. It's so hot."
 
Vance held a tin cup to Miller's lips, but the man could not swallow. The water ran down his chin, leaving clean streaks in the red dust on his throat.
 
"The storm," Miller gasped, his chest heaving. "They're... they're at the ditch, Vance. There's too many of them. The wind... we couldn't see them until they were on the wire. I shot three, but they just kept coming. They didn't even slow down when the bullets hit them."
 
"Lie still, Miller," Vance said, checking the bandage. The blood had turned black, soaking through four layers of heavy cotton. The skin around the wound was swollen and green, the veins standing out like charcoal lines under the skin.
 
Suddenly, Miller's hand shot out, his fingers locking around Vance's wrist with a strength that made the bones click. His eyes rolled back into his head, showing only the yellowed whites.
 
"They don't feel the sand," Miller whispered, his teeth chattering. "The dust... it gets in their eyes, but they don't blink. They just keep walking, Vance. In the dark. They don't need the light."
 
His head fell back, his grip on Vance's wrist loosening. He was dead.
 
Chapter Six: The Storm
 
"Get the rope," Vance said, his voice trembling as he rubbed his bruised wrist. "We have to tie him down before the change."
 
Martha was already at the foot of the cot, holding the hemp line. But before they could secure the body, the tent door flap tore away from its grommets. A blast of wind and red sand roared into the tent, knocking over the lantern stand. The light went out, leaving them in the howling dark.
 
The canvas overhead began to rip, the sound like dry linen tearing. The wind lifted the side of the tent, pulling the wooden stakes from the loose sand.
 
"Martha!" Vance yelled, his voice lost in the din.
 
He reached out in the dark, his hand touching the cold skin of Miller's face on the cot. But the face was moving. The jaw was snapped open, his teeth grazing Vance's palm. Vance pulled his hand back, his thumb striking the sharp edge of a cot bolt as he fell backward into the dirt.
 
A hand reached down and grabbed his collar, pulling him up. It was Martha.
 
"This way!" she shouted in his ear. "The main tent! The poles are still holding!"
 
They crawled through the gap in the torn canvas, the dust so thick they could not breathe without swallowing sand. Vance looked back once, but there was nothing to see but a wall of black grit and the shape of Miller rising from his cot, his silhouette moving slowly in the howling wind.
 
Chapter Seven: The Quarantine
 
They reached the main tent by crawling along the guide line, their hands raw from the friction of the hemp. The heat inside was stifling, the dust settling like a blanket over the cots.
 
Inside, the tent was a scene of quiet horror. The boy who had died in the morning was still tied to the cot, his body thrashing against the ropes, the iron frame of the cot scraping against the dirt floor with a steady, rhythmic sound. Three other patients, their fevers having run their course, were also moving, their limbs twisting in the dark as they tried to free themselves from their blankets.
 
"The door," Martha said, pointing to the rear entry.
 
Two figures stood in the opening, their outlines blurred by the blowing sand. They were not patients. They were guards, or what had been guards. One of them still held a Winchester rifle, but the barrel was pointing at the ground, the stock dragging in the dirt.
 
"Don't move," Vance said, holding a surgical scalpel in his hand. It was a tiny piece of steel, useless against the dead, but it was the only weapon he had.
 
The dead guards did not rush. They walked with a slow, heavy stomp, their boots lifting and falling in the dust. The one with the rifle stepped on a tin tray, the steel bowls and needles clattering across the floorboards.
 
Martha reached for the kerosene stove. She tipped it over, the hot oil spilling across the dry floorboards, igniting into a sheet of yellow flame that licked up the canvas wall.
 
"What are you doing?" Vance screamed, shielding his eyes from the sudden glare.
 
"The tent is gone anyway," Martha said, her face illuminated by the fire. "We have to clear the way. Get to the truck!"
 
Chapter Eight: The Blindness
 
The fire spread quickly, the wind feeding the flames through the holes in the canvas. The heat was intense, forcing Vance and Martha toward the front exit. The dry timber of the floorboards and the heavy canvas of the tent burned with a fierce, red hunger.
 
The dead guards did not turn from the heat. The fire caught their clothes, the wool coats smoldering and then bursting into yellow flame, but they kept walking, their charred hands reaching out through the smoke. Their skin bubbled and blackened, but they made no sound, their faces expressionless as they stepped through the fire.
 
Vance ran for the exit, but the smoke was thick, mixing with the dust to create a greasy, black soup that blinded him. He tripped over an iron cot, his knees hitting the dirt, the scalpel falling from his hand.
 
A figure loomed over him in the smoke—a burning shape, its face blackened and unrecognizable, its jaws snapping with a dry, clicking sound.
 
Martha stepped between them, holding a heavy iron prep-stand. She drove the iron legs of the stand into the burning zombie's chest, throwing her weight forward. The zombie fell backward into the flames, the iron stand pinning it to the burning floor.
 
"Out!" she screamed, grabbing Vance by the shoulder. "The roof is coming down!"
 
They tumbled out of the tent opening just as the main ridge pole snapped, the burning canvas collapsing in a shower of sparks and red dust.
 
Chapter Nine: The Lantern
 
They stood in the open camp, but there was no camp left.
 
The black blizzard had torn down most of the tents, leaving only a few wooden shacks standing in the gloom. The air was a swirling vortex of red sand, ashes, and the smell of burning canvas and hair. The wind was beginning to die down, the black wind turning into a steady, red drizzle of dust.
 
Vance looked at his hand. He had a deep scratch on his palm, the blood bright red against the dust. He did not know if it was from the cot bolt or from Miller's teeth. He did not want to think about it, but the thought was there, cold and heavy.
 
"We need to get to the truck," Martha said, her voice hoarse from the smoke. "Before the rest of them find us."
 
The Red Cross truck was a 1932 Ford pickup, parked behind the administrative shack. It had a heavy canvas tilt over the bed, filled with empty wooden crates and a few tins of gasoline.
 
They ran toward the shack, their boots sinking in the sand drifts. In the thinning gloom, Vance could see them. They were everywhere.
 
They were coming out of the collapsed tents, rising from the sand drifts like plants after a rain. Some were bare-headed, their hair matted with blood; others wore the overalls of farmers or the uniforms of the relief workers. They did not look at each other. They did not speak. They only walked, their faces turned toward the smell of the living.
 
Chapter Ten: The Dust
 
They reached the truck. The windows were covered in a thick layer of red silt, the windshield wipers useless against the dry mud.
 
Vance climbed into the cab, his fingers fumbling with the ignition key. He pressed the starter pedal, the engine turning over with a slow, dry groan.
 
*Chug. Chug. Whir.*
 
"Come on," Vance muttered, his palm sweating on the steering wheel, the scratch on his hand stinging. "Come on."
 
The engine caught, coughing up a cloud of black smoke before settling into a rough, rattling idle.
 
Martha climbed into the passenger side, her apron gone, her dress torn at the shoulder. She did not look back at the burning medical tent. She looked at the road ahead—a faint trail of yellow sand that was already being buried by the drifts.
 
"Where are we going, Doctor?" she asked.
 
Vance looked at his hand. The scratch on his palm was already beginning to throb, the skin around the cut turning a faint, yellow-grey. He could feel a cold chill starting in his bones, despite the heat of the engine and the dust.
 
"West," he said, shifting the lever into first gear. "Maybe the wind is cleaner there."
 
The truck moved forward, its tires spinning in the loose sand, its headlights cutting through the red haze. Behind them, the ruins of the relief camp burned in the dark, a tiny beacon of fire in a land that was being buried, inch by inch, under the dead and the dust.
 
Story Twenty-Four: The Bridge Builder
 
Chapter One: The Catwalk
 
The wooden catwalks of the Red River suspension bridge hung four hundred feet above the canyon floor, suspended between heaven and the dry red earth like a spider’s web spun by a blind insect. They were nothing but narrow pine planks, two feet wide, held together by wire ropes and suspended from the massive steel guide cables by thin, rusted wire stirrups. When the Oklahoma wind blew through the gorge—as it did almost constantly, carrying the hot, sulfurous breath of the panhandle—the catwalks swayed in long, sickening arcs, the raw wood creaking and groaning against the steel brackets. To walk them was to dance with gravity, with nothing but a single wire rope on either side to keep a man from the empty air.
 
Silas stood on the north anchorage platform, his heavy leather tool belt sagging on his hips, the brass rivets of his pouches cold against his thigh. He was the foreman of the cable spinning crew, his face lined by fifty years of sun, coal smoke, and New Deal dust. His hands were thick and calloused, the knuckles scarred from decades of high-iron work in Chicago and Pittsburgh. But those cities belonged to another world now, a world before the bank failures and the dust storms. Now he was here, in the barren borderland between Oklahoma and Texas, building a bridge that felt more like a monument to a dying nation than a link between two states.
 
Below him, the Red River was a thin, red ribbon of mud, winding through the red clay banks of the canyon. The drought had shrunk the river to a sluggish trickle, leaving vast flats of cracked mud that baked in the heat.
 
"The wind is rising, Silas," Jack said, leaning against the cold steel tower. Jack was twenty-four, a high-iron worker from Chicago who had come south when the building trades died. He was holding a heavy pneumatic riveting gun, its rubber air hose trailing back to the steam compressor on the canyon rim like a black snake. "The cables are starting to whistle. We ought to head down."
 
"We have to finish the guide wire before dark," Silas said, his voice loud over the hum of the wind. "The contractor is coming from Oklahoma City tomorrow. If we aren't spinning the main cables by Monday, the county will cancel the bond, and we'll all be back in the breadlines."
 
"The contractor isn't coming, Silas," Jack said, his voice quiet. He pointed toward the north approach road. "Nobody is coming."
 
Silas looked. The dirt highway that ran from the town of Altus was empty of trucks, but there was a line of people walking along the shoulders. They were not workers. They were families, carrying bundles on their backs, their faces turned toward the bridge. Behind them, a mile down the road, a dark, slow-moving mass was crawling through the dust, its edges spilling into the cotton fields on either side.
 
Chapter Two: The Rivet
 
The pneumatic compressor on the north bank gave a rhythmic, coughing thud. It was powered by a small steam boiler, coal-fired and manual, its pressure gauge needle vibrating near the red line. The smell of hot grease, coal smoke, and dry dirt hung in the air.
 
Jack adjusted his leather safety belt, clipping the iron safety hook to the guide cable. He stepped onto the catwalk, his boots slipping slightly on the red dust that covered the pine planks. His job was to rivet the steel cross-beams that held the roadbed structure, a loud, hot process that filled the canyon with the sound of metal hammering metal.
 
*Rat-tat-tat-tat.*
 
The rivet gun slammed against the red-hot steel pin, the sparks flying into the empty air below. The sound was deafening, echoing off the sandstone walls of the canyon like rifle fire. For a moment, it drowned out the wind and the fear, but only for a moment.
 
"Stop the gun, Jack!" Silas yelled from the tower platform.
 
Jack released the throttle, the compressor returning to a low, hissing idle. "What is it? Did a wire slip?"
 
"Listen," Silas said.
 
Through the wind, Silas could hear the sound from the north road. It was a low, collective murmur, like the hum of a distant swarm of bees, but filled with a raw, human panic. The refugees had reached the bridge approach. The guards at the toll shack had closed the iron gates, but the crowd was pressing against the wire fencing, their hands reaching through the mesh. They were screaming, though the wind tore the words from their mouths before they could reach the towers.
 
And behind them, the dark mass was getting closer. It was not a storm. It was a crowd of slow, grey figures, their clothes tattered, their arms outstretched. They walked with a heavy, dragging gait, moving forward with a terrible, unthinking purpose.
 
Chapter Three: The Canyon
 
The Red River suspension bridge was supposed to connect two counties, a monument to New Deal progress in a region that had been ruined by the drought. It was half-finished: the two steel towers rose two hundred feet above the rims, the massive anchorage blocks of solid concrete were poured, and the catwalks were strung, but the actual roadbed was nothing but a skeleton of steel girders.
 
Silas walked to the edge of the tower platform. Below, the canyon was a natural barrier. The sandstone walls were vertical, dry, and crumbly. Nothing could climb them, and the river, even in its shrunken state, was too wide to cross without a boat.
 
"We can pull the catwalks up," Jack said, joining him, his fingers tight on the steel rail. "If we disconnect the stirrups on the north side, the planks will hang down against the south tower. Nothing can cross the gorge."
 
"We have three hundred bags of cement on the north bank, Jack," Silas said, his eyes scanning the equipment. "And the steam shovel. We leave that, and the project is dead. The county won't pay for the loss."
 
"The project is already dead, Silas," Jack said, pointing to the gate. "Look at them."
 
A man had climbed the wire fence at the approach. He was one of the guards, his khaki uniform torn at the shoulder, his leather holster empty. He fell over the top of the fence, landing heavily on the concrete anchorage block. He did not get up immediately; he lay there, his fingers clawing at the concrete, his legs twisting in the dust.
 
A second later, two figures reached the fence behind him. They did not try to climb. They simply leaned their weight against the wire, their faces pressed into the mesh, their teeth clicking against the steel links. The fence groaned, the wooden posts shifting in the sandy soil.
 
Chapter Four: The Cable
 
The guard on the concrete block rose slowly. His name was Miller—a local boy Silas had known since he was in short pants. He was the son of a tenant farmer who had lost his land in '34, a quiet boy who had been glad to get the guard job for twelve dollars a week.
 
"Miller!" Silas called down from the tower.
 
Miller did not look up. He walked with a stiff, limping gait, his head hanging to one side as if his neck were broken. His collar was wet with dark, thick blood that had soaked into his shirt, turning the khaki fabric to a deep, greasy brown. He was heading for the wooden stairs that led to the catwalk anchor.
 
"He's hit," Jack said, his hand wrapping around the handle of his heavy riveting hammer. "He must have got caught in the panic at the gate."
 
"He’s infected," Silas said. He had seen the dead in town the night before. They had come through the alleyways, slow and quiet, biting the horses in the livery stable and the people in their beds. He had seen a deputy shoot one three times in the chest with a Colt revolver, but the man had kept coming, his jaws snapping until they took his head off with a shovel.
 
"Miller!" Silas yelled again. "Stay back! Don't come up the stairs!"
 
Miller reached the base of the catwalk. His hand, covered in dry clay and dried blood, caught the wire rope rail. He began to climb the narrow wooden stairs, his boots clattering on the treads with a dry, irregular beat.
 
Behind him at the fence, the wire gave way with a loud, splitting pop of tension. The crowd of refugees surged through the gap, running toward the bridge, their suitcases and bundles dropping in the dirt. And behind them, the dead followed, their slow, heavy steps carrying them into the enclosure.
 
Chapter Five: The Scaffolding
 
The catwalk was only two feet wide, with a single wire rope on either side for a handrail. There was no safety net below, only the four-hundred-foot drop to the rocks and the red mud of the riverbed.
 
Silas and Jack stood on the tower platform, watching the refugees crowd onto the north anchor platform. Some of them tried to push past Miller, but the infected guard turned, his jaws snapping, his hands catching a young woman by her wool shawl. She screamed, a high, piercing sound that was instantly cut off as they both fell together onto the first planks of the catwalk.
 
"They're coming onto the span!" Jack shouted, his face white.
 
"Get the rivet gun!" Silas said. He ran to the steam compressor hose, dragging the heavy rubber line toward the catwalk entry.
 
The catwalk was designed to hold the weight of six workers and their tools. It was not built for a crowd of panicking people. The pine planks groaned, the wire stirrups twisting under the load as twenty people crowded onto the narrow walkway, pushing each other to get away from the north bank.
 
"Slow down!" Silas yelled. "The planks won't hold! You'll break the stirrups!"
 
But no one listened. A man in a double-breasted suit pushed past a woman with a baby, his leather shoes slipping on the dusty wood. He lost his footing, his body tilting over the wire rail. He gave a short, sharp scream as he fell, his arms windmilling in the empty air before he disappeared into the red shadow of the canyon. The woman screamed, but the crowd behind her pressed forward, forcing her onto the swaying wood.
 
Chapter Six: The Crowd
 
The panic was infectious, spreading through the crowd faster than the dust.
 
The refugees pressed forward, their weight causing the catwalk to sway in a violent, three-foot arc. Miller, the infected guard, was crawling along the planks, his teeth locking onto the ankle of a man who was trying to climb over him.
 
"Jack!" Silas called out.
 
Jack was on the catwalk, his safety line clipped to the main guide wire. He held the heavy riveting gun like a club, the pneumatic hose trailing behind him. He stepped toward Miller, the wind catching his jacket, his boots steady on the swaying wood. He had no fear of the height, only of the thing that was crawling toward him.
 
He drove the steel nose of the rivet gun into Miller's temple. The blow was heavy, the skull cracking with a wet sound, but Miller did not stop. His hands remained locked around the man's leg, his jaws snapping even as the bone gave way.
 
Jack pressed the throttle of the gun. The pneumatic hammer slammed into Miller's head, the vibration shaking the entire catwalk section. The noise was deafening, a rapid-fire hammering that echoed off the canyon walls. The infected guard finally went limp, his fingers releasing the ankle, his body slipping between the pine planks into the abyss.
 
But the crowd behind him was already on the catwalk. They were not looking at Jack; they were looking at the dead who were coming onto the stairs behind them.
 
Chapter Seven: The Split
 
"Silas, the cables!" Jack yelled, his voice cracking.
 
The north anchorage was now completely overrun. The dead were pushing onto the catwalk, their slow, heavy steps driving the refugees further onto the narrow span. The pine planks were bowing under the weight, the wire stirrups creaking as they stretched.
 
"We have to cut the bridge," Silas said, his voice flat with resolve.
 
"How?" Jack asked, scrambling back to the tower. "We don't have the torches here! They're in the tool shack on the north bank!"
 
"The stirrup clamps," Silas said, pointing to the iron bolts that held the catwalk to the guide cables on the south tower. "If we loosen the clamps on the south tower, the catwalk will drop. It will swing down like a pendulum against the north face."
 
"We’ll drop the people too!" Jack said, his eyes wide. "There's women on there, Silas! Kids!"
 
"They're already dead, Jack," Silas said, looking at the crowd.
 
The middle of the span was a bottleneck. The infected had reached the refugees on the catwalk, the narrow space making escape impossible. A man was bitten on the shoulder, his blood spraying over the pine planks. A mother, holding her child, looked down at the river four hundred feet below, and then she jumped, her coat billowing like a parachute before she hit the rocks. There was no choice. The bridge had to be cut, or the dead would cross.
 
Chapter Eight: The Plunge
 
Silas ran to the south tower platform. He took a heavy iron spud wrench from his belt, his fingers greasy with tallow and sweat.
 
The catwalk was held to the tower by four massive steel U-bolts. Silas fit the wrench to the first nut, his muscles straining as he pulled against the rusted threads.
 
*Squeak. Squeak.*
 
The nut turned slowly, the red rust falling in a fine shower onto his boots.
 
"Jack, help me!" he called.
 
Jack was at the entry of the catwalk, trying to keep the first refugees from reaching the south tower. A man, his face grey, his eyes vacant, reached for Jack’s collar. Jack struck him with the riveting gun, the heavy steel tool breaking the man's collarbone, but the zombie did not flinch. It stepped forward, its jaws grazing Jack’s leather jacket.
 
Jack tripped, his safety line catching him as he fell off the catwalk, his body swinging in the wind fifty feet out from the tower.
 
"Silas!" Jack screamed, his rivet gun falling into the gorge.
 
Silas did not look up. He had three nuts left. He threw his weight against the wrench, the steel bar bending under his hands.
 
Chapter Nine: The Bolt
 
The second nut came free, dropping into the canyon with a tiny, metallic ring.
 
The catwalk tilted, the pine planks on the north side dropping six inches, the refugees sliding toward the center of the span. A chorus of screams rose from the gorge, drowned out by the roar of the wind.
 
Silas moved to the third bolt. His hands were bleeding, the skin torn by the rough iron of the wrench.
 
"Silas!" Jack called from below. He was climbing his safety line, his fingers wrapping around the braided steel rope, his boots kicking against the empty air.
 
Behind him, on the catwalk, the dead were crawling over the bodies of the fallen. They were heading for the south tower, their fingers catching the wire stirrups, their jaws snapping in the wind.
 
*Crack.*
 
The third bolt sheared under the tension. The catwalk dropped another foot, the wood splintering as the structural cables began to twist.
 
Silas reached the final bolt. The nut was jammed, the threads crossed by the weight of the catwalk pulling against it.
 
Chapter Ten: The Span
 
Silas looked at Jack, who had reached the edge of the tower platform, his face white with fear and exhaustion.
 
"Give me the wrench, Silas," Jack gasped, reaching out his hand.
 
"No," Silas said. "Get to the south bank, Jack. Take the compressor truck."
 
"Silas, no!"
 
Silas did not answer. He did not have the strength to turn the nut. He took the heavy riveting hammer from his belt and struck the U-bolt itself.
 
*Clang. Clang.*
 
The steel sparkled in the twilight. The bolt was thick, three inches of solid forged iron, but it was under immense tension.
 
On the third blow, the steel split with a sound like a cannon shot.
 
The catwalk dropped.
 
The entire four-hundred-foot span of pine planks and wire stirrups tore away from the south tower, swinging down in a massive, whistling arc toward the north wall of the canyon. The refugees and the dead were thrown from the wood, their bodies falling in a silent rain into the red mud of the river below.
 
The catwalk slammed against the north sandstone face, the pine planks shattering into matchwood, leaving only the two bare steel guide cables stretching across the empty air.
 
Silas sat on the edge of the south platform, his legs dangling over the four-hundred-foot drop. He looked at his hands, covered in grease, rust, and his own blood.
 
The wind was still blowing from the north, carrying the smell of the dead and the dust across the empty canyon, where the bridge would never be finished, and the two halves of the country were separated forever.
 
Story Twenty-Five: The Siphon
 
Chapter One: The Intake
 
The intake chamber of the Colorado River Pumping Station No. 4 was a cavernous concrete pit, smelling of river silt, green slime, and hot cylinder oil. A massive, steam-driven centrifugal pump, cast in Chicago in the optimistic spring of 1912, sat in the center of the brick floor, its twelve-foot flywheel turning with a heavy, rhythmic thud that shook the masonry and vibrated up through the soles of George’s boots. The station was a concrete monolith built into the red canyon wall, designed to lift the river’s muddy water and send it rushing down the three-mile concrete siphon to irrigate the vast, thirsty cotton fields of the Imperial Valley.
 
George stood on the iron gallery, his hand on the cold brass handrail. He was the chief engineer, a man with black oil ground permanently into the lines of his palms and a profound, ringing deafness from thirty years of boiler rooms and steam engines. His face was weathered to the texture of boot leather by the desert sun, and his eyes were permanently squinted against the glare. Below him, the muddy brown water of the Colorado surged through the concrete channels, sucked in by the massive, invisible vacuum of the pump and discharged into the high concrete canal that ran like an aqueduct across the dunes.
 
"The vacuum pressure is dropping, Father," Leo said. Leo was nineteen, his face pale and smeared with coal soot from the boiler fire. He was standing by the control panel, his fingers resting on the heavy iron valve wheel of the steam bypass line. He looked thin and hollowed out, the product of a year of skimpy rations and constant, grinding anxiety.
 
George tapped the glass of the vacuum gauge. The needle was vibrating at fourteen inches of mercury, down from its normal, steady twenty-two. "The intake grates are clogging again. The suction is choking."
 
"Is it the driftwood?" Leo asked, looking up at the high gallery. "The spring rise must be bringing down the cottonwoods from the canyons up north. We had a lot of brush come down yesterday."
 
"No," George said, looking out the narrow, steel-framed window at the river. The water was a thick, swirling soup of red mud and yellow foam. "It’s not wood. The wood doesn't have shoes, Leo. Look down there."
 
Through the brown, silt-laden water near the bank, George could see the slow, dark outlines of figures. They were not swimming; they were walking along the riverbed, their heads submerged, their hair floating like black weeds in the current. Some of them were caught against the outer trash racks, their limbs twitching as the powerful water pushed them against the iron bars.
 
Chapter Two: The Clog
 
The Colorado River was the lifeblood of the valley, but lately, it had become a sewer for the dead.
 
For two weeks, the bodies had been drifting down from the towns upstream—from Needles, from Kingman, from the mining camps in the hills. They fell into the canyon creeks, or walked into the shallows and were swept away by the swift, cold current. The water did not drown them. It bloated their flesh, turned their skin a greyish-white, and wore away their clothes until they were nothing but tattered rags, but they did not stop moving. Even under ten feet of muddy water, their lungs full of river silt and gravel, their jaws continued to snap, their fingers clawing at the gravel beds.
 
"We have to clear the rack, Leo," George said, walking down the iron steps to the wooden diving platform that projected over the intake well.
 
"The river is running high, Father," Leo said, his voice tight with fear. "The current is three knots in the channel. If the air line kinks or the current pins you against the rack, you won't have three minutes. Let's just shut the gates and wait."
 
"If the pump runs dry, the packing gland will burn out," George said, his voice quiet and stubborn. "And we don't have another set of flax packing in the storehouse. If the pump dies, the valley dies, Leo. The cotton will rot in the dirt within a week, and the twenty thousand people in the relief camps at El Centro will have no water to drink. We do our job."
 
He walked to the heavy oak locker where the diving gear was kept. It was an old Schrader manual diving dress: a heavy, stiff suit of rubberized canvas, a breastplate of solid copper with four viewing ports of thick glass, and two lead-weighted boots that weighed twenty pounds each.
 
| Component | Weight (lbs) | Material |
 
| --- | --- | --- |
 
| Canvas Suit | 15 | Rubberized canvas |
 
| Copper Breastplate | 25 | Forged copper & brass |
 
| Helmet | 30 | Copper with glass ports |
 
| Lead Boots | 40 (pair) | Cast lead and leather |
 
Chapter Three: The River
 
Leo helped his father into the heavy canvas suit. He laced the collar, his fingers pulling the leather gaskets tight against the copper studs of the breastplate, checking each brass wingnut with a heavy T-wrench.
 
George stepped into the lead boots, buckling the thick canvas straps around his ankles. He could feel the weight of the gear pressing down on his shoulders, making him feel old, clumsy, and fragile. The air hose was connected to the back of the copper helmet, a fifty-foot length of braided canvas and rubber that coiled on the timber floor.
 
"Keep the air pump turning, Leo," George said, his voice muffled inside the copper dome. "Three strokes a minute, steady. If the pressure drops below ten pounds, tap three times on the copper air pipe. I’ll hear it through the helmet."
 
Leo nodded, his face pale as he took the iron handle of the two-cylinder hand-cranked air pump. The pump sat in a pine box, its brass cylinders gleaming in the lantern light. It was manual work, requiring steady, tireless muscle to keep the air flowing down the line.
 
*Squeak. Squeak. Squeak.*
 
The leather plungers moved in the cylinders, the air hissing through the check valves.
 
George climbed down the iron ladder into the intake well, the brown water rising past his knees, his waist, and then closing over the glass windows of his helmet with a soft, bubbling hiss.
 
Chapter Four: The Valve
 
Underwater, the world was a green, silent gloom, a world of mud and pressure.
 
The current was strong, pulling at the canvas of his suit, trying to sweep him against the iron trash rack. George moved slowly, his lead boots sinking into the thick, red river silt at the bottom of the well. The air hissed into his helmet from the hose above, smelling of hot leather and pump oil, a dry, metallic taste that made him want to cough.
 
He reached the trash rack. It was a grid of vertical iron bars, six inches apart, designed to keep logs and branches out of the pump’s suction pipe.
 
A body was caught against the bars.
 
It was a woman, her floral dress torn into rags, her long black hair wrapped around the iron grate like river grass. She was waterlogged, her skin swollen and white, but she was not still. As George approached, her head turned toward him, her milky eyes staring through the glass port of his helmet. Her mouth opened in a silent, slow-motion scream, her teeth clicking against the iron bar.
 
George took a three-pronged iron rake from his belt. He drove the prongs into the woman's shoulder, using the leverage of the bars to pry her body loose from the rack. The current caught her, carrying her away into the dark water downstream, but as she went, her fingers clawed at his copper sleeve, leaving wet, greasy smears on the canvas.
 
Chapter Five: The Silt
 
There were more.
 
As George cleared the first rack, three more bodies drifted out of the green gloom. One was a young man in overalls, his legs broken and twisting in the current. Another was a child, his small limbs bloated to the size of logs. They were drawn by the powerful suction of the intake, their bodies slamming against the iron grid with a heavy, muffled thud that George could feel through the water.
 
The vacuum gauge in the pump room began to scream. It was a high, whistling note that echoed down the intake well.
 
"More air, Leo," George whispered, though his son could not hear him.
 
The air in the helmet was getting warm and oily. The manual pump was sixty feet away on the gallery, and Leo had been turning the crank for twenty minutes without a break. George could feel the pressure dropping, the rubber suit pressing against his legs as the water pressure squeezed the air out of the canvas.
 
He worked faster, his iron rake striking the bars, prying the bloated limbs from the grate. The silt was rising from the bottom, stirred up by his boots, turning the green water into a thick, brown wall. He was working by touch now, his fingers feeling the cold, slippery flesh of the dead caught in the iron bars.
 
Chapter Six: The Grate
 
Suddenly, George’s foot slipped.
 
He fell forward, his brass helmet striking the iron trash rack with a metallic clang that rang in his ears like a church bell. His rake fell from his hand, disappearing into the silt at the bottom of the well.
 
Before he could recover, a hand reached through the iron bars from the river side.
 
It was a strong hand, its fingers dark with rot, its nails worn down to the bone. It caught the brass air-inlet valve on the back of his helmet, twisting the valve handle.
 
The air hissed loudly in his ears as the valve closed.
 
George gasped, his chest contracting as the fresh air stopped. The canvas suit began to squeeze his body like a vice, the water pressure at twenty feet forcing the air out of the suit through the exhaust valve.
 
He reached behind his head, his heavy canvas gloves fumbling with the brass valve. The hand was still there, its fingers locked around the valve stem, resisting his touch with the dead, mechanical strength of the river.
 
Chapter Seven: The Diver
 
George struggled in the dark.
 
He could feel his heart pounding against his ribs, his lungs burning for oxygen. The viewing ports of his helmet were covered in red mud, making it impossible to see. He was alone in the river, twenty feet below the surface, with a dead man holding his air valve.
 
He drew a heavy steel utility knife from his belt. He reached over his shoulder, feeling for the arm of the zombie through the bars.
 
He found the arm—it was cold, soft, and slick like grease. He drove the knife into the wrist, twisting the blade until the tendons parted. The fingers on the valve went limp, releasing the brass handle.
 
George twisted the valve open.
 
A rush of cool, oily air flooded his helmet, inflating the suit and lifting the weight from his chest. He stood up, gasping for breath, his forehead pressed against the glass window of his helmet.
 
Through the red mud on the glass, he saw the severed hand still clinging to the iron bar of the trash rack, its fingers twitching in the current.
 
Chapter Eight: The Bubble
 
"George!"
 
A sound echoed down the air hose. It was not a voice; it was three distinct knocks on the copper air pipe at the top of the well.
 
*Tap. Tap. Tap.*
 
The pressure was dropping again. The air coming down the line was weak, carrying the smell of burning leather.
 
Leo was tiring. The manual pump was designed for two men, but he was alone in the pump house, his muscles cramping as he tried to maintain the pressure.
 
George climbed the iron ladder, his lead boots heavy as stone. As he rose, he looked out through the side viewing port.
 
The river was full of them.
 
A line of the dead was walking across the riverbed, their bodies aligned with the current, their heads bowed against the flow. They were heading for the intake well, drawn by the vibration of the massive pump. There were dozens of them, a slow, unstoppable parade of the drowned.
 
Chapter Nine: The Pressure
 
George climbed out of the water, landing heavily on the timber platform.
 
Leo was leaning against the air pump box, his face red, his breath coming in shallow gasps. His hands were blistered, the skin raw from the wooden handles of the crank.
 
"Father," Leo gasped, letting go of the handle. The pump stopped with a dry clatter.
 
George reached up, his wet fingers unscrewing the front viewing port of his helmet. He took a deep breath of the hot, oily air of the pump room.
 
"We have to stop the pump, Leo," George said.
 
"We can't," Leo said, pointing to the discharge canal. "The water level is already dropping. If the canal goes dry, the siphon will break. We’ll have to prime the whole three-mile line by hand."
 
"The river is full of them," George said, his voice shaking as he unbolted his breastplate. "They're coming into the intake well. If they get past the trash racks, they’ll get into the impeller. The blades will chop them to pieces, but the bones... the bones will jam the casing."
 
A loud, metallic screech came from the center of the pump room.
 
The twelve-foot flywheel shuddered, its iron rim sparking against the safety guard as the rotation slowed. The vacuum gauge needle dropped to zero.
 
Chapter Ten: The Overflow
 
The pump had jammed.
 
George ran to the gallery rail. Below, the water in the intake well was rising. The clogged grates had backed up the river water, and the sump was overflowing, the brown, silt-laden water spilling over the brick floor of the pump room.
 
Through the rising water, the dead were emerging.
 
They climbed out of the intake well, their wet boots clicking on the iron steps. One of them had a severed arm, the bone showing through the grey flesh—the zombie George had cut at the rack.
 
"Leo, the boiler!" George shouted.
 
"We can't leave the steam up!" Leo cried. "If the water level hits the firebox, the boiler will explode!"
 
"Let it," George said, grabbing his son's arm.
 
They ran for the stairs that led to the high canal road. Behind them, the water in the pump room was two feet deep, the oily surface covered in floating coal dust and the white shapes of the drowned.
 
The steam boiler gave a low, whistling roar as the cold river water touched the hot iron plates.
 
*BOOM.*
 
The explosion shook the concrete foundations of the station, throwing George and Leo into the mud of the canal road. A column of white steam and red brick dust rose into the twilight, followed by the sound of rushing water as the river took the ruins of the pump house.
 
George stood up, his canvas suit wet and heavy with silt. He looked down at the river. The pump was gone, the valley’s water supply ruined, but in the muddy shallows, the figures were still moving, their slow, wet shapes crawling out of the steam toward the bank, completely indifferent to the fire, the water, and the dark.
 
Story Twenty-Six: The Ice House
 
Chapter One: The Saw
 
The steam-driven ice saw screamed through the frozen surface of the lake, spraying a shower of white ice-shavings over Carl's heavy boots. The saw was a massive, circular steel blade, four feet in diameter, mounted on a heavy wooden sled frame that was dragged across the ice by a lumbering draft horse. The horse’s breath rose in thick, white plumes that instantly froze into ice crystals on its woolly neck. The engine, a small vertical boiler mounted on the rear of the sled, hissed and coughed, its piston rod clanking with a steady, metallic rhythm that echoed across the empty, snow-blanketed valley.
 
Carl held the guide handles, his hands wrapped in thick wool mittens that were stiff with frost. He was fifty-four, his beard a wild tangle of grey hair and frozen breath, his cheeks red and chapped from the biting winter wind of northern Colorado. His shoulders ached from the vibration of the engine, but he did not loosen his grip. The ice was his living, or at least it had been when the world made sense.
 
"Keep the horse moving, Pete!" Carl yelled over the roar of the steam engine.
 
Pete, his nineteen-year-old helper, pulled the leather reins, guiding the draft horse along the scored chalk line. Pete was a local boy whose father had lost his farm in the dry years before the mountain winters turned cruel. He wore a patched sheepskin coat that was too large for him, the sleeves frayed at the wrists, exposing red, wind-burned skin.
 
The saw cut deep, leaving a neat, blue-black channel in the three-foot-thick ice. Behind the sled, two other workers used heavy iron ice-gaffs to pry the three-hundred-pound blocks from the lake, sliding them up a wooden ramp onto the flatbed railway cars. It was the winter harvest, the only time of year when a man could earn cash in the high country. The ice would be shipped south in insulated boxcars to the railway fruit-express warehouses, keeping the lettuce of California and the beef of Texas cold during the hot summer months.
 
But this winter, the trains had stopped coming. The tracks were covered in snow, and the town at the foot of the pass was silent.
 
Chapter Two: The Vault
 
The ice house of the Pacific Fruit Express stood beside the railway siding—a massive, windowless warehouse built of double-walled brick and insulated with twelve inches of packed sawdust. Inside, the air was a constant, dry twenty-eight degrees, smelling of damp wood, horse manure, and the faint, sweet scent of ammonia from the backup refrigeration compressor. It was a cathedral of cold, a place where summer was kept at bay by the sheer mass of the frozen blocks.
 
Carl stood in the main vault, his lantern casting a dim, yellow glow over the towers of ice. The blocks were stacked twenty feet high, separated by layers of pine straw to keep them from freezing together into a solid mass. The ice was a deep, translucent blue, containing tiny bubbles of air frozen in time.
 
"The compressor is turning slow, Carl," Pete said, looking at the belt-driven machine in the corner. The compressor was powered by a horizontal steam engine, its coal-fired boiler occupying the annex next door.
 
"The coal is running low," Carl said. "We've only got enough for another day’s fire. And the water pump for the condenser is frozen. If the temperature in the vault rises above freezing, the ice will start to rot, and the sawdust will mold."
 
"We don't need the compressor," Pete said, wrapping his arms around his chest and stamping his feet. "It’s ten below zero outside. The cold is our friend."
 
"The cold is only our friend as long as we're on the right side of the door, Pete," Carl said.
 
He looked at the massive oak door of the vault. It was six inches thick, lined with felt gaskets and secured by a heavy iron latch that could only be operated from the outside. Through the wood, Carl could hear the sound of the wind, and another sound—a low, rhythmic thudding that had been going on since the sun went down.
 
Chapter Three: The Block
 
The dead had come out of the mountain passes three days ago.
 
They were not the local townspeople; they were travelers from the highway and workers from the silver mines up the canyon. The mountain cold had frozen them stiff, their limbs covered in hoarfrost, their lips black and split by the wind. They did not bleed, and they did not breathe, but they walked. Their joints cracked like breaking twigs with every step, their frozen boots clicking against the gravel of the railway bed.
 
"They're at the coal bunker, Carl," Pete whispered, pointing to the brick wall.
 
Through the masonry, they could hear the dry, scraping sound of fingers clawing at the pine boards. The dead did not feel the cold. The mountain frost that would kill a living man in two hours had no effect on them; it only preserved their flesh, preventing the rot that would otherwise slow them down.
 
Carl took a heavy ice-tongs from the rack. The iron prongs were sharp, designed to bite into the frozen blocks, but they made a formidable weapon in a man's hands.
 
"We have to stack the blocks against the door, Pete," Carl said.
 
"They can't get through the oak, Carl," Pete said, his voice shaking. "It's six inches of solid heartwood."
 
"The oak is old," Carl said. "And the hinges are cast iron. If three of them lean their weight against the latch, the iron will split. We stack the ice."
 
Chapter Four: The Cold
 
They worked for two hours in the dark, moving the three-hundred-pound blocks of ice by hand.
 
It was brutal, back-breaking work. They used the ice-tongs to drag the blocks along the pine straw floor, stacking them three deep against the massive oak door. The ice was slick and cold, the frost biting through their mittens, numbing their fingers until they could not feel the iron handles.
 
"My feet," Pete gasped, sitting down on a block of ice. "Carl, I can't feel my toes. It's like walking on wooden pegs."
 
"Don't sit," Carl said, grabbing Pete by the shoulder and pulling him up. "If you sit in the vault, the cold will take you, and you won't get up. Keep moving. Drag another block."
 
The air in the vault was getting thicker. The double-walled insulation that kept the heat out also kept the air in. With every breath, a white plume of vapor rose from their lips, condensing on the brick walls as a layer of white frost.
 
A loud, splitting crack echoed from the door.
 
The oak panel groaned, the iron latch bending under a sudden, heavy pressure from the outside. A line of yellow light appeared at the edge of the door as the felt gasket tore away.
 
Carl ran to the stack, driving the prongs of his ice-tongs into the topmost block, bracing his weight against the wood.
 
Chapter Five: The Insulation
 
Through the gap in the door, Carl could see them.
 
They were jammed into the anteroom—three of them, their faces white with frost, their eyes open and milky. One was a railroad brakeman, his wool cap still pinned to his head by a brass clip, his face torn by the mountain wolves. He did not yell; he only pushed, his frozen boots sliding on the ice-covered floorboards of the platform.
 
The smell of them came through the gap—a cold, damp odor of wet wool, old grease, and dead flesh that made Carl's eyes water.
 
"Pete, the gaff!" Carl shouted.
 
Pete ran forward, holding a six-foot pine pole with an iron hook at the end. He drove the hook through the gap, catching the brakeman by the collar of his sheepskin coat, pulling him against the oak frame.
 
The brakeman's jaws snapped through the opening, his teeth clicking against the iron hook with a dry, metallic rattle. He did not feel the steel biting into his collarbone. He simply pushed, his hands clawing at the wood, his frozen fingers breaking off one by one as they caught in the hinge gap.
 
"Push the block!" Carl yelled.
 
They threw their weight against the three-hundred-pound block of ice, sliding it six inches until it wedged against the door frame, sealing the gap and cutting off the light.
 
Chapter Six: The Grid
 
The vault returned to a heavy, frozen silence.
 
The steam engine in the annex had stopped. The coal was gone, the boiler fires dead. The backup refrigeration compressor gave a final, wheezing sigh and fell silent, the smell of ammonia slowly fading from the air.
 
"The power is gone," Pete said, his voice trembling. "The town... the lights on the hill are out."
 
Carl struck a match. The flame was small, blue, and flickered in the thin air. He looked at the thermometer on the wall.
 
It read thirty degrees.
 
Without the compressor and the outside cold, the vault would slowly begin to warm. The twelve inches of sawdust insulation would delay the heat, but they could not stop it. In the summer, a vault like this could hold the ice for three months, but that was with the doors sealed and the compressor running.
 
"We have to wait for the thaw," Carl said.
 
"If we wait, the ice will melt," Pete said. "And the dead... they'll still be there."
 
"They're frozen, Pete," Carl said. "If the temperature drops below zero outside, they won't be able to move. The frost will lock their joints."
 
"But if it warms up?" Pete asked.
 
Carl did not answer. He looked at the towers of ice, their blue surfaces gleaming in the matchlight like the walls of a tomb.
 
Chapter Seven: The Melt
 
By the second day, the temperature in the vault had risen to thirty-four degrees.
 
The ice was beginning to weep. A slow, steady drip-drip-drip echoed through the darkness as the frost on the brick walls turned to water, trickling down the mortar joints onto the sawdust floor.
 
The sawdust, which had been dry and clean, was now a wet, brown mush that smelled of rot.
 
"Carl," Pete said, his voice hoarse from the damp. He was lying on a bed of pine straw, his feet wrapped in a wool blanket. "The water... it's coming under the blocks."
 
Carl walked to the door. The ice blocks they had stacked against the wood were shrinking, their sharp edges rounding off, their surfaces slick with water. The door latch was creaking, the wood softening in the damp air.
 
From the other side of the door, he heard a new sound.
 
It was a wet, sliding movement. The dead in the anteroom were no longer frozen stiff. The rising temperature was thawing their limbs, the frost melting from their joints, allowing them to move with their normal, slow persistence.
 
*Thud. Thud. Thud.*
 
They were leaning against the oak again, their boots sliding in the wet sawdust of the platform.
 
Chapter Eight: The Frost
 
"We have to climb the ice," Carl said.
 
"Climb?" Pete asked, looking up at the twenty-foot towers.
 
"The top blocks are still dry," Carl said. "The air is colder up there. If we get to the top gallery, we can wait until the winter comes back."
 
They climbed the ice towers, using the ice-tongs to score handholds in the frozen blocks. The ice was slippery, the water running down the channels, freezing their fingers even as the air warmed.
 
Pete slipped once, his boot losing its hold on a wet block. He fell five feet, his side striking the edge of a stack before Carl caught him by the collar, pulling him onto the top platform.
 
"I'm alright," Pete gasped, though his face was white with pain, his hand holding his ribs.
 
They lay on the top row of blocks, three feet below the pine rafters of the ceiling. The air here was colder, but it was thin, and the smell of the damp sawdust below was rising, thick and heavy.
 
Chapter Nine: The Drip
 
The dripping did not stop.
 
*Drip. Drip. Drip.*
 
It was the sound of their sanctuary dissolving. Every hour, the blocks beneath them grew smaller, the stack shifting and settling with a low, grinding groan that made the ice shake.
 
"Carl," Pete whispered in the dark. "How long does it take for a three-hundred-pound block to melt?"
 
"In this vault? A month," Carl said. "If the door stays closed."
 
"But the door isn't closed," Pete said.
 
A loud splash came from the floor below.
 
The stack of blocks against the door had shifted. One of the bottom blocks, eaten away by the running water, had split in two, the weight of the upper blocks pushing the pieces aside.
 
The oak door creaked open six inches.
 
Through the gap, the dead began to crawl. They did not climb; they slid through the wet sawdust, their hands reaching into the vault, their faces covered in brown silt and melting frost.
 
Chapter Ten: The Thaw
 
Carl held the ice-tongs, his knuckles white in the dark.
 
Below them, the floor of the vault was a shallow pool of cold, brown water, filled with floating pine straw and melting ice chips. The dead were wading through the pool, their slow, heavy steps splashing the water against the brick walls.
 
One of them—the railroad brakeman—looked up. His milky eyes focused on the top gallery, his jaws opening in a silent yawn of hunger. He began to climb the ice stack, his fingers catching the melt-channels Carl had cut.
 
"Carl," Pete said, his voice quiet and steady now. He was holding his pine gaff, his boots braced against the ice. "The winter... it isn't coming back, is it?"
 
"No," Carl said. "The wind is from the south."
 
He stood up on the shrinking block of ice, the ceiling rafters touching his back. He raised the heavy iron tongs, his frozen fingers locking around the handles for the last time.
 
Below them, the ice continued to weep, the blue blocks turning to water, dissolving their fortress inch by inch into the rising tide of the dead and the thaw.
 
Story Twenty-Seven: The Quarry
 
Chapter One: The Pit
 
The limestone quarry at Oolitic, Indiana, was a grey, rectangular scar in the green, rolling hills of Lawrence County. It was three hundred feet deep, its vertical walls cut into clean, stepped benches by the steam-driven channeling machines that ran on narrow iron tracks along the rock faces. The air in the pit was cold, damp, and smelled of wet stone, sulfur, and coal dust. The quarry floor was a labyrinth of stone blocks, discarded tools, and pools of milky, alkaline water that turned to white mud under the wheels of the stone cars.
 
Marcus stood in the wooden cab of the Bucyrus steam shovel, his heavy leather boots greasy with tallow and coal soot. The shovel was a massive, ninety-ton machine of riveted steel plate, its long iron boom holding a four-cubic-yard bucket that could scoop up six tons of stone in a single bite. The engine hissed and groaned, its horizontal boiler radiating a dry, intense heat that kept the cold damp of the pit at bay.
 
"How is the steam, Caleb?" Marcus called, looking through the wire-mesh window of the cab.
 
Caleb, a young Negro fireman from the southern part of the state, was shoveling coal into the boiler firebox, his shirtless back gleaming with sweat and coal dust. "We have ninety pounds, Marcus. The draft is bad because of the damp air in the pit. The coal is wet, too. It’s burning dirty."
 
Marcus nodded. He pulled the heavy steel lever that engaged the hoisting drum, the steel cables tightening with a loud, metallic whine that echoed off the high limestone walls. The boom rose, the bucket swinging over a flatbed railway car that sat on the spurs at the bottom of the pit.
 
The quarry was a silent, vertical world, isolated from the roads and towns above. The only way in or out was the steep, winding railway incline that hauled the stone cars up to the surface.
 
But today, the incline winch at the rim was silent, its cables hanging slack down the slope.
 
Chapter Two: The Shovel
 
The steam shovel was the center of the quarry, a steel giant in a stone wilderness.
 
It sat on heavy oak blockings near the center of the pit floor, surrounded by piles of waste stone and the rusted frames of abandoned channeling machines. The engine was a two-cylinder horizontal type, its exhaust pipe shooting a plume of white steam and black soot fifty feet into the air.
 
"Marcus," Caleb said, leaning out of the boiler annex. "Look at the high bench. The third tier."
 
Marcus climbed out of the cab onto the iron cat-walk. He looked up at the rim of the quarry, where the oak forests met the limestone edge.
 
A line of figures was standing on the three-hundred-foot cliff. They were small, grey shapes against the grey sky, their clothes fluttering in the wind. As Marcus watched, one of the shapes stepped off the edge.
 
It did not scream. It fell in silence, its arms and legs twisting in the air like a rag doll, before it struck the second bench eighty feet down with a heavy, wet thud. It lay still for a moment, its back broken, its limbs bent at impossible angles, and then it began to crawl, its fingers catching the rough limestone surface as it dragged itself toward the next ledge.
 
Then, three more fell.
 
Chapter Three: The Edge
 
The dead had been coming for days, driven by some instinct they did not understand.
 
They did not follow the winding railway incline. They simply walked in straight lines, tumbling over the edges of the high cliffs like limestone blocks. The fall broke their bones, shattered their skulls, and tore their flesh, but it did not stop them. The quarry floor was already littered with the bodies of those who had fallen, their grey, broken shapes crawling through the pools of lime-water that had collected in the depressions.
 
"They're coming down the benches, Marcus," Caleb said, his hand wrapping around a heavy iron slicing-bar.
 
"They can't climb down the vertical cuts," Marcus said, his voice tight. "The benches are twenty feet high between the tiers. They'll break themselves to pieces."
 
"They aren't climbing," Caleb said. "They're just sliding. They hit the benches and they keep going. They don't care about the bones."
 
One of the fallen zombies—a woman in a blue gingham apron, her legs crushed by the fall—had reached the bottom bench. She dragged herself along the limestone track, her chin scraping the stone, her mouth open in a silent bite.
 
Marcus looked at the incline. A group of fifteen survivors—local farmers and quarrymen—had tried to climb the tracks an hour ago. But the dead had blocked the top of the incline, and the refugees had been pushed back down into the pit. Now they were scattered among the stone piles, their hunting rifles firing in the dark, the shots echoing off the limestone walls like firecrackers.
 
Chapter Four: The Boiler
 
"We have to get the steam up, Caleb," Marcus said, stepping back into the cab. "We need the power."
 
"The coal is almost gone, Marcus," Caleb said. "We have three barrows left in the bunker. After that, the fire dies."
 
"We can burn the timber blocks," Marcus said, pointing to the heavy oak timbers they used to support the shovel's jacks. "And the coal cars. We keep the boiler hot, we have the shovel. It's the only thing they can't climb."
 
The steam shovel was more than a tool; it was a fortress. The cab was built of heavy sheet iron, three-sixteenths of an inch thick, and sat eight feet off the ground on a massive steel turntable. If they closed the iron doors and shuttered the wire windows, nothing could reach them from the floor.
 
Marcus engaged the crowd engine, the shovel bucket dropping to the floor with a heavy slam that shook the rock.
 
"Caleb, the dynamite," Marcus said.
 
Caleb looked at him, his eyes wide in the light of the firebox. "The blasting shack is at the foot of the incline. It's full of them, Marcus. We can't get close."
 
"We need the powder to blow the benches," Marcus said. "If we blast the incline and the low ledges, we can bury the approach under fifty tons of limestone slide. It’s the only way to keep them from crawling down to the floor."
 
Chapter Five: The Fuse
 
Caleb ran for the blasting shack, his slicing-bar held tight in his hands.
 
The shack was a small, wood-frame building, built against the rock face to protect it from stray blasts. It was surrounded by a dozen of the dead—workers who had been bitten in the first panic and had retreated to the shadow of the cliff to die.
 
Marcus watched from the shovel cab. He swung the massive boom, the four-yard bucket scraping the limestone floor with a deafening screech that made his teeth ache.
 
He drove the bucket into the pile of waste stone, then swung it toward the blasting shack, using the heavy steel box as a shield to clear the path for Caleb.
 
*SMASH.*
 
The bucket struck two of the zombies, crushing them against the rock wall. The others turned slowly, their milky eyes focusing on the massive steel machine, their fingers clawing at the rusted teeth of the bucket.
 
Caleb reached the door of the shack, kicked it open, and disappeared inside. A minute later, he emerged carrying a wooden crate of Atlas L.F. dynamite and a coil of hemp fuse.
 
He was running, but three of the dead had cut him off, their slow, heavy steps blocking the path back to the shovel.
 
Chapter Six: The Blast
 
Marcus did not hesitate.
 
He pulled the hoisting lever, the bucket rising ten feet. He swung the boom in a fast, ninety-degree arc, the steel bucket striking the three zombies from behind, throwing them fifty feet across the floor into the lime-pit.
 
Caleb scrambled up the iron ladder of the shovel, sliding the wooden crate onto the catwalk.
 
"Did you get the caps?" Marcus asked.
 
"I got them," Caleb gasped, his chest heaving, his hands covered in yellow paraffin from the dynamite cartridges. "But the incline... Marcus, they're down. The fence at the top is completely gone."
 
Marcus looked. The incline tracks were covered in a slow-moving carpet of grey figures. They were walking down the steep slope, their boots slipping on the limestone ballast, their bodies leaning forward at an angle.
 
"We have to blast the foot of the tracks," Marcus said.
 
He took three sticks of the dynamite, wrapping them with the fuse. He did not have a copper crimping tool, so he used his teeth to crimp the blasting cap to the fuse, a dangerous trick he had learned from the old Cornish miners who had worked the deep shafts.
 
Chapter Seven: The Slide
 
They ran to the foot of the incline, their boots splashing through the pools of lime-water.
 
The dead were only fifty yards away, their dragging steps making a dry, rhythmic sound against the steel rails of the track.
 
Marcus climbed the first rock bench beside the incline. He drove a steel crowbar into a vertical seam in the limestone, widening the crack until he could slide the dynamite sticks deep into the rock.
