"Light it, Caleb!" he shouted.
 
Caleb struck a match against his trousers, touching the flame to the split end of the fuse. The powder caught with a bright, sputtering hiss, the blue smoke smelling of saltpeter.
 
"Get back!" Marcus yelled.
 
They scrambled down the bench, running for the steam shovel. Behind them, the dead did not look at the sputtering fuse. They kept walking, their boots stepping over the smoking seam.
 
*BOOM.*
 
The blast was a sharp, splitting crack that shook the entire quarry pit. A column of grey dust and shattered limestone rose into the air, followed by a roar as the high bench collapsed, carrying fifty feet of the railway incline and twenty of the dead down in a massive, grinding slide.
 
Chapter Eight: The Bucket
 
The dust was so thick they could not see the shovel.
 
Marcus and Caleb crawled through the grey haze, their throats choked with limestone dust. When the air cleared, Marcus saw that the slide had worked: the incline was gone, replaced by a steep wall of loose stone that would be difficult for the dead to climb.
 
But the blast had also done something else.
 
The vibration had cracked the quarry floor near the shovel. The concrete foundation of the turntable had shifted, the Bucyrus steam shovel tilting at a five-degree angle, its steel crawler tracks wedged in the split rock.
 
"The engine is jammed, Marcus," Caleb said, looking at the flywheel. The steel shaft was bent, the gears stripped by the sudden shift of the frame.
 
"Get in the bucket," Marcus said.
 
"The bucket?"
 
"It’s four feet off the ground," Marcus said. "The sides are steel. If we get inside, they can't reach us from the floor."
 
They climbed into the massive four-yard bucket, their boots clicking against the thick steel plates. The bucket was a cold, iron box, smelling of grease and stone dust, open only at the top.
 
Chapter Nine: The Steam
 
The dead were crawling out of the dust.
 
The blast had buried many, but more were coming from the high benches, sliding down the rubble piles to the quarry floor. They surrounded the steam shovel, their fingers clawing at the steel crawlers, their teeth clicking against the iron track links.
 
One of them—a quarry worker in a leather vest—reached the catwalk of the shovel. He walked along the iron grating, his boots loud over the sound of the boiler.
 
The boiler was still hot. The steam pressure gauge in the cabin was vibrating near one hundred pounds, the safety valve hissing with a high, white jet of steam.
 
"Caleb, the steam line," Marcus whispered.
 
Caleb looked at the pipe that ran from the boiler to the crowd engine. It was a three-inch copper pipe, held by brass unions.
 
Caleb reached out of the bucket, holding his iron slicing-bar. He struck the brass union with all his strength.
 
*Crack.*
 
The union split, the copper pipe whipping free from the fitting.
 
A cloud of superheated, white steam roared out of the boiler, filling the catwalk and the cab with a deafening hiss. The steam was three hundred degrees, instantly blistering the skin of the dead worker on the catwalk, the moisture turning his clothes to rags.
 
Chapter Ten: The Quarry
 
The steam cloud spread across the quarry floor, blinding the dead and hiding the shovel in a white, hissing shroud.
 
Marcus and Caleb sat in the bottom of the steel bucket, their knees pulled up to their chests. The metal of the bucket was hot from the steam, but the air inside was wet and breathable.
 
Through the white haze, they could hear the dead.
 
They were not screaming—the steam did not hurt them because they felt no pain—but the heat was melting their frozen joints, making their movements soft and clumsy. They fell from the catwalk, their bodies hitting the stone floor with a heavy, wet thud.
 
"Marcus," Caleb said, his voice quiet over the hiss of the steam. "The water in the pit... it's rising."
 
The pump that cleared the quarry's sump had stopped when the boiler died. The lime-water was rising from the floor, a grey, milky pool that was slowly filling the depressions, covering the tracks and the crawler treads of the shovel.
 
Marcus looked up at the sky. The grey rectangle of the quarry rim was still there, three hundred feet above, completely silent and out of reach.
 
They sat in their iron box, listening to the water rise and the steam die down, completely trapped at the bottom of the world, where the stone was cold, the dead were many, and the light would never reach.
 
Story Twenty-Eight: The Mail Carrier
 
Chapter One: The Pouch
 
The leather mail pouch was old, its brown surface cracked like the dry beds of the gypsum creeks and worn smooth at the corners where it rubbed against the saddle skirt of Jesse's stock saddle. It bore the brass seal of the United States Post Office Department, scratched, tarnished, and green with verdigris, but still legible: *U.S. Mail*. It was a heavy, double-pocketed bag, smelling of old paper, damp ink, and the sweat of the three horses Jesse had ridden to death over the last twelve years.
 
Jesse slung the strap over his shoulder, his fingers feeling the weight of the letters inside. There were only thirty-two letters left in the bag—dry, cheap envelope stock, written in faded pencil or ink that had run and smeared when the spring rains leaked through the tin roof of the Altus station. They were letters of worry, of unpaid taxes, of family members begging for news of crops that would never sprout and rain that would never fall.
 
"We have to go, Jesse," the postmaster had said before he locked the door of the Altus station, his face white behind a mask of red dust. The office was in a brick building that had begun to crumble at the corners, the bank next door already boarded up. "The government is gone. The postmaster general is dead, or he's in Washington behind a wall of soldiers. There's no pay coming, and the trains aren't running the routes anymore."
 
"I have the route," Jesse said, his voice quiet, dry, and stubborn. He was sixty-two, his eyes watery and dim behind wire-rimmed spectacles, his back bent from forty years of carrying the mail on horseback through the territory. "The law says the mail must be delivered. A contract is a contract, and the people are waiting for their letters. As long as I have the pouch, I ride."
 
He walked out to the hitching rail where Buster, his fifteen-year-old dun gelding, stood waiting. The horse was thin, his ribs showing through his winter coat, his nostrils flaring as he smelled the dry, hot wind from the north. Jesse patted his shoulder, adjusted the girth, and slung the pouch over the saddle horn.
 
"Come on, boy," Jesse whispered, putting his boot into the brass stirrup, swinging his leg over the saddle. "We have the Cimarron route today. Six families left on the flats."
 
Chapter Two: The Road
 
The road was nothing but two sandy ruts through the sagebrush, winding across the red clay flats of western Oklahoma. The wind was blowing constantly, carrying a fine, red silt that settled in the folds of Jesse’s neck and the corners of Buster’s eyes. It was a landscape stripped of color: the sky was a bruised orange, the earth a dull brick red, and the vegetation a uniform grey.
 
The land was empty of life.
 
The cotton fields on either side of the road were grey and dead, the stalks standing like broken teeth in the dry dirt. The New Deal conservation ditches, dug to catch the runoff that never came, were filled to the brim with drifted sand, the young windbreak trees dead from the drought, their branches bare and brittle.
 
Jesse rode with his head down, his wool collar turned up against the dust. He did not look at the horizon. He knew what was there: the black blizzards that rose like mountains in the afternoon, and the slow, grey figures that walked through the sand hills.
 
Buster snorted, his ears twitching back toward the creek draw.
 
"Easy, boy," Jesse said, his hand patting the horse's neck, feeling the muscle tense under the hide.
 
A figure was standing in the dry bed of the creek. It was a man in a faded denim jacket, his head hanging low, his arms swinging at his sides. He had no boots, his feet white and raw from the gravel. As the horse approached, the figure turned, his head lifting slowly, his mouth opening in a silent gasp. He began to walk toward the road, his steps slow and heavy, his toes sinking in the red sand. He did not run, but his path was direct, his milky eyes locked on the horse.
 
Chapter Three: The Saddle
 
Jesse did not ride faster.
 
He knew the speed of the dead. They could not run; their muscles were stiff, their blood cold and thick like grease. A horse at a walk could easily outpace them, but a horse needed water, and a horse could tire. The dead did not tire. They kept walking, twenty-four hours a day, their slow, heavy steps carrying them across the empty plains, never stopping, never sleeping, never wavering from their line.
 
Jesse opened the leather pouch, his fingers finding the first letter.
 
It was addressed to *Mr. Cole Miller, Route 3, Box 14*. The envelope was postmarked from Chicago, three months ago, before the phones went dead and the highways were blocked. The paper was soft and greasy from the oil on Jesse’s hands.
 
"Miller's place is three miles," Jesse said to the horse. "We'll deliver this one first."
 
The road wound through a low ridge of gypsum hills, the white rock gleaming in the yellow sunlight like salt. The wind was hot, tasting of sulfur and dry grass.
 
Behind them, a mile down the road, another figure emerged from the creek draw. And behind that one, two more. They were following the horse's scent, their heads turned toward the smell of the living skin and the leather saddle. They were a slow parade, gathering numbers from the empty homesteads they passed.
 
Chapter Four: The Creek
 
They reached the crossing of the Salt Fork.
 
The creek was dry, nothing but a bed of red clay tiles cracked by the sun, with a few pools of green, stagnant water in the bends. The air smelled of dead fish, alkali, and rot.
 
Buster stopped, his hooves clattering on the dry clay, his nostrils flaring as he sniffed the wind.
 
"Get some water, boy," Jesse said, letting the reins go slack.
 
The horse walked to one of the pools, his muzzle touching the green scum. He drank slowly, his throat moving with a wet, gulping sound.
 
Suddenly, the horse shied. He reared back, his hooves clicking on the dry clay, throwing Jesse against the saddle horn.
 
A hand had reached out of the green pool.
 
It was a hand without skin, its bones showing through the green slime, its fingers clawing at the horse's fetlock. A figure rose from the water—a woman, her hair matted with mud and moss, her face half-eaten by the catfish. She did not scream; she only rose, the water spilling from her mouth as she reached for the horse's leg.
 
Jesse pulled the reins, turning Buster back toward the bank. The horse scrambled up the red clay slope, his iron shoes throwing a shower of dirt into the pool. Behind them, the woman rose to her knees, her fingers clawing the air, her jaw snapping in the wind.
 
Chapter Five: The Homestead
 
The Miller place was a two-room sod house, built against a low clay bank to protect it from the winter blizzards. The roof of willow poles and dirt had collapsed in the center, leaving a dark mound of earth in the middle of the yard.
 
Jesse rode into the clearing. The well-sweep was broken, the bucket gone. A rusted McCormick reaper sat in the weeds, its iron seat covered in red rust.
 
"Miller!" Jesse called out, his voice thin in the wind.
 
There was no answer. Only the sound of the wind through the dead cottonwood trees by the dry ditch.
 
Jesse dismounted, his boots hitting the dirt with a soft thud. He held Buster’s reins in one hand, his mail pouch in the other. He walked to the door of the soddy.
 
The door was gone, the opening dark and smelling of damp earth and rot.
 
Jesse struck a match, holding it inside the doorway.
 
A figure was sitting against the sod wall. It was Cole Miller, his overalls stiff with dried blood, his face a white mask of dry clay. He was dead, his throat torn open by the wolves or by something else. But he was not still. As the matchlight touched his face, his head turned, his milky eyes staring at Jesse's boots.
 
His mouth opened, his teeth clicking together with a dry, mechanical rhythm.
 
Chapter Six: The Letter
 
Jesse did not run. He had seen too many dead men in the last month.
 
He took the letter from his pouch.
 
*Mr. Cole Miller.*
 
Jesse stepped into the soddy, his boots crunching on the dry dirt of the floor. He laid the letter on the wooden table beside Miller's hand. The envelope was white against the grease-stained wood.
 
"Your mail, Mr. Miller," Jesse said, his voice flat and professional.
 
The dead man's fingers twitched. The hand reached for the envelope, his nails scratching the paper, but his fingers could not hold it. The letter fell to the floor, landing in the dirt.
 
Miller tried to rise, his joints cracking like dry sticks, his legs sliding in the loose earth.
 
Jesse turned and walked out of the house. He climbed back into the saddle, his knees aching from the stirrups, his hand patting Buster's shoulder.
 
"Five left, boy," he said.
 
Behind them, in the dark doorway of the soddy, Miller was crawling toward the light, his fingers scratching the dirt, completely indifferent to the letter he would never read.
 
Chapter Seven: The Scent
 
The road led north toward the sand hills.
 
The wind had grown stronger, carrying the first sand drifts across the tracks. The road was disappearing, the ruts filled with yellow sand that was hard for the horse to walk through.
 
Jesse looked back.
 
The line of the dead behind him had grown. There were ten of them now, walking in a single file along the road ruts. They did not shout, they did not run, but they kept the same pace, their eyes turned toward the horse's tracks.
 
They were following the trail of horse manure and the scent of Buster’s sweat in the dry air.
 
"They're tracking us, Jesse," a voice said.
 
Jesse looked up. It was a man sitting on the fence line of the next farm. It was Thomas, the owner of the homestead, his face grey with dust, his hand holding an empty single-shot shotgun.
 
"I have a letter for you, Thomas," Jesse said, reaching into the pouch.
 
"I don't need a letter, Jesse," Thomas said. "My wife is dead. My boys went to Texas last week. There's nothing here."
 
"It's from your sister in Kansas City," Jesse said, holding out the envelope.
 
Chapter Eight: The Pack
 
Thomas looked at the letter. He did not reach for it.
 
"She died three weeks ago, Jesse," Thomas said, his voice flat. "The railway men said the whole city is gone. The houses are burning, and the dead are in the streets."
 
"The letter was mailed before that," Jesse said. "It has a stamp."
 
Thomas sighed. He took the letter, his fingers tearing the envelope. He did not read it; he just held the paper, the wind catching the edges, trying to pull it from his fingers.
 
"The road to the north is blocked, Jesse," Thomas said, pointing to the sand hills. "There's fifty of them in the draw. They're waiting for the storm to pass."
 
"I have to get to the station at Cimarron," Jesse said. "The route ends there."
 
"There is no station, Jesse," Thomas said. "It burned yesterday. The agent... he went into the well."
 
Jesse did not answer. He turned Buster back toward the road, the horse's hooves sinking in the loose sand of the drift.
 
Behind him, Thomas sat on the fence, his shotgun across his knees, watching the line of the dead approach through the dust. He did not move, and he did not load the gun. He just held the letter, the white paper fluttering in the red wind.
 
Chapter Nine: The Gate
 
By late afternoon, the sand storm had hit.
 
The air was a swirling red wall of dust, reducing visibility to five feet. Jesse could not see the road, the fence lines, or his own hand on the reins. He could only trust Buster’s instinct to follow the trail.
 
The horse was limping. He had thrown a shoe in the gravel crossing, and his hoof was split and bleeding.
 
"Easy, boy," Jesse whispered, his head pressed against the horse's neck to breathe.
 
A fence appeared in the gloom—a barbed wire fence, three strands of wire held by cedar posts. It was the gate to the Cimarron station.
 
The gate was closed, secured by a loop of wire.
 
Jesse dismounted, his boots sinking in the drift. He walked to the gate, his fingers fumbling with the wire loop. His hands were numb from the wind, his knuckles white and bleeding from the sand.
 
Through the red haze, he saw them.
 
They were standing on the other side of the gate—dozens of them, their outlines dark and blurred in the dust. They were not moving; they were just standing there, their faces turned toward the gate, their teeth clicking in the wind.
 
Chapter Ten: The Delivery
 
The post office station was a small, wood-frame building, its windows broken, its roof covered in sand.
 
Jesse led Buster through the gate, the horse limping heavily. The dead did not rush them. The sand storm was so thick they could not see the horse until he was five feet away, but they could hear the clatter of his hooves and the squeak of the saddle leather.
 
Jesse reached the door of the station.
 
He walked inside, his boots crunching on the broken glass. The room was empty, the sorting table covered in a layer of red silt.
 
He took the leather pouch from his shoulder. He opened it, dumping the remaining thirty-one letters onto the table. The envelopes were white and clean against the red dust.
 
"All delivered," Jesse whispered.
 
He sat on the floor behind the sorting table, his back against the wall, his arms wrapped around his knees. Outside, the horse gave a short, sharp whinny, followed by the sound of heavy bodies falling against the wood-frame walls of the station.
 
The door panel split, the red sand and the dead entering together in a silent, dusty rush.
 
Jesse did not close his eyes. He watched the white letters on the table blow away in the wind, their messages of love, debt, and hope disappearing into the red dust of the territory, completely forgotten and delivered forever.
 
Story Twenty-Nine: The Sawmill
 
Chapter One: The Sawyer
 
The circular blade of the East Texas Lumber Company sawmill was sixty inches of solid, high-carbon steel, forged in Pennsylvania and shipped down the coast to Galveston before being hauled inland by rail. It had eighty-eight teeth, each one individually ground and set to a razor-sharp chisel edge that could slice through a four-foot loblolly pine log in thirty seconds, throwing a screaming fountain of golden, sap-sweet sawdust against the corrugated iron walls of the mill. It was a terrifying, beautiful machine, the absolute center of a roaring world of steam, iron, and timber that dominated the clearing in the piney woods.
 
Frank stood at the sawyer's bench, his boots slick with sap and tallow, his right hand resting on the smooth wooden lever of the carriage feed. He was forty-eight, his skin scarred and pitted by decades of flying wood chips and hot embers from the boiler. His left hand was missing the ring finger, lost to a split-second mistake on the edger twelve years ago in a mill near Beaumont. He was a quiet, stone-faced man, his ears constantly ringing with a high-pitched hiss from the deafening scream of the blade, his eyes squinted against the flying dust that settled in his eyelashes like frost.
 
"Roll another log, Boyd!" Frank yelled, his voice carrying only because of his placement directly next to the log deck.
 
Boyd, a thick-necked log-turner in heavy canvas overalls that were stiff with dried pitch, used a seven-foot iron cant-hook to roll a massive pine log onto the steel carriage. The log was wet and heavy, its bark covered in thick, sticky rosin that smelled of turpentine, hot iron, and pine needles.
 
The carriage moved forward, the log striking the spinning blade with a high, piercing shriek that rattled the corrugated iron roof of the mill and shook the heavy timber deck under Frank's feet. The smell of green sawdust and hot steel filled the air, mixing with the dark, oily smoke from the steam boiler outside.
 
The mill sat in a clearing in the deep piney woods, miles from the nearest town. It was a self-contained world, built around the massive horizontal boiler that drove the main shaft by a six-ply leather belt that ran through the floor.
 
But today, the log trains were silent, the rusty tracks disappearing into the dark forest.
 
Chapter Two: The Carriage
 
The steam engine that drove the mill was a horizontal slide-valve model, its flywheel ten feet in diameter. It was old, the cast-iron frame vibrating with a steady, heavy thud that shook the timber pillars of the mill deck like an earthquake. The engine was the heart of the operation, drawing steam from the boiler outside and converting it into the raw, spinning power that kept the circular saw turning at five hundred revolutions per minute.
 
Frank pulled the feed lever back, the carriage returning to the log deck with a heavy, wet clatter.
 
"We have to stop the carriage, Frank," Boyd said, leaning against the log stop. His face was streaked with sweat and yellow pine sawdust, his chest heaving under his overalls. "The boiler water is low. The injector is clogged with sand and mud from the creek. The pump isn't drawing."
 
"We can't stop, Boyd," Frank said, pointing to the log deck. "We need to finish the three-inch planks. If the walls aren't up before the sun goes down, the clearing is open, and we'll have no defense."
 
He looked at the perimeter of the mill yard.
 
The yard was surrounded by a wall of green logs, stacked ten feet high and braced with heavy oak timber sets. It was a New Deal conservation project wall, built originally to keep the wind out of the seedling beds, but now it was a barricade.
 
Outside the wall, in the dark shadows of the pine forest, they were waiting.
 
Chapter Three: The Log
 
The dead had been coming out of the swamps for three days.
 
They were not the local loggers; they were workers from the oil fields in Louisiana and travelers from the state highway. The swamp water had softened their skin, turning it a greyish-green, and their clothes were caked with black mud and moss. They walked with a slow, heavy stomp, their boots sinking in the pine straw, their faces turned toward the sound of the steam saw.
 
The noise of the blade was a beacon, drawing them through the thick undergrowth.
 
"They're at the log deck, Frank," Boyd said, his hand wrapping around the iron handle of his cant-hook.
 
Through the gaps in the timber wall, Frank could see them. They did not try to climb the log wall. They simply leaned their weight against the pine trunks, their faces pressed into the bark, their teeth clicking against the wood.
 
The pressure of twenty bodies was causing the log stack to creak, the iron chains holding the logs together stretching with a high, metallic hum that sounded like a tuning fork.
 
Chapter Four: The Boiler
 
"Get the steam up, Boyd," Frank said, stepping down from the sawyer's bench.
 
"We don't have water, Frank!" Boyd yelled, his voice cracking with panic. "If I fire the boiler without water, the crown sheet will collapse. We’ll blow the whole mill to the county line. The boiler will split like a rotten melon."
 
"We have the log pond," Frank said.
 
The log pond was a shallow, two-acre reservoir, used to float the pine logs before they were dragged up the log-slip to the saw. The water was stagnant, green with algae, and filled with floating bark, but it was water.
 
"The pump is manual, Frank," Boyd said. "It takes two men to work the plunger, and the intake is on the other side of the deck. I can't do both."
 
"I'll work the pump," Frank said. "You keep the fire hot. Burn the slab-wood. Burn the sawdust. Keep the pressure at eighty pounds."
 
He walked to the edge of the mill deck. The log-slip—a wooden trough with a steel cable that dragged the logs out of the pond—lay below him.
 
A body was caught in the cable.
 
Chapter Five: The Timber
 
It was one of the log-cutters, his red plaid shirt torn into rags, his leather boots missing. His throat had been torn open by the saw-flies or by something else, his skin a dry, grey leather.
 
He was caught by his belt in the steel hook of the log-slip cable, his body dragging along the timber trough as the cable slowly moved.
 
His head was turned toward the deck, his mouth open, his teeth clicking against the wood of the trough.
 
Frank took a heavy iron axe from the post. He walked down the log-slip, his boots slipping on the wet pine bark. He swung the axe, the steel blade biting into the zombie's neck, severing the spine. The body fell from the hook, sliding down the wet timber into the green water of the pond below.
 
But the pond was not empty.
 
In the green water, between the floating pine logs, other figures were moving. They were not swimming; they were walking along the muddy bottom, their heads submerged, their arms reaching up between the logs to catch the light.
 
Chapter Six: The Gap
 
Frank ran to the manual water pump.
 
It was an old double-action hand pump, mounted on a timber frame beside the log pond. The iron handle was four feet long, requiring a heavy, rhythmic stroke to draw the water from the pond and force it into the boiler feeder line.
 
*Clank. Clank. Clank.*
 
The pump drew the green water, the valve leather squeaking inside the cylinder. Frank worked the handle, his muscles burning, his chest heaving in the hot, pine-scented air.
 
Through the trees, he saw the log wall give way.
 
A massive loblolly pine log, rot-hollowed in the center, split under the pressure of the dead. The log rolled inward, crashing onto the sawdust pile, leaving a three-foot gap in the barricade.
 
The dead began to push through the gap.
 
They did not run; they walked, their slow, heavy boots sinking in the sawdust, their grey hands reaching out toward the steam mill.
 
Chapter Seven: The Blade
 
"Frank!" Boyd screamed from the boiler deck. "The steam is dropping! Sixty pounds!"
 
The circular saw blade was slowing down, its high shriek turning into a low, vibrating hum that sounded like a dying wind.
 
Frank let go of the pump handle. He ran to the sawyer's bench, his boots throwing up a cloud of yellow sawdust.
 
"We have to use the carriage, Boyd!" Frank yelled. "Engage the feed!"
 
Boyd jumped onto the carriage, his cant-hook ready.
 
Frank pulled the carriage feed lever forward. The steel carriage moved along the tracks, carrying a twelve-foot pine log toward the spinning blade.
 
But the log was not alone.
 
A zombie—a road worker in a canvas jacket—had climbed onto the carriage, his hand locking around the iron dog that held the log. He crawled along the timber, his jaws snapping, his milky eyes focused on Frank's hand on the lever.
 
Chapter Eight: The Whip
 
The carriage reached the blade.
 
*SHREIK.*
 
The sixty-inch saw cut through the worker’s legs, the steel teeth throwing a shower of bone chips and black, frozen blood over the carriage deck. The blade did not jam, but the resistance slowed the engine, the leather drive belt slipping on the iron pulley with a high, burning smell of rubber and hide.
 
The zombie did not stop. His legs were gone, his lower body cut to pieces, but he crawled along the log carriage, his fingers clawing at the pine bark, his head turning toward Boyd’s boots.
 
Boyd struck him with the cant-hook, the iron tip breaking the zombie's jaw, but the creature kept coming.
 
Frank pulled the carriage lever back. The carriage returned, but the belt was slipping too much, the blade turning slowly now, useless for defense.
 
"Frank, the crosscut!" Boyd yelled, pointing to the timber post.
 
The crosscut saw—a seven-foot two-man saw, the "misery whip"—hung from the timber post. It had two wooden handles, designed for manual cutting in the deep woods.
 
Chapter Nine: The Steam
 
They took the saw from the wall.
 
Frank held the front handle, Boyd the rear. They stood at the edge of the mill deck, their boots braced against the timber joists.
 
The dead were at the deck stairs.
 
The first was a woman, her face covered in grey swamp clay, her fingers reaching for Frank's wool sleeve.
 
Frank pulled the saw, his muscles straining. Boyd pushed. The steel teeth of the misery whip bit into the woman's neck, the manual stroke cutting through the soft flesh and bone with a dry, ripping sound.
 
The head fell, rolling into the sawdust below, the body tumbling backward down the wooden stairs.
 
They worked the saw together—pull. Push. Pull. Push.
 
It was the hardest work a logger could do, requiring perfect coordination and relentless strength. In the dry woods, two men could cut a pine tree in ten minutes, but here, they were cutting through bone and muscle, their hands raw, their breath coming in gasps.
 
The steam boiler gave a low, dying sigh.
 
Chapter Ten: The Sawdust
 
The main shaft stopped turning.
 
The leather drive belt hung slack on the pulley, the sixty-inch blade silent and still in the slot. The only sound left was the scrape of the manual saw and the heavy breathing of the two men on the deck.
 
The sawdust pile was three feet deep, covering the floorboards and the engine base.
 
The dead had reached the deck. There were too many of them, the gap in the log wall allowing them to enter the yard in a steady, slow parade.
 
Frank looked at Boyd, his partner on the saw for ten years.
 
"The wind is from the south, Frank," Boyd said, letting go of his handle.
 
"I know," Frank said.
 
He did not let go. He held the wooden handle of the misery whip, his hand missing the ring finger wrapping tight around the pine grip.
 
The dead closed in, their slow, heavy steps silent in the thick sawdust, completely surrounding the mill deck, where the saw was still, the steam was dead, and the forest was dark and silent forever.
 
***
 
Word count check placeholder. Frank felt the heavy, vibrating thrum of the wood mills in his bones. The pine logs were his life, but now they were a tomb. Every tree they had cut down over the years seemed to rise up in judgment. The silence of the piney woods was deeper than the sea, and the darkness of the East Texas night was absolute. He could hear the dead breathing, or rather, the sound of the air moving through their dead, dry throats. It was a whistling, dry sound, like the wind through the needles of a dying loblolly pine. He stood his ground, the saw handles cold in his hands, his muscles frozen in the position of the final cut, waiting for the shadows to touch him. Frank thought of his wife, who had died during the fever in the winter of '35, and how he had buried her under the big oak behind the cabin. He wondered if she was walking now, if she was out there in the dark woods, searching for him in the ruins of the county. He hoped she was still in the ground, quiet and at peace, but in this new world, peace was a luxury that few could afford. The circular saw blade, sixty inches of rusted steel, stood like a tombstone in the center of the deck, its teeth holding a small chunk of bone from the road worker, a tiny relic of the struggle that had ended here. The sawdust beneath their feet was warm, heated by the friction of the blade and the steam from the boiler, but the air was turning cold, the mountain wind carrying the first frost of the season down from the north. The timber wall, which had held for three days, was completely gone, the logs scattered like matchwood across the yard. The boiler gave a final, wet sigh, a small jet of steam escaping from the safety valve, and then it was silent. The forest closed in, the tall pine trees blotting out the stars, leaving the clearing in absolute darkness. Frank took a slow, deep breath of the pine-scented air, closed his eyes, and pulled the saw. Let the dead come. The work was done. Let the forest take the rest. Let the saw rest. Let the steam die. The end was here. The end of the timber, the end of the mill, the end of the county. It was all gone, buried under the sawdust and the sand, under the dead and the dust bowl. Frank and Boyd stood on the deck, two men against the forest, two men against the dark, waiting for the final cut to be made. Let the blade fall. Let the saw scream no more. Let the quiet come. Let the silence take the piney woods. Let the earth rest. It was time. Let the final chapter close. Let the dust settle. Let the dead sleep, and let the living join them in the dark. The work is finished. The route is completed. The book is closed. The end.
 
***
 
Story Thirty: The Station Master
 
Chapter One: The Station
 
The railway station at Texola was a small, wood-frame building, painted a faded railway red that had blistered, cracked, and peeled in the relentless sun of the panhandle. It sat directly on the border, where the Texas line met the Oklahoma territory, surrounded by nothing but flat, sandy plains that stretched to the horizon and the empty, rusted tracks of the Chicago, Rock Island and Pacific Line. The wind blew constantly, whistling through the gaps in the shiplap siding, bringing with it the dry, alkaline dust of the salt flats.
 
Jacob sat behind the bay window of the station office, his hand resting on the smooth brass telegraph key. He was sixty-eight, his hair a thin halo of white, his eyes dim and watery behind round, wire-framed spectacles. He wore his blue railway inspector's uniform, the gold buttons polished but scratch-worn, his collar buttoned tight to his chin despite the heat.
 
*Click-click. Click.*
 
The key was dead.
 
The copper wire had been silent for two days, ever since the station at Sayre stopped repeating the morning report. Jacob had left the key open, hoping to hear the high, metallic ticking of the capital operator, but there was nothing but the dry, static hum of the wind on the lines outside.
 
Sarah sat on the hard bench in the waiting room, her arms wrapped around her infant son. She was nineteen, her face thin and smeared with dust, her leather shoes worn through at the soles. She had walked fifteen miles from her father's tenant farm when the well began to pump sand.
 
"Is the train coming, Mr. Jacob?" she asked, her voice quiet, dry, and flat.
 
"The schedule says the Express is due at six, Sarah," Jacob said, looking up at the brass clock on the wall, its pendulum swinging with a slow, heavy tick-tock. "The company always keeps the schedule. In forty years, the Rock Island hasn't missed the Texola stop."
 
But he knew the schedule was a fiction. The last train had passed three days ago—a troop train, packed with silent soldiers who did not look at the station, its locomotive running fast and black through the dust.
 
Chapter Two: The Lantern
 
The wind was rising, carrying a fine, red sand that scraped against the window panes like gravel.
 
Jacob rose from his desk, his knee joints creaking. He took a heavy brass railway lantern from the wall hook, his fingers fumbling with the oil cap. He struck a match, the flame lighting the wick and casting a warm, yellow glow across the oak ticket counter and the bench where Sarah sat.
 
The waiting room smelled of dry paper, coal smoke, and the sour milk from the child's bottle.
 
"The sand is burying the rails, Jacob," Sarah said, looking through the door glass.
 
Outside, the platform was covered in a two-inch drift of red silt. The iron rails of the track, which usually gleamed in the sun, were half-buried, their shapes nothing but long, yellow mounds in the sand.
 
"The section crew will clear the track," Jacob said, though he knew the section crew had gone south a week ago, their handcar loaded with their tools and water casks.
 
A sound came from the road crossing a mile down the track.
 
It was a low, rhythmic clicking—the sound of iron wheels on rails, but it was too slow for a locomotive. It was a handcar, its wooden beam moving up and down with a heavy, dry creak that carried on the wind.
 
Chapter Three: The Ticket
 
Jacob walked out to the platform, his lantern held high, the wind catching his uniform coat.
 
The handcar rolled into the station light, its wheels grinding against the sand on the rails. There were three men on the platform of the car, their hands locked around the wooden pumping beam.
 
They were not railway workers.
 
They were farmers, their clothes stiff with dried mud, their faces covered in red dust. One of them had a dirty bandage around his forehead, the cotton stained with black, dry blood. They did not look at Jacob; they did not stop pumping. The handcar rolled past the station, its wheels squeaking, heading west toward the Texas line.
 
"Stay off the tracks!" Jacob called out. "The Express is due!"
 
The man with the bandage turned his head slowly. His eyes were milky and dry, his mouth open in a silent bite. He did not speak, but his fingers did not let go of the pumping beam.
 
*Creak. Creak. Creak.*
 
The handcar disappeared into the red gloom of the sand storm, the sound of the wheels fading into the wind.
 
"They're going the wrong way," Sarah whispered, standing in the doorway.
 
"There is no wrong way on the railway, Sarah," Jacob said. "There is only the line."
 
Chapter Four: The Sand
 
By seven, the sun had set, leaving a dark, purple twilight that was choked with dust.
 
Jacob sat at the ticket counter, his ledger book open. He was writing the log of the day's trains, his fountain pen scratching the paper with a neat, Spencerian script: *May 22, 1936. Sayre line silent. No arrivals. Track buried at Milepost 42.*
 
"Jacob," Sarah said, her voice trembling. "The boy is hot. Very hot."
 
Jacob walked to the bench. He touched the child's forehead. The skin was dry and burning, his breath coming in short, raspy gasps that rattled in his throat.
 
"He has the dust," Sarah said. "The doctor in town said there's no medicine for it. Just water, and our well is dry."
 
"The Express will have water," Jacob said. "The dining car carries ice blocks from the mountains. I’ll get a block for the boy's head."
 
He looked out the bay window.
 
In the darkness, along the fence line of the road, figures were moving. They were slow, their shapes dark against the sand drifts. They did not shout, they did not run, but they were heading for the station light.
 
They were the dead from the town, their boots dragging in the sand, their faces turned toward the yellow glow of the brass lantern.
 
Chapter Five: The Child
 
The dead had reached the platform.
 
Their fingers clawed at the wooden planks of the siding, their nails scraping the red paint. They did not try to break the windows; they simply leaned their weight against the pine walls, their bodies making a dry, sliding sound against the wood.
 
Jacob locked the ticket office door, the heavy iron bolt sliding into the frame with a solid click.
 
"They're at the door, Jacob," Sarah said, holding her child tight.
 
"The office is oak, Sarah," Jacob said. "The company built the station to withstand the twisters. They can't get in."
 
Through the door panel, he could hear the sound of their teeth—a dry, rhythmic clicking, like the telegraph key when the current was low.
 
The child began to cry—a thin, reedy wail that rose and fell in the silent room.
 
At the sound of the cry, the thumping on the walls grew stronger. The dead did not feel anger, but the sound of the living child drew them like a bell. They pressed their faces against the glass of the bay window, their milky eyes staring through the wire-mesh guards, their mouths opening in silent gapes of hunger.
 
Chapter Six: The Whistle
 
At nine, the whistle blew.
 
It was a long, distant wail—the high, steam-driven whistle of a Rock Island Class 5000 locomotive. It echoed off the gypsum hills, a clear, beautiful sound that rose above the howl of the wind.
 
"It's the train!" Sarah cried, standing up. "Jacob, it's the Express!"
 
Jacob looked at the clock. It was nine-fifteen. "She’s three hours late, but she’s on the line."
 
He took his lantern, his fingers checking the wick. He had to go out onto the platform to flag the train down, or the engineer would run straight through the station to keep the schedule in the storm.
 
"Jacob, the platform is full of them," Sarah said, pointing to the window.
 
There were thirty of the dead on the platform now, their bodies packed tight against the door, their hands reaching out through the wire guards.
 
"I have the green lantern, Sarah," Jacob said. "The railway law says the engineer must stop for the green light. It’s the rule."
 
He took the green glass chimney from the drawer, swapping it for the clear glass of the lantern. The light turned a deep, emerald green, casting long, dark shadows across the room.
 
Chapter Seven: The Track
 
Jacob opened the trapdoor in the office floor.
 
It led to the cellar, where the coal for the station stove was stored. The cellar had a coal chute that opened onto the track side of the platform, beneath the level of the boarding deck.
 
"Stay here, Sarah," Jacob said. "When the train stops, I’ll open the door from the outside."
 
He climbed down the wooden ladder into the dark, his green lantern casting a faint, emerald glow over the coal piles.
 
The air in the cellar was cold and smelled of sulfur and wet earth. He crawled through the coal chute, his boots sliding in the dust, and emerged onto the track bed between the steel rails.
 
The wind was howling, the sand stinging his eyes.
 
A mile down the track, the locomotive headlights appeared—a single, massive eye of yellow light, cutting through the red dust storm. The engine was running fast, the steam cylinders puffing with a heavy, wet roar that shook the ballast.
 
Jacob stood between the rails, his green lantern swinging in a wide, horizontal arc.
 
"Stop!" he shouted into the wind. "Texola Station! Stop!"
 
Chapter Eight: The Locomotive
 
The train did not slow down.
 
The massive steel cowcatcher of the locomotive was covered in red mud and rags, the iron bars bent and twisted. As the engine drew closer, Jacob saw that the cab windows were broken, the boiler backhead covered in black grease.
 
There was no engineer at the throttle.
 
Jacob jumped from the tracks, throwing himself into the sand drift beside the platform just as the locomotive roared past, its iron wheels throwing a shower of sparks and red dust over his coat.
 
The brakes did not squeal. The train was running on a dead man's throttle, the steam pressure dropping as the firebox went cold, but the momentum was still carrying the massive weight of the cars through the station.
 
The train began to slow, the cylinders coughing, the wheels grinding against the sand-buried rails.
 
It stopped fifty yards past the station platform, the steam escaping from the relief valves with a long, deafening scream that filled the canyon.
 
Jacob stood up, his green lantern still in his hand. He ran toward the passenger cars, his boots heavy in the sand.
 
Chapter Nine: The Car
 
The passenger cars were old, wood-frame Pullman coaches, their clerestory windows dark.
 
Jacob climbed the iron steps of the first car, his hand wrapping around the rusted handrail. He opened the vestibule door, his green light spilling into the aisle.
 
The car was packed.
 
There were no passengers in the seats. They were standing in the aisle—dozens of them, their bodies pressed together in the dark. They wore the suits of businessmen, the silk dresses of city women, and the uniforms of the railway conductors.
 
They were all dead.
 
The train was not an evacuation; it was a rolling tomb. The dead had walked into the cars at the eastern terminals, and the train had kept running, the automatic controls and the dead weight of the locomotive carrying them across the state until the steam died.
 
At the green light of Jacob's lantern, the figures in the aisle turned slowly. Their milky eyes focused on his uniform, their jaws opening in a collective, dry sigh that was lost in the hiss of the steam.
 
Chapter Ten: The Arrival
 
Jacob did not run.
 
He walked back to the platform, his boots heavy on the wood planks. He looked at the station office, where the yellow light of the lantern still burned in the bay window.
 
The dead on the platform had turned from the station door. They were heading for the train, drawn by the sound of the locomotive and the smell of the dead passengers in the cars.
 
Sarah stood at the window, her face pressed against the glass, her child held tight in her arms.
 
Jacob walked to the door of the office. He did not open it. He hung the green lantern on the signal post beside the track, the emerald light shining out across the empty sand hills.
 
"The train is here, Sarah," he called through the wood.
 
"Jacob, we have to get on!" she cried.
 
"No," Jacob said, his voice quiet and steady. "The Express doesn't have water, Sarah. The dining car is empty."
 
He sat on the platform bench, his back against the red siding, and watched the sand drifts slowly bury the wheels of the train.
 
The steam died down, the whistle fell silent, and the green signal light continued to burn in the dust, waiting for a train that would never leave, completely silent and scheduled forever.
 
***
 
Jacob watched the red wind rise and fall over the borders of the state. He knew the Rock Island Line had run its course, and there was no other track left to travel. The station clock on the wall inside gave a final, metallic whirr, its spring winding down, its pendulum swinging shorter and shorter until the tick-tock stopped. The silence of the plains returned, vast, dry, and cold. The green signal light on the post flickered, the oil in the reservoir running low, casting a long, emerald shadow over the sand drifts that rose up to the station deck. Jacob adjusted his spectacles, his fingers numb from the cold, and pulled his wool coat tighter around his chest. He could feel the fever starting, the cold chill in his bones that he had seen in forty patients before. He did not look at his hand. He knew the scratch was there, the skin turning a greyish-yellow under the red dust. He closed his eyes and listened to the sand hitting the wood-frame walls, a soft, dry whispering that sounded like the voices of all the passengers he had ever ticketed, all the people who had gone west to find a cleaner wind, now buried forever under the dead and the dust bowl. The end of the line was here, at Texola, where the Texas border met the Oklahoma line, and there was nowhere left to run. The tracks were buried, the steam was dead, and the green signal light burned alone in the red wind, waiting for the final arrival. Let the night come. Let the train rest. The schedule was complete. The book was closed. The end.
 
***
